MANUEL PRATIQUE D JARDINAGE CONTENANT LA MANIÈRE DE CULTIVER SOIME UN JARDIN OU D'EN DIRIGER LA CULTURE PAR COURTOIS-GÉRARD Marchand grainier, horticulteur. SEPTIÈME ÉDITION PARIS LIBRAIRIE SCIENTIFIQUE, INDUSTRIELLE ET AGRICOLE Eugène LACROIX, Éditeur LIBRAIRE DE LA SOCIÉTÉ DES INGÉNIEURS CIVILS QUAI MALAQUAIS All rights reserved. The progress that time brings with it, even more rapidly in the practice of horticulture than in the other branches of human work, has occurred in large numbers, not only since the first edition of the Gardening Manual, but also since the sixth edition, which this one replaces. We must therefore point out that several sections have been thoroughly reworked to bring them up to date with current horticultural knowledge; any gaps that may have appeared over the years have been carefully filled. The calendar, revised and recast, has been expanded with important new details. Interesting and lucid explanations complete the chapter on vegetable gardening. The Assolement article, the first to be published on gardening, can be consulted with advantage by all those who want to obtain the most abundant harvests that a well-kept vegetable garden can produce; for not a fact advanced by the author has been taken outside what is consecrated by practice. In the section on the fruit garden, in addition to more detailed explanations of a number of operations, we find lists of the most esteemed fruit trees in each series - lists which are constantly being enriched by the many advances in Pomology - and data of great practical value for the treatment of tree diseases. But it is above all in the section devoted to the pleasure garden that we find the most remarkable improvements to the Gardening Manual, in its seventh edition, from the composition of lawns, a pitfall where so many inexperienced gardeners fail, to the direction of the most difficult plants to obtain in all their beauty. Indeed, it is not enough for the floriculture enthusiast to pile up, in the beds or flowerbeds of his parterre, the infinite variety of beautiful ornamental plants of every season that contemporary horticulture has at its disposal; it is above all necessary that these plants grow and flower with all the charm that is theirs; otherwise, instead of generating the most harmless and varied pleasures, floriculture offers only a long succession of misfortunes and setbacks. Numerous examples of flowerbeds made up of plants that can advantageously contribute to the ornamentation of gardens will enable amateurs who consult this work to often make the most of the resources at their disposal. Books which, like the Gardening Manual, smooth the way, enable the amateur to foresee and overcome difficulties, and ensure the success of his amusing work: these books are important services rendered to horticulture as a whole; for, by removing obstacles, they propagate the taste for it; and the extension of the taste for horticulture in all social ranks benefits everyone. As for the care given to the material execution, avoiding a luxury which would unnecessarily make the price of a work which by its nature should be within everyone's reach, it is what it should be, as in previous editions, from the point of view of neatness and the most scrupulous typographical correction. As you can see, this new edition of the Gardening Manual combines everything that can earn this book the continuation of the fame it has enjoyed since its birth. CHAPTER ONE. General layout of a vegetable garden. The advice we give here on how to set up a garden may seem superfluous to those who are forced to accept fixed locations, bastard layouts and arrangements made in advance; but we feel it is our duty to set out the conditions that it is essential to meet whenever you are master of choosing or laying out your plot. As for the interior layouts, they are designed to make the work as easy as possible, and are intended to show how crops can be grown one after the other without interruption, which is very rare in gardens cultivated by people unfamiliar with horticulture, and yet requires only a little attention and practice, and a book they can trust. The most suitable soil for cultivation is one that is 1 metre deep and made up of good soil, with the surface composed of free, soft earth, and the subsoil of sand suitable for vegetation. With this kind of soil, manure and water, we can be sure of successfully cultivating all the plants used in everyday farming. We don't mean to imply that soils of different kinds are unsuitable for vegetation; they are all suitable when they are light enough to be permeable to air, without moisture remaining too long, and yet cool enough to give the roots time to absorb the fluids they need for nutrition. Our aim is simply to highlight the most fertile soils, and those whose cultivation most amply rewards the gardener for his care. If the soil is not as we have indicated, manure, soil improvers and ploughing will make up for the qualities it lacks. The vegetable garden we represent (fig. I, at the end of the volume) forms a parallelogram of 54 ares 52 centiares; but this extent, chosen arbitrarily, in order to have more varied examples of cultivation, can change without there being the slightest alteration in the rotation or succession of crops we indicate; only, when the land is smaller, only one bed of each vegetable will be planted, instead of two or three. To make the work easier, we have combined crops of the same species wherever possible. Crop rotation should be carried out in such a way that one bed does not produce the same vegetables two years in a row. From every point of view, the kitchen garden should be enclosed by walls to the north, east and west, giving us southern, western and eastern exposure. To the south, the wall can be replaced by a hedge. Walls must be at least 2.65 m above ground level, and must be plastered on the inside. If they are built of plaster or stone, the espaliers can be trellised with rags; but if they are of hard rubble, they must first be lined with trellis. The coping should be lined with tiles projecting from Om, 12 to Om, 15, to protect the fruit from the damaging effects of rain, and to preserve the grapes well into the future. By suspending cloths or mats from the wall and keeping them at a distance, you can protect the blossoming peach trees from spring frosts. The southern walls will be planted with peach trees. To the east, grapevines will be planted and taken to Thomery. To the west, peach trees will be planted, with pear trees in between. To the north, the walls will be lined with Pear trees, and when planting at this exposure, care must be taken to choose the earliest varieties, so that the fruit can reach perfect ripeness. You can also plant plum trees and a few late cherry trees, which ripen at a time when we are usually deprived of these fruits. A hole should be dug away from the vegetable garden, and as far as possible from view, to dispose of weeds, vegetable peelings and plant debris, which can be used as fertilizer once reduced to potting soil. An important point to observe in setting up a garden is water distribution. In the vegetable garden, this should be done via underground pipes, which should be laid in the paths, so that any necessary repairs can be made without disturbing the plantations. The water will be collected in one or more basins placed in the middle of the garden, or, as indicated on the plan, in a basin in the center n. 62, and in barrels placed at the end of boards n. 8, 9, 24, 25, 34, 35, 50 and 51. These arrangements are all the more important in view of the vital importance of watering, not only in times of drought, but also to accelerate germination of seedlings and facilitate the recovery of newly transplanted plants. The middle aisle will be 3 meters wide, while the circular and cross aisles will be only 2 meters wide. The small alleys around the beds should be Om.60 wide, and lined with sorrel, civet, watercress, parsley, chervil, wild chicory, salad burnet, strawberry plants, etc. The width of the beds should be 1.60 meters. The width of the beds will be 1u, 33, with a senlier of Om 35 between each one, which will suffice for passage; for the first row of each bed must always be at least Om, 10 from the edge. The flowerbeds n. 61 on either side of the middle and cross aisles will be Im, 65 wide, and lined with Boxwood. A row of cattail Pear trees will be planted, spaced about 8 meters apart, with a dwarf Apple tree between each Pear tree. The square formed by the first eight beds will be used for sowing and transplanting the vegetables and flowers needed for the beds and flowerbeds of the pleasure garden. N. 9 to 29 and 50 to 30. Planting beds for cultivation. N. 51. Site for diapers. N. 52. Currants to be planted. N. 53. Raspberry plantation. N. 54. 2m, 75 wide hillside for vegetable cultivation. N. 55. Shelf 2 metres wide, where a row of English Rhubarb will be planted. N. 56 and 57. Tray of same width to be cultivated. N. 58. Counter-row of vines, part of which can be heated each year. N. 59. Tray approximately 1 metre wide. N. 60. Rows of vines supported by stiles placed at the end of each plank, so as not to obstruct the paths. N. 61. Tray 1m, 65 wide. N. 62. Basin. N. 63. Manure entry door. N. 64. Path leading to pleasure garden. CHAPTER II. Timetable. In drawing up this calendar, our aim has been to indicate, in a manner that is at once succinct and precise, the various operations that must follow one another without interruption in the course of a year, so that vegetable, fruit and flower production is always abundant, and the soil never rests. To achieve this result, it was necessary to do more than outline the work involved in each month; it was also necessary to indicate with precision the time of sowing, that of harvesting, the place occupied by each type of crop, and the nature of the plants that should succeed those that have completed their period of vegetation. We believe we have achieved this last goal by referring to the numbers on the plan (see pl. 1), which leaves no uncertainty as to the choice of location to assign to each plant; and as the calendar indications would have been insufficient in the form we have given them, or would have required developments that a synoptic table does not include, we refer, for the cultivation specific to each species, to the special article devoted to it. Contrary to custom, we begin the year with the month of August, because that's when the horticultural year begins, whereas starting with the month of January, as is common practice, separates autumn work from that of spring, with which it is intimately linked, since it is the necessary preparation. AUGUST. Mean barometer height, 756 mill. 380 (1). Mean temperature, maximum - 21°,20. minimum - 160,46. Quantity of rain, 48 mill. 59. Hygrometer reading, 700.5. GENERAL WORK. - This month is one of those that demands the gardener's utmost care and activity. Not only does he maintain, by watering and basking, the vegetation of the plants he expects to harvest towards the end of the season, but he is also already busy sowing and planting the plants destined to overwinter and produce the following year. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - There is little need for beds in this month, and the only work to be done is to plant cauliflowers on the melon beds, and to make a mushroom wheel at n. 8. Coastal n. 9 - In the second fortnight, Scarole is planted, sown in the open ground in the second fortnight of July. (1) BOUVART, Observations météorologiques faites à l'Observatoire de Paris. Open ground, n. 14 - Dutch spinach is sown. N. 23: Savoy cabbage sown in July. N. 32: Turnips are sown. N. 37. Turkish Celery, sown in the first half of June. Sow bulbous chervil and pink radishes. White onions, winter red Romaine, Passion lettuce, Passion chicory, Chervil, York, beef and sweetbread cabbages are sown in the second fortnight. Carrot, Lettuce, Onion, Chive, Leek, Parsnip, Parsley, Crambe and Maritime seeds are harvested. FRUIT GARDEN. - Finish trellising espaliers; remove branches that tend to get carried away. Water peach trees and uncover fruit nearing maturity. Graft fruit trees and ornamental trees and shrubs. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - All care consists of watering, raking, hoeing, cutting lawns, putting autumn flowers in place, such as Balsamines, Daisies, Carnations, etc., if these tasks have not already been carried out; finishing carnation marcots. Graft tree peonies onto tubers of herbaceous peonies. (See page 74, the Grafting article.) Separate and replant double Juliennes, bed out seedling Carnations or plant them in flowerbeds. Plant Imperial Crowns and Martagon Lilies. Sow seeds of herbaceous Peonies; this method of propagation is extremely time-consuming, and is rarely used except to obtain new varieties. Sow large-flowered pansies in September, transplanting them to a nursery with good exposure, and planting them out only in spring. Also sow Pelargoniums, Calceolaria, Cinerea, Mimulus and Giroflées quarantaines, but in pots and under frames. GREENHOUSES. - Towards the end of the month, cut back and repot Pelargoniums and plants whose pots have been buried during the summer, so that they will be ready when you bring them into the greenhouse. Take cuttings of Pelargoniums, Calceolaria, white-flowered Chrysanthemums, Cuphea, Ageratum Mexicanum, Fuchsia, woody Veronica and Verbena. SEPTEMBER. Average barometer height, 156 mill. 399. Mean temperature, maximum - 170.87. minimum - 13°.74. Rainfall, 57 mill. 26. Hygrometer reading, 750.2. GENERAL WORK. - Gardening work is beginning to diminish, as the year draws to a close, and maintenance requires less diligence. Watering is becoming less frequent, and only takes place in the mornings; because of the cool nights, evening watering has to be stopped. On the other hand, it's harvest time: the seeds are ripe or about to be, and it's time to think about picking them. The careful gardener will lay out his greenhouses and fruitbeds to bring in his fruit, vegetables and plants when the time comes. We also need to make the necessary repairs to the trunks and panels, so that they'll be in good condition as soon as the cold weather arrives. fortnight, we top up the bed with potting soil and plant gotte lettuce, sown in the first fortnight of the month. As the cold approaches, these lettuces are covered with frames and given as much air as possible, to prevent rotting; with care, they can be kept until December. Coastal, n. 10 - Passion Lettuce, Passion Chicory and Winter Red Romaine, sown in August, are planted in the first fortnight. Open ground. - In the first days of the month, sow cauliflower, York and beef cabbage. Chicory is sown under cloches, but cold. We sow salad burnet and pink radishes on teens, and we continue to sow ordinary chervil, bulbous chervil, lamb's lettuce and spinach. Cardoons and Celery are being blanched. N. 56. Early carrots are sown. N. Al. Long leeks are sown. N. 42, 43, 44 and 45. Five rows of four-season strawberries are planted in each bed, and placed at Om, 35 on the row. N. 46 and 47. Plant four rows of Queen's Strawberry seedling, or any other large-fruited species, in each bed, and set them at Om, 50 on the line. Chicory, celery, beet, pear and cauliflower seeds are harvested. FRUIT GARDEN. - This month's work is limited to little else. Nevertheless, we are keeping an eye on Peach tree vegetation, in order to maintain the balance of the sap. Trees that had grown too vigorously during the previous month are grafted, and the fruit is protected from the voracity of birds and insects. We give the last hoeing. 1. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Same care and maintenance as in previous months. This is the best time to sow lawns with grass, as there is still enough time for it to cover the ground before winter, and in spring it is in a position to resist drought, which is very contrary to it. This is also the time to mow boxwood borders, sow Clarkias, Collinsias, Coréopsis, Crépis, Énothères, Alyssum maritimum, Gilias, Mufliers, Némophiles, Œillets de Chine, Silènes, Saponaire de Calabre. Thlaspis, etc., and prick out the plants in the nursery before winter, before planting them in the spring. Sow Anagallis, Brachycomes, Centranthus, Eucharidium, Leptosiphon, Nemesia, Nycterinia, Malopes, Phlox Drummondi, Schizanthus, Trachymene and Viscaria in pots and winter them under a frame. Also plant Iris Germanica, sow Giroflées quarantaines to transplant on teens, or in pots, which we bring in as soon as the cold sets in. Sow Ranunculus and Anemone in pots or in the ground. Separate and replant herbaceous Peonies, but in such a way that the buds are only covered by about Om, 5 or Om, 3 of soil. Plant Pancratiums Illyricum, bulbous Fumeterres and Alstræmerias, which can withstand full soil; also plant Daffodils Om, 5 or Om, 6 deep, as well as Muscaris, Hyacinths, Crocuses, early Tulips and Narcissus, which you want to heat through the winter. You can also take cuttings of Calceolaria, Ageratum Mexicanum, white-flowered Chrysanthemums and Verbena. GREENHOUSES. - Around the 15th, we bring in the warm greenhouse plants, which will suffer from the drop in temperature. Temperate greenhouse and orangery plants are repotted, so that they can be taken up before they are brought in, and panels are put back on greenhouses and tarpaulins. OCTOBER. Average barometer height, 754 mill. 465. Mean temperature, maximum - 14°.73. minimum - 90.46. Rainfall, 48 mill. 10, Hygrometer reading, 82°,5. GENERAL WORKS. - Start winter ploughing, planting work and changes to garden layout; make tree holes, plant even if in a hurry. Separate borders and clumps of perennials, prune hardy trees, start mowing hedges, cover delicate plants; harvest seeds, fruit and vegetables. If you have land to move for certain garden layouts, you can get started. In the evening, we make mats, so as to be able to cover the frames and greenhouses in the course of the following month. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - Green asparagus is first heated, and pineapple eyelets are planted in pots proportionate to the strength of each; immediately after planting, the pots are placed on a layer, and from this time until spring, the heaters must be reshuffled every month. Plants planted at the same time the previous year should be removed from the soil and placed in the greenhouse. In the first fortnight, we sow small black lettuces under cloches and on ados; we raise these lettuces without ever giving them air. At the same time, we sow Romaine verte maraîchère, and in the second fortnight Laitue rouge, Laitue gotte and Romaine blonde ou grise maraîchère. When the plant begins to have a few leaves, place three rows of bells along the length of the bed, and transplant around thirty plants under each row. For the first week after planting, give the plant some air by raising the bells by about Om, 3, then after this time, gradually increase to Om, 8, and only lower the bells when it freezes at 2 or 3 degrees. When properly cared for, this plant will be used for all plantings from December to the end of February. Early cauliflowers and cabbages are transplanted in the nursery. N. 6 Towards the end of the month or at the beginning of November, fine Chicory sown in the first fortnight of September and two rows of Cauliflower sown in the first days of September are planted on the ground, but under a frame. If, towards the end of the month, we fear frost, we should put the panels on the Lettuces planted n. 3. Open ground, n. 19 - Spinach is sown for spring. In the second half of the year, white onions sown in the second half of August are transplanted to n. 18, and chervil or lamb's lettuce are sown in between. The last sowings of bulbous Chervil are made. Towards the end of the month, or at the beginning of November, cut off the old Asparagus stems, and give each bed a light hoeing with a fork, then spread a good mulch of manure. Celery and cardoons continue to be blanched. FRUIT GARDEN. - There's nothing left to do to the trees until it's time to prune them; however, we can mark those we intend to remove, so that they can be lifted the following month. This is the time to pick the fruit and place it in the fruit garden, which should only be done when the weather is dry and as the trees stop vegetating. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Cut the stems of perennials that have stopped flowering. Clean, smoke and plough flowerbeds before planting plants that will flower in the spring. Collect leaves that have fallen into the pathways and give them a final facelift. Sow in place Cynoglosses, Larkspur, Poppies, Mahon's Clover, Adonides, etc. Redo borders of Mignardise, Boxwood, Marjoram, Thyme and Hyssop; wean Carnation marcots, and plant them in pots or in the ground. Plant Hyacinths at Om, 10 deep, and pot those intended for heating. Plant Spanish and English irises at Om, 3 deep; Crocus in pots, borders or lawns, where they will produce a charming effect in spring and will be deflowered before it is necessary to cut the lawn. Tulips should be planted about Om.6 deep. You should also plant Golden Garlic and Italian Scillium, and raise Gladioli planted in spring. As soon as the first frosts set in, remove Dahlias, Erythrins and Balisiers from the soil, and place them in the orangery or in a dry cellar to overwinter. Do the same with all greenhouse plants planted in the spring to line beds and flowerbeds. They should be pruned and potted; then, to restore them, put them under a panel for some time. GREENHOUSES. - Pelargoniums are brought in during the first fortnight, and orange, pomegranate and laurel trees during the second, unless there is premature cold. The most delicate plants, those that need air and light, are placed close to daylight, and the hardiest are placed behind. When all the plants have been brought in, we hoe the soil in the pots or crates, and give them a light watering. NOVEMBER. Average barometer height, 755 mill. 614. Average temperature, maximum - 10°,15. minimum - 4°,74. Quantity of rain, 55 mill. 87. Hygrometer status, 820.2. GENERAL WORKS. - We begin planting and plowing, as well as clearing the land for new planting. Care is taken to preserve plants that fear frost, and leaves are collected to make layers and cover frames. When you have a choice, Oak and Chestnut leaves are the ones to be preferred. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - We continue to heat green asparagus and start heating white asparagus. In the first few days of the month, we sow Peas under chassis, but in the ground, for replanting under chassis. We sow Georges Lettuce under cloches and on ados, and treat the plant as we have indicated for red Lettuce and gottes. Green Romaines are lifted and immediately replanted under cloches, but this time only twelve or fifteen are planted per cloche. If the Romaine blonde or gris plant advances too much, it must be transplanted a second time, as with the Romaines vertes. In the event of severe frosts, the bells covering the Lettuce and Romaine plants should be lined with dry old manure or leaves, and a stove raised to the surface of the bells should be used to line the back of the bed. The cover should be increased according to the intensity of the cold, and the bells uncovered when the sun is shining; but it is necessary to check first whether the plant is not affected by frost, because then, instead of uncovering, the cover should be increased and the plant should be allowed to thaw gradually. Open ground. - We pull out the Celery planted in n. 37, to replant it in rows in a trench; when heavy frosts come, we cover it with litter or leaves, which we remove when the weather becomes milder. N. 31. Towards the end of the month, we sow Michaux Peas, and, in the intervals, Lettuce for cutting. Artichoke stalks and the longest leaves are cut off, then mounded, an operation which consists in raising the soil around each clump, so that it is buried almost to the top. When there's a hard frost, we cover them with litter or leaves, which are removed when the weather is mild. To make sure you don't run out of supplies during frosts, all vegetables that could be damaged by frost or prevented from being pulled out, such as carrots, beets, turnips, chicory, scarole, celery and cardoons, should be pulled out and placed in a gauge, either in the vegetable greenhouse or in the vegetable garden. You'll need to cover with straw or leaves any plants you've straddled in the garden, such as leeks, salsify, scorzonera and turnips, and uncover them whenever the weather permits. Also, during the month, pull out the cabbages from which the apples are made, and put them in gauges, so as to be able to use them during the frosts. Later in the month, but as late as possible, cut cauliflower heads, which should only be done when the weather is dry, and put them in the vegetable greenhouse, where they can be stored until April. FRUIT GARDEN. - Prune old or debilitated trees, to prevent the sap from rising in the buds that need to be removed, and the tree from becoming exhausted. Grub out trees that you intend to remove. Start planting fruit trees in chalky, light or sandy soils, where it is always preferable to plant in autumn, except for Mulberry and Fig trees, which should only be planted in spring. Fruit trees intended for heating the following year should also be planted in pots. If a few panels are available in the first fortnight, they should be placed in front of the vine espalier; in this way, grapes can be preserved in all their beauty until January. Towards the end of the month, Fig trees can be stuffed, or, if the branches are flexible enough to be lowered to the ground, they can be secured with wooden hooks, then covered with soil. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Spade lawn edges. Pull up defoliated annuals. Plough flowerbeds and beds. Divide and replant perennials whose clumps have become too large. Put yellow Giroflées in place, as well as Hollyhocks, Poetic Carnations, Golden Corbels and Thlaspis. Finish planting tulips, hyacinths and daffodils. Sow the first Sweet Peas. Weed out Dahlias in the second half of the month, if the weather has been favorable enough to leave them in the ground until then. Butter the free-standing roses, which usually suffer in our winters. Start planting ornamental trees and shrubs, except for resinous trees, Catalpas, Magnolias grandiflora and Tulip trees, which should not be planted until spring. When it starts to freeze, pot carnations, large-flowered wallflowers and Cocardeau should be covered or placed in the greenhouse. GREENHOUSE. - All greenhouse plant care consists in renewing the air as often as possible, keeping them clean and only wetting them with the utmost reserve. We start covering the warm greenhouse at night, and continue until April. When, in the evening, the weather is clear, the wind is easterly, and the thermometer registers no more than 3 or 4 degrees above zero, cover all the frames and greenhouses with straw mats. DECEMBER. Average barometer height, 754 mill, 953. Average temperature, maximum- 70.93. Minimum -3°, 53. Rainfall, 43 mill, 60. Hygrometer reading, 870.5. GENERAL WORKS. - Work in the open ground is very limited; fertilizers can be transported and spread on the soil in which they are to be buried. Plowing continues, frost permitting. Towards the end of the month or at the beginning of January, the trenches are emptied of the old melon beds, so that the site can be laid out. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - The cessation of work in the open ground gives gardeners time to take care of their beds, which require all their care. As soon as the beds indicated for this month are made, they should be covered with panels, so that the manure will ferment more quickly; and, unless there is weather to the contrary, those on which you have sown or planted can be uncovered every day, taking care to cover before nightfall. Continue heating white and green asparagus. In the first fortnight, sow early Raves, and in the second fortnight Leeks. N. 3. around the 15th of this month, sow early short carrots on beds and under frames, and plant small black lettuce sown in the first fortnight of October. N. 4 Plant clumps of sorrel on beds and under frames. N. 5. in the first fortnight, transplant Peas sown in the first days of November to the ground, but under a frame. Open ground. - At the onset of frost, Broccoli are lifted into clods and planted close together, deep enough for the stem to be buried up to the first leaves. Cardoons are tied, lifted into clods and moved into the vegetable greenhouse. Parsley is covered with straw or leaves, and when the weather is mild, artichokes are uncovered a little; but it's wise to cover them in the evening; and, if the frost increases, they should be covered with a greater quantity of leaves or litter. We continue to sow Michaux Peas, and in the first fortnight we plant at n. 12 York Cabbages sown in the first days of September. FRUIT GARDEN. - Planting continues, and in heavy, clayey or damp soils, holes are made only for planting in February or March. The soil is ripped, smoked and ploughed, and towards the end of the month the mobile greenhouses are placed on the Vines and espaliered fruit trees to be heated. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Continue with layout changes, prune trees, break up old lawns for reseeding, repair damaged paths and continue planting. Renonculas can be planted in the second fortnight. GREENHOUSE. - Maintain the temperature of the pineapple greenhouse, fill the strawberry shelves, renew the air in the temperate greenhouse and orangery when it's not freezing, and close them before the sun disappears. Cover Camellias, Rhododendrons, Magnolias and other hardy shrubs. Although they can remain three months without light and without any care, it is nevertheless a good idea to visit them occasionally to remove the mildew that the absence of air can produce. JANUARY. Mean barometer height, 757 mill. 759. Mean temperature, maximum - 7°,10. minimum - 4°,41. Rainfall, 36 mill. 27. Hygrometer reading, 86°,5. GENERAL WORK. - This month is usually cold and wet; but, weather permitting, we continue the ripping and plowing that could not take place during the previous month; and we open the asparagus pits for spring; we also bring to the field manure destined to be buried; we pile up that which will be used to make layers, stoves, etc.. When the weather is severe enough to prevent outdoor work, the necessary repairs are made to the gardening tools, the seeds are cleaned and everything necessary for subsequent operations is prepared. The gardener who has greenhouses and beds has a constant occupation, and at this time of year they require all his care. If the weather is wet, you must remove the bedding covering the artichokes and cover them as soon as you fear the return of the cold. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - Layers must be cared for, stoves must be refilled, and those that don't need refilling must be filled so that they are always as high as the surface area of the panels. Unless the weather is unfavorable, uncover the panels every day, and give the plants some air when the sun is shining by lifting the panels on the side opposite the wind; it's a good idea to cover them before frost forms on the glass. We start heating pineapples that are ready to bear fruit, continue heating white and green asparagus, and plant Marjolin potatoes. The first fine Chicory is sown on very hot beds, for planting under frames. Parsley is also sown in trays, or new plants are planted. Continue planting sorrel clumps. Early Peas are sown in cloches or under frames, to be transplanted into the ground, and towards the end of the month, frames are put over the Strawberry plants that we want to force into the ground. N. 4 In the second half of the month, transplant Dutch Beans sown in the first half of the month, on a bed and under a frame. N. 7. we make a layer on which we place three rows of bells. Under each bell, plant four small black lettuces (sown in the first fortnight of October), and in the middle a green Romaine, chosen from those sown in the first days of October. Open ground. - There is little work to be done in the open ground; however, if it doesn't freeze towards the end of the month, you can plant York and Oxheart cabbages, sown in the first few days of September, and Romaine verte, sown in the first fortnight of October, in light soil with good exposure; then sow early carrots and leeks among them. FRUIT GARDEN. - Continue planting and, weather permitting, start pruning espalier and cattail apple and pear trees when it's not freezing, continuing until March, always starting with the least vigorous; remove dead wood and destroy caterpillar nests. Cut branches for grafting, or take cuttings and bury them in sand to the north, away from frost and atmospheric influences. When cold weather or other circumstances prevent trees intended for planting from being put in place immediately, they should be laid out in gauges and their roots covered to protect them from frost. Dull the trees and treat them with lime milk, to destroy lichens and insects that attach themselves to the bark. For the same purpose, whiten the walls of espaliered trees, which can be done with a garden syringe similar to the one used to wash greenhouse plants. Start heating Vines, Cherry, Peach, Plum and Fig trees. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - There is little work to be done in the harsh season; however, we take advantage of the favorable weather to do the groundwork, plow the beds and bury the leaves. Planting can also continue. In the second half of this month, we can start sowing Madagascar Periwinkle on a hot bed. GREENHOUSE. - Put potted Hyacinths, Narcissus, early Tulips, Bengal Roses, Lilacs, Rhododendrons, etc., whose flowering is to be brought forward, under chassis or in the greenhouse. Uncover the greenhouses every day, unless the weather is adverse, and, when the sun is shining, give the orangery and the temperate greenhouse a little air; lastly, carefully visit the plants placed in the greenhouses, and make sure they are not affected by rot. FEBRUARY. Average barometer height, 757 mill. 706. Mean temperature, maximum - 70.08. minimum - 00.94. Quantity of rain, 40 mill. 50. Hygrometer reading, 830.2. GENERAL WORKS. - Finish all ploughing that could not be done during the previous month, and complete work that winter has suspended; you must hurry to finish them, so as not to be stopped when vegetation starts up again. Whenever the weather permits, give air to artichokes and celery, which you cover when you fear frost. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - Same care as last month. We sow the first frame melons and cucumbers; we continue to heat white asparagus, sow fine chicory, Dutch beans to be planted under frames, wild chicory, pink radishes and early carrots on beds, but in the open air. We sow kohlrabi, red cabbage, savoy and Brussels sprouts. Towards the end of the month, Aubergines are sown, and early Cantaloupe melons, sown in the first days of the month, are planted at No. I. Coastal. - If the weather does not permit earlier planting, green Romaine, sown in the first fortnight of October, is planted at no. 9, and cauliflowers, sown in the first fortnight of September, are counter-planted in the Romaine; then early Carrots and Leeks are sown among the rest. Open ground. - Sowing starts again this month, and has grown in importance; we are beginning to butter Crambes to blanch them. N. 15. Early carrots and radishes are sown. N. 21. Beef heart cabbage is planted in the first days of September, and after planting, spinach is sown between the cabbages. N. 22. plant York sprouts, also sown in early September, and sow with chervil or spinach. N. 34. sow early dwarf broad beans. N. 48. Sow Scorzonera. N. 49. Sow Salsify. Towards the end of the month or in the first days of March, sow n. 40 with Oignon jaune des Vertus; n. 14 with Pois Michaux de Hollande, sown on a bed in January or February, and n. 29 with Pommes de terre Marjolin or fines hâtives. FRUIT GARDEN. - Start planting in heavy, clayey or damp soils; continue pruning Pear and Apple trees, and start pruning Apricot, Plum, Cherry, Peach and Vine trees. Prune redcurrant bushes and cut back raspberry bushes, to obtain more fruit. Dull trees and apply whitewash, if this operation has not been carried out at the time previously indicated. Plant Müriers, finish ploughing. Stratify almonds to be planted in spring, and sow pear and apple trees. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Plough copses and beds, as well as lawns to be sown at the end of the month. Prune hardwood roses, such as centenaries, Provins, non-remontant hybrids, etc. Replant borders of Boxwood, Lavender, Sage, Hyssop, Mignardise, Pâquerette, etc. Plant resinous trees. Planting can continue until March. Sow larkspur, poppies, Mahon wallflowers, Thlaspis, reseda, if not sown in autumn. Plant Ranunculus, Om, 05 deep. Sow in the bed: Giroflées quarantaines, Amarantes, Pervenches, Petunias, Mimulus, Lobélias; almost all annual plants, except autumn ones; then, when the seedling is strong enough, transplant it in the nursery, to be planted out only in April and May. Cuttings of Coleus, Lantana, Lobelia, Heliotropes, Nierembergia, Petunia, Salvia and Verbena are also taken from the bed and, if properly cared for, can be planted in the pleasure garden in May. GREENHOUSES. - Renew the air in the temperate greenhouse and orangery, remove dead leaves. Hoe pots and water moderately. MARCH. Average barometer height, 755 mill. 852. Mean temperature, maximum - 9°,94. minimum - 20.66. Quantity of rain, 39 mill. 89. Hygrometer reading, 75°,0. GENERAL WORKS. - This month's work is the first of the year to require the full activity of gardeners. We must hurry to finish ploughing, bury manures and fertilizers and redo the borders everywhere. Plants that have been ridged and covered to protect them from frost are uncovered; seedlings and plantations must still be covered with a small layer of compost or light mulch, to protect them from spring frosts and sunst, and seedlings that have been stored in a gauge or in the vegetable greenhouse are planted. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layering. - During this month, the beds require a great deal of supervision, as the frames often have to be shaded during the day and covered at night. We raise the cauliflower, bean, etc. trays whenever necessary; and if, towards the end of the month, the weather is favorable, we remove the cauliflower panels; but, as at this time of year the nights are often very cold, we have to cover them with straw mats during the night and in bad weather. We start heating second-season pineapples. We continue to sow frame melons, cucumbers, eggplants and wild chicory. Potato cuttings are taken, and towards the end of the month, Tomatoes and Peppers are sown. N. 7 In the first fortnight, three Lettuces gottes sown in the second fortnight of October are planted under each bell. N. 2. in the second fortnight, we plant on a trench bed Melons sown in the second fortnight of February, on which we bring back the frames that were on the Carrots sown in n. 3. Coastal. - Sow a row of parsley along the wall. Open ground - Potatoes and all seed carriers are planted. Sow white onions, Saint-Denis, quintal, Milan and Brussels sprouts, lettuces, Romaine, parsnips, celery, pear blond, spinach, chives, peas, broad beans, salsify, scorzonera, wild chicory, salad burnet, parsley and chervil. N. 13. Two rows of Crambes are planted. N. 14. Sow pink radishes in the first fortnight. N. 17. Blonde Romaine is sown towards the end of October. N. 20. Plant red lettuce sown around October 15, and counter-plant cauliflower sown in the first fortnight of September. N. 25, 26, 27, 28: Plant four rows of Dutch asparagus, spaced about Om, 40 apart along the row. N. 32. Auvergne peas and wrinkled peas are sown. 39. Semi-long Carrots are sown. Broccoli and turnips are sown in the second fortnight. FRUIT GARDEN. - Finish planting, complete pruning of fruit trees, except for those that are too vigorous; plough the base of espalier trees. Graft Vines, and at the time of the first movement of the sap, make the transverse incision to develop the branches necessary for the framework of the trees that you want to raise in a regular shape. Plant Fig trees and uncover stuffed ones; cut out dead wood and cut back branches that are too thin. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - In addition to the work indicated at the top of this month's calendar, complete the planting of trees, shrubs and perennials; completely clean and sand the paths. Uncover all roses that have been stuffed or stubbed out. Finish pruning them, except those that have suffered from the cold; wait until they have started to vegetate, and prune them long rather than short, except to shorten them a fortnight later. Rebuild boxwood borders and plough heather beds. Sow grass lawns in areas where it has not been possible to sow in autumn. Plant Ranunculus and Anemones that have not yet been planted. Plant Tigridias Om.5 deep, Gladiolas Om.10 deep and St. Jacques Lilies in pots or in the ground Om.15 deep. Separate and replant perennials. (See page 69, Multiplication by roots.) 27 Sow Crépis, Malopes, Lavateres and Reseda, which you can continue to sow throughout the summer. Sow, for transplanting, Yellow Wallflowers, Chinese Carnations, and almost all the annuals you have previously sown in the bed. Sow Coreopsis and Thlaspis too, if you didn't sow them in autumn. Sow Tuberoses in the bed, and put Dahlias tubers under a frame to encourage bud development. Sow verbenas, balsamines, amaranths, amarantoids, Indian Senecon, zinnias, etc. on beds, and when the plants are strong enough, transplant them to the nursery bed for planting out in May. Also sow Cobeas, and as soon as they have a few leaves, transplant them into small pots and leave them on the bed until the end of April or the beginning of May, when they can be planted in the ground. You can also take cuttings of Coleus, Lantana, Lobelia, Heliotropes, Nierembergia, Petunia, Salvia, Verbena, when those taken in February do not promise a good recovery. GREENHOUSES. - In this month, there is no longer any need to light fires, as the sun has gained enough strength for its rays to heat up the atmosphere, and it is often necessary to shade the greenhouses, so as not to let the plant leaves burn. Watering will gradually become more frequent and abundant. We clean everywhere, and syringe plant leaves as often as the temperature demands. APRIL. Mean barometer height, 754 mill. 789. Mean temperature, maximum - 120.70. minimum - 50.59. Rainfall, 45 mill. 53. Hygrometer reading, 650.8. GENERAL WORKS. - We are continuing the work that was not completed during the previous month; but, as frosts are less to be feared, we can plant seedlings of all kinds. Weed out and thin out seedlings, mulch plantations to protect them from sunstroke, and, if watering is necessary, do it in the morning and during the day only, because of the cool nights. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - We sow Melons to be planted under cloches, Cucumbers, Gherkins and Pumpkins to be planted in the ground; under a frame, Flageolet Beans to be transplanted also in the ground; and on a bed, but in the open air, Fine Chicory and Cauliflower. N. 3 In the first fortnight, we plant Melons sown in the first fortnight of March, on which we bring back the frames which were on the Cauliflowers planted n. 6. N. 7 We plant under cloches two rows of Eggplants sown at the end of February. N. 4 In the second half of the month, we make a dull layer, and plant under cloches a row of Melons sown in the second half of March. N. 8. Towards the end of the month or at the beginning of May, plant a row of Potatoes, 2. Tray-band, n. 55. - A row of English Rhubarb is planted. Open ground. - Potatoes continue to be planted. We sow cauliflower and continue to sow Broccoli, Savoy, Brussels and Pomeranian cabbage, Peas, Broad Beans, Carrots, Radishes, Spinach, Lettuce and Romaine, Celery for cutting, Parsley, Chervil, Burnet, Wild Chicory; and, towards the end of the month, semi-hard Cauliflower, Alen cress, etc. N. 30. Savoy cabbage, sown towards the end of February. N. 37. Sorrel is sown. N. 50. Quintal cabbage sown around March 15. N. 51, 52, 53 and 54. Two rows of artichoke carnations are planted in each bed; they are placed one meter apart on the row. FRUIT GARDEN. - Complete pruning of vigorous trees and peach trees; use canvas or straw mats to protect flowering trees threatened by frost; finish plowing and planting; take cuttings and bedding; spread mulch to prevent drought; split graft cherry, plum, pear, apple, mulberry trees, etc.; transplant almonds that have been stratified. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Plant Magnolias grandiflora and Tulip trees, which, at this time of year, recover much better than at any other time. Complete ploughing and all clean-up work in the ornamental garden. Sow resinous tree seeds; sow (Fillets; repot or put in the ground the previous year's marcottes; sow in place large and dwarf Nasturtiums, runner beans, Volubilis, annual Lupins, Belles-de-nuit, Nigelles, etc.; and sow, for transplanting, India Carnations and India Roses, which can be sown successively until June; divide Chrysanthemum clumps. GREENHOUSES. - Give air when the weather permits, to strengthen plants that will soon be able to be exposed to the open air; start greenhouseing plants around mid-day, and give moderate watering. In the second fortnight, we start to bring out the less delicate plants. MAY. Average barometer height, 754 mill. 863. Mean temperature, maximum - 17°,67. minimum - 100,98. Quantity of rain, 56 mill. 80. Hygrometer condition, 700.0 GENERAL WORK. - We won't go into detail about this month's horticultural operations, which are many and varied. The gardener needs all his activity, and each part of the garden requires all his care. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - We make a layer of Om, 50 thick, which we cover with Om, 25 of good soil, to plant Pineapples in full ground, under frame. In the first fortnight, we sow Gherkins and the last Melons; then on a layer, but in the open air, we sow Fine Chicory, Meaux Chicory and Scarole; we make a dull layer at n. 5, and we plant under cloches a row of Melons sown in the first fortnight of April. In the second fortnight, a muted bed is made at n. 6, and a row of Melons, sown in the second fortnight of April, is planted, on which the cloches that were on the Aubergines planted at n. 7 are brought back. Côtière, n. 9 - In the second fortnight, white Cucumbers and green Gherkins, sown on beds in April, are planted. N. 11. Chervil is sown. N. 10. Beans are sown in clumps or rays in the second half of the year. Open ground. - The last potatoes are planted. Cardoons, kohlrabi, Turkish celery, black radishes, blond pear, golden purslane are sown, and cauliflower, broccoli, savoy, Brussels and Pomeranian cabbages, carrots, radishes, spinach, lettuces and Romaine, peas, broad beans, sorrel, parsley and watercress continue to be sown. Around the 15th, potatoes can be planted in the ground. In the first days of the month, we plant, at n. 15, semi-fine Chicory sown on beds in the first days of April. N. 19. Blonde Romaine sown in the second half of April is planted, and towards the end of the month, cauliflowers sown on an old bed towards the end of April are counter-planted. N. 35. Chicory always white is sown. N. 33. Row beans are sown. N. 23. Dwarf beans are sown. In the second fortnight, we plant, at n. 22, Grey Lettuce sown in the first fortnight of the month, and we counter-plant two rows of Tomatoes sown on beds towards the end of March. N. 17: Fine Chicory sown on beds towards the end of April, and four rows of Celery root sown on beds in February. N. 24. Two rows of Cardon de Tours are sown immediately in place, and three rows of Romaine sown in the first fortnight of the month are counter-planted. N. 41. Plant one row of pumpkins sown in April. N. 36. Towards the end of the month or at the beginning of June, plant four rows of Pomeranian cabbage sown towards the end of April. N. 51, 52, 53 and 54. Between each row of artichokes, plant a row of Brussels sprouts sown towards the end of April. FRUIT GARDEN - In addition to general work, the gardener must take care to maintain the balance between the different parts of his trees and to encourage their development. Disbudding begins; the first hoeing operations are carried out, and budding begins. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Plant the last evergreen Magnolias. In the second half of the month, plant Dahlias; but there is no advantage in planting them earlier, as they often fail to bloom by September, when they are at their most beautiful. Start mowing the lawns, which should then be cut as often as possible, because if you want beautiful lawns, you must avoid letting them go to seed. Now is the time to plant Erythrina, Balisier, Pelargonium zonale, Heliotrope, Calceolaria, Coleus, Petunia, Verbena, white-flowered Chrysanthemum, etc., in the open ground. Prune Lilacs and Ribes sanguineum as soon as they are out of bloom, as they only produce beautiful flowers on young wood. Replant yellow Amaryllis in borders. Sow Giroflées quarantaines, then Giroflées grosse espèce, and transplant them in the nursery towards the end of June; in September, raise them to plant them in pots. GREENHOUSE. - Bring out the orangery plants and some of the Pelargoniums (leaving the more advanced ones in the greenhouse), and when the first flowers begin to bloom, bring them in to enjoy the beauty of their bloom, which lasts for the whole month, and sometimes for the first fortnight of June. During the middle of the day, a canvas is laid over the greenhouse to protect the flowers from the sun's heat. Around the 15th, we take out the orange trees, and a few days later the hot-house plants, which can spend four months of the year outside. We proceed with repotting, and return to the greenhouse those plants that cannot remain outdoors in the Paris climate. Remove the frames from temperate greenhouses, and uncover the covers for Camellias, Rhododendrons, etc. JUIR. Average barometer height, 756 mill. 966. Mean temperature, maximum - 21°,19. minimum - 14°, 42. Rainfall, 54 mill. 34. Hygrometer reading, 67°,8. GENERAL WORK. - We refer to each of the sections dealing with cultivation specific to that month for 35 all the work to be done. Maintaining cleanliness by weeding, hoeing and raking; not sparing with watering when the weather is dry; hunting down harmful animals and insects: these are the general tasks requiring the gardener's attention. VEGETABLE GARDEN. Layers. - In this month, we can do without making new beds, as the plants do well in the open ground; we remove the boxes and frames, and after harvesting the Melons planted n. 1, we plant two rows of Cauliflower sown in May; then Chicory or Scarole sown on beds in the first fortnight of May. Open ground. - We sow Chicorée de Meaux and Scarole, Rapunzel, Vaugirard cabbage and cauliflower for autumn, and Ciboule and Poireau for winter. Broccoli, Savoy and Brussels sprouts, early carrots, Turkish celery, pink, Madras and black radishes, lettuces, Romaine, turnips, sorrel, purslane, chervil, Alençon cress, peas and beans continue to be sown; it's even time to sow all those you want to net. In the first fortnight, plant four rows of Romaine (sown in the second fortnight of May) in n. 21, and in the second fortnight, plant three rows of Poirée à cardes (sown in the first days of the month) in the Romaine, at Om,50 on the line. N. 18. four rows of Lettuce sown in the second half of May. N. 31. Turnips are sown. N. 30. Beans are sown. In the second fortnight, four rows of Turkish Celery sown in the first days of May are counter-planted in the Chicory planted n. 15. N. 14. four rows of Scarole sown in the first days of the month are transplanted, and a few days later, kohlrabi sown towards the end of May is counter-planted. Towards the end of the month or in the first few days of July, we sow Chicorée de Meaux (n. 29) immediately in place. Later in the month, Rhubarb stalks are cut to make jam. Chervil, watercress and lamb's lettuce seeds are harvested. FRUIT GARDEN. - We start trellising vines and espaliered trees, finishing towards the end of the season. We continue disbudding, pinching and removing useless buds, the only way to keep trees looking good and producing a guaranteed product. We pinch off the terminal buds of Fig trees, to ensure fruiting. Towards the end of the month, or at the beginning of July, we prune Muriers whose leaves have been used to raise silkworms. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Mow lawns, hoe flowerbeds and beds, water annuals and perennials, stake Dahlias, Hollyhocks, etc. Graft all varieties of roses. Prune overgrown Chinese Glycines later this month. Transplant seedling carnations, repot Chrysanthemums and cut them back; lift Belladonna Amaryllis and replant shortly afterwards. Sow Hollyhocks, Jerusalem Crosses, Dandelions, Campanulas, Golden Corbels, Delphinium, Poet's Carnations, Forget-me-nots, Perennial Linen, Primroses and all perennials suitable for the pleasure garden. GREENHOUSE. - Take Pelargoniums out of the greenhouse as soon as they have deflowered, put them in a shady spot for a few days, then place them in a well-ventilated position; to prevent the soil in the pots from drying out, bury them about halfway down. If you don't want to remove the frames from the Pelargonium greenhouse, you can reline it with double Laurel-roses, which will flower better and more reliably than in the open air. JULY. Average barometer height, 756 mill. 193. Mean temperature, maximum - 21°,19. minimum - 16°,99. Rainfall, 47 mill. 21. Hygrometer reading, 680.2. GENERAL WORK. -Same as June. Redouble activity and care, as all parts of gardening are of equal importance, and demand the gardener's solicitude. VEGETABLE GARDEN. - Layers. In the first fortnight, remove the manure from layer n. 2, fill the trench with the soil taken from it, plough the whole, and, after having dressed the ground, plant Cauliflower sown in the second fortnight of June; then counter-plant Chicory or Scarole sown in the first fortnight of June. N. 3. in the second fortnight, the manure is also removed from the bed, and, as in n. 2, the trench is filled in and cauliflowers sown in the first fortnight of the month are planted; then Chicory or Scarole sown in the first fortnight of the month are counter-planted. FULL GROUND. - Final sowings of Savoy cabbage, Carrots, Chicory, Scarole, Roman lettuce, and continue to sow Turnips, Rapeseed, Pink Radish, Rat-tail Radish, Golden Purslane, Chervil, and anything else that can mature before winter. N. 18. In the first days of the month, we counterplant the Lettuce with Chicory or Scarole sown in the first fortnight of June; then we plant on each side of the bed a row of Vaugirard Cabbage sown in the second fortnight of June. N. 20. Rapunzel is sown. In the second fortnight, four rows of Romaine blonde or Lettuce sown in the first fortnight of the month are planted at n. 16, and two rows of Cauliflower sown in the first fortnight of June are counter-planted. Harvest seeds for sorrel, spinach, cabbage, peas, salsify, scorzonera, etc. FRUIT GARDEN. - Visit the espaliers, maintain the balance between the different parts of the trees, trellis, disbud, uncover without stripping them the fruits whose ripening you want to accelerate. During hot spells, water the trees at the base and syringe the leaves in the evening. From the end of the month to mid-September, graft cherry, plum, peach, apricot, pear and apple trees. ORNAMENTAL GARDEN. - Start disbudding Dahlias; plant Lilies Om, 15 or Om, 20 deep as soon as they are defoliated. Remove Ranunculus, Anemones, Daffodils and Daffodils as soon as the leaves have dried out. Sow perennial Lupins as soon as the seeds have ripened; in autumn, transplant them into pots, which you can then overwinter in the orangery or under a chàssis, and in spring put them in the ground. Towards the end of the month or in August, marcot Carnations and start grafting Roses, in dormant-eye patches. GREENHOUSES. - Greenhouse plants are almost all outside and require only watering. Those that remain in the greenhouse should be given fresh air, sheltered from the sun's rays and watered as needed. CHAPTER III. Gardening tools. All the instruments listed in this chapter are indispensable for cultivating a garden; and, although we have no intention of charging those who draw information from our book, we advise them to obtain most of them, in order to simplify operations. § I. - Tools for ploughing and planting. BÈCHE DE SOISSONS. - The blade is a little hollowed out in the middle; it is Om, 27 long by 0.20 wide at the top, and Om, 16 at the bottom. Instead of having a socket, the blade is fixed to the handle with two riveted pins. BÈCHE DE SENLIS. - The handle is 1 meter long, not including the part inserted in the socket; the iron is Om, 30 high, Om, 22 wide at the top and Om, 18 at the bottom. BINETTE. - The hoe is a small hoe whose blade is just Om, 16 long by Om, 12 wide. It is used to stir up the soil between plants whose rows are a little apart, and to make holes for planting beans and potatoes. FANG HOE. - This hoe has a double blade, with a cutting edge on one side and two long teeth on the other. HOYAU. - A tool for making trenches, uprooting trees and preparing compact soils for ploughing with a spade. The handle has Om, 76, and the blade, which forms a right angle with the handle, Om, 32. HOULETTE. - The houlette is a small spade whose blade, about Om, 15 long, Om, 10 wide, is folded cylindrically on its sides: it is intended for lifting cloddy plants. PICKAXE OR TURNER. - This instrument is particularly used for earthworks; it is also useful for making holes and removing large trees. PLANTOIR. - To make a planter, select a tree branch bent at the end, then taper the part to be driven into the ground, and to make it more durable and penetrable, have it lined with iron or copper. TRIDENT TRACEOIR - The advantage of this instrument is that you don't have to move the chalk line as many times as you need rays in a board. To trace six rays, you need only stretch the string twice, and for five rays only once, placing it in the middle of the board. The spokes can be made more or less widely spaced, as the two side branches are fixed at the bottom by means of a hinge, and on the crosspiece by a small movable pin, which enables them to be moved further apart or closer together as required. SHEARING SCISSORS. - These are large scissors, Om 40 in length, whose handles form a very open angle with the blade; they are used for mowing hedges, lawns, borders, etc. The blades should have some play and not be too sharp. The blades must have some play and not be tightened by a nut. To use them, the blades must be pressed together with opposing forces. SIMPLE IRON-TOOTHED RAKE. - Used to clean paths, smooth the surface of newly-ploughed ground, then lightly cover seedlings. You need at least two of them, one about Om, 30, and the other about Om, 45. RATISSOIRE A POUSSER. - Handle, 1m, 40 long; blade, Om, 20. SHOOTING SHOVEL. - Used in areas where the earth is hardest. The blade, which is Om, 20 wide, is made from a piece of old scythe. The handle is 1m, 15 long. ROLLER. - The roller is a large cast-iron or stone cylinder, fitted at each end with a rounded auricle, turning like an axle in an iron loop; it is used with advantage for rolling earth and turf. SARCLOIR. - This instrument is used to weed between plants that are not sown too thickly. Its total length is Om, 25. SII. - Transport tools. WHEELBARROW WITH TRUNK. - The proportions of a wheelbarrow are: length, 1m,50 to 1m,60; width of the trunk, Om, 50 to Om, 55; spacing of the arms at their ends, Om, 65; outside diameter of the wheel, Ou, 48. HOOK FOR CARRYING BOXES. - These hooks are made of iron, and one end forms a loop through which a 2-meter-long stretcher is passed; with the help of these hooks, two men can easily transport very heavy crates. DIABLE. - This device is indispensable for entering and removing crates that cannot be transported using the hooks. To remove them, approach the device in such a way as to engage the chins under the crate; chock the wheels, and press down on the jib so that the crate is positioned obliquely; you can then easily direct it wherever you wish. FOURCHE ORDINAIRE (ORDINARY FORK) - This instrument is used to make beds, transport manure and harrow vegetable garden beds. HOTTEREAU (pronounced HOTTRIAU). - Used to transport manure and potting soil. In market gardens, it replaces the wheelbarrow. WOODEN SHOVEL. - As this instrument is more or less the same everywhere, we think it unnecessary to give its proportions; it is used to remove earth and potting soil, to pile them up, and so on. SIII. - Watering instruments. WATERING CAN. - This instrument, which must be made of copper to last longer, holds about ten liters of water. SPRINKLER for wetting greenhouses. It should be at least a third smaller than the apple watering can. To water seedlings and cuttings, use the apple instead of the extension spout. POMPE A MAIN ET A JET CONTINUUU, by M. Petit. - This instrument, which sprays water from a height of 5 or 6 meters, is used to water trees that are too high to use a syringe. SERINGUE for washing tree heads and orangery and greenhouse plants. Its total length is Om, E0. § IV. - Instruments for pruning and trimming trees. GROWING. - This instrument, whose blade is placed at the end of a more or less long handle, is used for pruning trees and weeding; but for the latter purpose, it is advantageously substituted for the weeder, whose operation is much less tiring. BUD CUTTER. - A type of secateur with curved blades, used for disbudding and facilitating this operation. ECHENILLOIR. - In certain circumstances, this instrument can be replaced by the crescent, but the latter is always less convenient. SEWER. - The choice of this instrument is of the utmost importance, for if it is badly made, it will skin the branches and harm the tree's vegetation. Its advantage over the pruning knife is that it makes pruning easier, but it cannot compete in all circumstances with the latter, which always makes sharper wounds. HAND SAW, designed to remove branches that cannot be cut with pruning shears. Its overall length is Om, 25. EGOHINE, used to cut branches in places where the hand saw cannot reach. SERPET. - Various sizes are made, but the most commonly used have a handle about Om, 12, long, and a blade from Om, 08 to Om, 09. SERPE. - Used for felling large branches, making the points of stakes and stakes, etc. CHAPTER IV. Ripping and Lahours. When establishing a new garden, the first thing to do is to assess the condition and depth of the soil, which is essential, especially if trees are to be planted. If the top layer of soil is poor, or has been under cultivation for a very long time, and the fertility has been exhausted, it must be broken up. If, as is often the case, the land is covered with tall grasses, they should be pulled up, piled together and burned when dry enough. The ashes should be spread over the site after breaking, which should be done in autumn or winter as follows: At one end of the site, open a trench 1.60 to 2 meters wide (called a gauge). Usually, all you need to do is remove two spade-heads, and dig the bottom of the trench before filling it. However, in some areas, the trench must be dug much deeper. The site is divided into two, three or four parts, depending on the number of workers. The earth from the first trench is deposited at the end where the work is to be completed, to fill the void in the last trench. Each trench is successively replaced by another of the same length, taking care to bring the soil from the bottom to the surface. Remove all the bad earth and stones, and place them in the large alleys, from which all the good earth will be removed. Once the earth has been broken up, give it a good blow with a fork to break up the clods and even out the surface; then rake out the stones, which will be used to fill in the alleys. 1. Ploughing - In light soils, deep ploughing should be carried out annually on fine winter days; in compact, damp soils, chains of ploughing should be carried out in autumn, i.e. the soil should be removed from the surface of the ground, and brought together in mounds or, better still, in parallel lines. Frosts will dry them out, and in spring they will be much easier to cultivate. In gardens, work is done with a spade. Before starting, remove soil to form a gauge a good spade-shank deep (Om 25 to Om, 30 approx.), Om, 30 to Om, 35 wide, and the length of a single man's plank. If two planks are to be ploughed side by side, the soil from the gauge is placed on the next plank and on the same end. If you only have one, place it at the end where you want to finish, so as to have enough to fill the last gauge. As it is also at this time of year that manure is buried, it should be spread evenly over the whole plot, which often makes it possible to plough during frosts; without this precaution, the cold would harden the surface of the soil and prevent any work. We plough backwards, taking the soil by spadeful, which we replace on the other edge of the gauge, turning it over each time so that the bottom one is on top, then we push manure into each gauge, taking care not to bury it too deeply, so that it is within reach of the roots. The clods of earth should be picked up with a spade, and any stones thrown aside. When ploughing, be especially careful to put the 3. soil from the paths into the bed, as it will have had a year's rest. For winter ploughing, the surface of the land should not be too even; only when planting or sowing is required, it should be levelled with a fork. Proceed as described for the entire garden, always maintaining the same gauge width. When ploughing at the foot of trees, care must be taken not to damage their roots. Whenever you want to follow one crop with another, you should plough the land, but not as deeply as in winter, and take care to remove weeds beforehand, whose seeds will germinate quickly once buried. In extremely thin soils, where some fertilizer is always necessary, it should only be used very sparingly. 2. Weeding. - Weeding consists in removing foreign plants and weeds from the soil. This operation is carried out by hand, and requires a certain amount of practice to distinguish at a glance which plants need to be removed and which need to be kept. It is easy to understand that this work must be very difficult when the soil is dry: that's why, in this case, you must take care to baste, at least one hour before starting this operation, the beds that need to be weeded. 3. Hoeing. - Hoeing is an operation no less necessary for vegetable plants than weeding; it is carried out with the hoe, and, as required, with the blade or teeth. The purpose of hoeing is to divide the soil surface, making it permeable to atmospheric influences and watering. In some circumstances (e.g. for transplanted plants), hoeing can replace weeding, and sometimes a rake can be used instead of a hoe. CHAPTER V. Manure and fertilizers. We will only discuss the most common fertilizers; the others, such as horn shavings, animal black, dried blood, bone powder, guano and perazote fertilizer, are used only in large-scale cultivation. The ones we recommend, and which are easiest to obtain, are decomposing plant debris. You can also use pond sludge and street sweepings, which are also good fertilizers, but only after they have been left in piles for a long time, stirred several times during the winter and matured by the influences of the atmosphere. The pomace of grapes and cider pears is also very good, when it has been left in the heap long enough to prevent the seeds from germinating once in the ground. Pigeon and hen droppings should only be used very dry and reduced to dust, and, like poudrette, lightly broadcast, and only in strong soils. This type of manure can only be used in very limited circumstances, whereas manure made from litter mixed with the urine and excrement of domestic animals can be found in abundance everywhere. The hottest manures are horse, mule, donkey and sheep; the most compact and coldest are beef, cow and pig. Whatever manure is used, we believe it should be buried only after fermentation, without waiting until it has been fully consumed; fresh manure does not act as fertilizer, but as an amendment; therefore, in strong, damp and cold soils, which need to be divided, only unconsumed manures should ever be used. Another disadvantage of these manures is that they still contain seeds whose fermentation has not been able to destroy the germinative principle, and which quickly cover the soil with weeds. For light, hot soils that need to be bound, use cow manure; if this is not available, use another type, but it should only be used when half consumed. Manure should be spread in all areas where it is to be buried, in winter and during the first frosts. Whatever fertilizer you use, you should apply as much as possible every year, especially to vegetable beds that are always under cultivation. Manures and leaves almost consumed from old beds should be reserved for spreading each year as mulch on flowerbeds and all growing areas, which is still a good fertilizer; but garden weeds should not be mixed with these manures until they have been completely reduced to potting soil. Fertilizers should be buried early enough so that they have had time to consume themselves before plant roots can reach them, especially fleshy roots. Some vegetable plants, such as Leeks and Onions, do better in smoked soil from the previous year. Watering. 49 As water is one of the main agents of vegetation, it is indispensable in a garden. When you don't have a watercourse to dispose of as you please, it is necessary to obtain water by every possible means. Rainwater, which is more beneficial to vegetation than any water flowing on the surface of the ground, must be collected with care; to this end, all roofs must be fitted with gutters, so as not to let any rainwater escape. The water should be collected in a reservoir placed at a certain height, to facilitate the distribution of water through pipes to all parts of the garden. In the absence of running or gushing water, you will have to resort to well water. In this circumstance, you will need to consider the best way of drawing it; for it is deplorable that in many localities we are still reduced to the necessity of using rope and buckets, an exercise that is as long as it is tiring; whereas with a triple-acting pump, we can easily obtain 12 to 1,500 liters of water per hour. It's true that you need a horse to do this, and that many people are so unconcerned about their gardener that they'd rather leave their horses in the stable than put one at his disposal for a few hours each day during the summer months; however, this would be highly advantageous, as well water almost always contains carbonate and often sulfate of lime; In some places, these substances are so abundant that the water deposits a layer of calcareous salts on the soil and plant leaves, preventing the roots from benefiting from atmospheric influences and the leaves from fulfilling their physiological functions, which sometimes leads to the loss of crops, or more often to a state of languor no less detrimental. In such cases, it is essential to have a reservoir so that the water can be used only a few hours after it has been drawn off, allowing the harmful substances it contains to settle. It's also a good idea to let the water warm up in the sun; for the watering of delicate plants or those grown on beds, the water should never be less than 8 to 10 degrees hot. The same is not true for large vegetables; on the contrary, the water should be used as soon as it is drawn from the well, otherwise it would activate their vegetation too much, and they would not be able to develop to their full potential. Soil fertility can be enhanced by watering with manure, cow dung, sheep dung or other animal matter. Pellets, poudrette and guano, used in small doses of around 3 or 4 kilograms per hectoliter of water, also make a good liquid fertilizer, and can be used to water potted or outdoor plants once a week during the summer. The quantity and frequency of watering cannot be determined in advance; we can only say that it should be more or less abundant, depending on the temperature and nature of the soil. To ensure that plants benefit as much as possible from watering during the hot days of June, July and August, water only in the afternoon; in spring and autumn, when nights are usually cool, water in the morning. The most frequently used watering cans, especially for wetting seedlings and recently transplanted plants, are those with knobs; spouted watering cans are used for wetting potted plants. Apple watering cans are used to wet delicate, newly transplanted plants. CHAPTER VII. Diapers. In northern climates, where vegetation is suspended by the cold for varying lengths of time, gardeners resort to artificial means to compensate for the sun's heat and produce premature crops. Even in the humblest garden, it is impossible to do without a bed, even if only to sow certain flower or vegetable seeds that cannot succeed in the open ground or only produce late. I. LAYERS FOR EARLY PLANTS. - Layers should always face south, and the site on which they are planted should be dug out by about Om 20. Their thickness depends on several circumstances: 1° Those made in December, January and February should be thicker than at any other time of the year; 2° On cold, damp soil, they should be thicker than on sandy soil; 3° The narrower they are, the thicker they should be. We usually make them Im, 30 wide, plus a path of Om, 40 which we leave between each one and which we fill with manure; to maintain and revive the heat, we surround the layers with stoves of new manure, which we renew from time to time. New horse manure, i.e. that which comes out of the stable, is preferred for the layers: the more it is soaked with urine, the better it is. We mix it with half tree leaves, grape marc or a third of manure from old layers. The heat is not as strong as with manure alone, but it lasts much longer, is more regular, and you have less to fear from excessive heat development, which can sometimes lead to the loss of young plants. In the absence of manure, you could even make do with leaves or grape marc. New manure alone should be used only for warmers and some seedlings, such as melons. Before starting to build up a layer, to mix the manures evenly, place them as close as possible to the place they are to occupy. Always work backwards, taking care to mix the dry parts with those most impregnated with urine, and to distribute the manure evenly. The edges of the layer should be raised vertically; and as soon as a bed of manure has been formed, it is wetted more or less, as required, with the apple watering can, so that everything is damp enough to produce prolonged fermentation and prevent the manure from drying out in the center, which could compromise the result of the operation. To give the layer an even density at all points, it is tamped down with the feet and the back of the fork; then manure is added in the hollow places, so that the thickness is even. The same is done with each bed, until the layer has reached the desired height, after which the paths are filled in and the troughs are placed. Once the coffers have been placed, the layer is loaded with potting soil; then the panels are laid, which must be kept covered for a few days to facilitate fermentation. Before sowing or planting on a new layer, it is advisable to wait until the initial heat has moderated. If, in spite of this precaution, the heat develops too much, the stoves should be quickly removed from the trunk; and if this is not enough, a few watering cans should be poured around the bed to cool it down. Thermosiphon. - In some circumstances, manure layers can be replaced by thermosiphon heating. This is usually done by laying a very thin layer (1) to protect the plants from the dampness of the soil, and then running the pipes over the layer. Alternatively, a wooden floor can be laid underneath (1) Figure 2 shows a double-walled copper boiler filled with water; the A feed pipe is also used to introduce the water into the boiler; it communicates with the B circulation pipes by means of an elbow of the same diameter. As soon as it is exchanged, the water in the boiler expands, pushing the water in the pipes up to point C, where it is circulated by the thermosiphon pipes; but plants grown on this floor require too frequent watering; This is why we think it would be better (for crops where it would be necessary to heat the soil) to run the pipes in the paths, i.e. between the chests; and in this case we would cover them with boards and straw, or any other poor conductor of caloric. What leads us to believe that this method would be applicable to the cultivation of vegetables forced under panels is that, for certain crops, it is only by means of stoves that the heat required by the plants can be obtained. II. MUTED LAYERS. - It's hardly until April that we start using these kinds of beds. To establish them, we make a trench Om, 75 to I mêtre wide and about Om, 35 deep. The same materials are used to make them, as are used to heat the boiler and circulate it back through the pipes when it is sufficiently hot. As for the pipes, when the course is of a certain extent, they are given a flat shape, in order to heat a larger surface area than in cylindrical pipes, and to contain a smaller quantity of water that can reach boiling point more quickly than in the latter. These pipes are generally given a thickness of Om,02 to Om, 03 over a variable height of Om, 10 to Om, 15, depending on the cube of air and the need to raise the temperature. Pipes measuring Om, 21 by Om, 2 are quite commonly used. In fact, cylindrical pipes with an external surface corresponding to that of the Om, 21 high on Om, 2 pipe contain 4 liters 20 of water, and the same length of pipe with a similar external surface, i.e. with a diameter of Om, 15, would contain 15 liters 17. It's true that the hot water contained in the cylindrical pipe will cool less vitc than in the other; but also it will have been necessary to burn more fuel to bring it to the boil without having obtained more heating surface, and consequently more heat, the previous ones; they are given Om, 60 to Om, 80 in thickness; they must be slightly domed in the middle. They are filled with potting soil or good earth, depending on the type of crop to be grown; then covered with a long bed of manure to concentrate the heat. 1. stove. - Throughout the cold season, i.e. from the end of November to mid-April, it is necessary to maintain or revive the warmth of the layers, without having to repeat them. This is achieved by means of reheaters, which consist, as we said earlier, of filling the paths around the layers with new or annealed manure, and reshuffling them every fortnight or every week; then, as required, adding a portion of new manure each time. In doing so, the state of the atmosphere must be taken into account, i.e. if the weather is dry, wet manure should be used, and if it's damp, dry manure; then care should be taken to cover them with straw mats during bad weather, in order to concentrate the heat. 2.
