Teens. - Ados are a safe and economical way to encourage the cultivation of early crops: plants do better there than on horizontal ground. They consist of a slope of Im, 33, facing the sun. To set up an ados, proceed as follows: after choosing a favorable site, the soil is ploughed well, taking care to remove the soil necessary to recharge the backside by about Om, 20; after which the soil is united; then about Om 10 of earth mixed with loam is spread over the whole. These ados are first used to sow Radishes, and then three rows of bells are placed to sow lettuce and transplant young plants. CHAPTER VIII. Plant propagation. Under this heading, we cover the series of operations involved in multiplying plants, but we have only given each of them a scope proportionate to the actual difficulty they present. We invite our readers to read this chapter carefully, so as to become fully acquainted with the principles it sets out; and, by faithfully following our instructions, they will acquire the manual skill necessary to count on certain success. S I. - Sowing. Whatever the method of sowing, preparation of the soil is a preliminary operation of the utmost importance. The soil must be carefully ploughed, so that the clods are well divided, and after ploughing, the stones and clods on the surface are removed with a fork and a rake. Most vegetable seeds can be sown in spring, then successively, at intervals calculated according to the length of vegetation of each plant. With the exception of some lettuces, vegetables that are to be eaten in the same year should not be sown later than July. Before sowing, therefore, it is necessary to know not only how long the seeds will germinate, but also how long it will take for the plants to reach full development. The time of sowing should then be brought forward or back according to the nature of the soil; for the colder and wetter the soil, the later the sowing and the less the seeds need to be covered; and the finer the seeds, the less they need to be buried; for some, it is even sufficient to spread a little potting soil over them after harrowing and treading; others need not be covered, but only shaded with a little litter. There are two main methods of sowing: overplanting and sowing in the ground. I. SOWING ON A BED. - As it is often necessary to sow at a time when the temperature does not allow the seeds to be delivered to the soil, it is necessary to sow on a bed. Although the heat of the bed must vary according to the different species of seed, we can say that 12 to 15 degrees seems to be the most favorable temperature (except for Melons, Eggplants and Chicory, which require more heat); for all the vegetable seeds we have subjected to this temperature have succeeded perfectly. Sowing on a bed does not differ in any way from sowing in the ground, i.e. the seeds must always be covered in proportion to their fineness. These seedlings are often much more successful than those sown in the ground, because the temperature, light and humidity conditions required for perfect seed development can be altered at will. II. SOWING IN THE GROUND. - These seedlings are broadcast, in rows or rays, and in pockets. 1. Broadcast sowing. - The soil is prepared as described above, and a small amount of fine soil is raked to the edge of the bed. A handful of seeds is then taken and spread over the soil, passing between the fingers with a brisk, regular back-and-forth movement. In order to sow more evenly and avoid spilling seeds into the pathways, sow the width of the bed in two passes, starting at the edges. When the seeds are good, they should not be sown too thickly, in order to produce vigorous plants; and if, despite this precaution, they are too thick, they should be thinned out by hand.

mix with dry sand or fine tere. After sowing, the soil should be harrowed lightly with a fork, then trodden a little, although this should not be done if the soil is damp. To cover the seeds, use the back of the rake to spread the soil around the edges of the bed, leaving a little to retain water from watering. You can also spread a little well-consumed manure over the seedlings. If the weather is dry, germination should be encouraged by watering the seeds with an apple watering can. 2. Sowing in rows or rays. - Using either a hoe or a tracing knife, draw rays about Om, 3, or Om 5 deep, and more or less distant from each other, depending on what you want to sow; after scattering the seed, cover it lightly, raking up some of the soil from the sides with the back of the rake. When the seedling is out of the ground, we finish filling the rays by passing the rake or hoe between each row. This method of sowing is highly advantageous, especially in areas where frequent hoeing is required. 3. Sowing in pockets. - This consists in making holes with the hoe, arranged in a chessboard pattern, the distance and depth of which will be calculated according to the development that each clump should take; then, after placing a few seeds in each hole, we cover them by folding back the soil a little, and, when the plants are high enough, we finish filling the holes by passing a stroke of rake between each clump. SII. - Transplanting. Transplanting is necessary for all plants which cannot be sown in situ; and, to be sure of the success of the operation, you must not wait until the plant is too old, because not only is recovery more difficult, but the products are less beautiful; for plants which take root slowly, you must, before putting them in situ, transplant them in a nursery, i.e. put them in a good position and very close to each other. Successive transplanting has the advantage of producing a large quantity of shoots, which will ensure that the plants take root again when they are finally planted. Transplanting should only take place in well-prepared soil, on which a short mulch of manure has been spread, to ensure that the plant benefits as long as possible from watering, and that watering does not cause the plant to stick to the soil, which often leads to leaf rot. Transplanting in summer should, as far as possible, be carried out when the weather is overcast; and, if the weather is not favorable, the operation should be carried out towards the end of the day, and the recovery facilitated by watering. When you have a lot of plants to transplant and the weather is very dry, don't wait until you've finished before starting to water. SIII. - Onions. The only care to be taken to obtain success with bulbous plants you wish to propagate is to choose them in good health and plant them in circumstances most favorable to their growth. § IV. - Bulbs. This is the name given to the small bulbs or onions which form around the crown of bulbous plants such as Tulips, Hyacinths, etc., and which serve to multiply them. Caiuses should only be removed when they are ripe, which is when the leaves are completely dry. Cloves should always be planted in soft soil, at least one month before flowering onions, as they dry out more quickly due to their small volume. These onions usually wither after three or four years. § V. - Bulbils. Several bulbous plants produce on their stems, often in place of seeds, small onions called bulbils, which serve to multiply them; they should be removed when ripe, and treated like bulblets. §VI. - Tubers. Tubers are fleshy masses of underground stems, from which small fibrous roots usually grow. Some plants, such as Potatoes, Potatoes, etc., have eyes capable of providing new stems; and, to multiply them, they can be cut into as many pieces as there are eyes: each section will produce a new plant. Others have eyes on only part of the plant: these are Dahlias, Iris Germanica, herbaceous Peonies, and in such cases, when dividing them, care must be taken to leave each part of the collar of the plant; otherwise they would not grow. § VIF. - Claws or Paws. These are the names given to the roots of Ranunculus, Anemones, etc.; they are separated by splinters, but in such a way that there is always an eye for each. SVIII. - Eyelets. This is the name given to the offshoots that grow around certain plants (artichokes, etc.); they are separated from the old plants, taking care to remove them as far as possible with a heel; they should not be left to wither, so that they will grow again more quickly. S IX. - Root separation. Some perennial plants, such as Peonies, have roots that start from a common collar, and have one or more eyes that develop the following year. To multiply them, they can be split into as many parts as there are eyes. Other plants, such as Chrysanthemums, have their roots almost at the surface of the soil, and form thick clumps which can be divided into small parts; they must then be lifted from the ground, and, after separating them, only the circumference is replanted, which will produce much more beautiful clumps than if the center were replanted, which, being the oldest part of the plant, is naturally the least vigorous. SX. - Stolons or Flowers. Some plants, such as Strawberries, have runners, which produce offshoots at each node, rooting into the soil. Separated and transplanted in a favorable season, they produce as many new plants. SXI. - Marcottes. Marcottes are branches that are laid down in spring, either in the ground or in pots, and only separated from the mother branch when they have produced roots. When the branches you wish to propagate are placed in such a way that they cannot be lowered to the ground, you need pots or cups with slits in the sides, which you hold on a small board nailed to a support, the end of which you push into the ground. There are several ways of marcotting: we will only mention the most commonly used; but, whatever the method used, the soil in which the marcottes are placed must be constantly moist, to encourage the roots to emerge; and, to preserve the moisture of the watering, it is a good idea to cover the soil with consumed manure or moss. 1. simple Marcottes. - These are used to propagate plants that root easily, such as Vines, etc. The whole operation consists in laying a branch down in a trench of varying depth, depending on its size. After removing any leaves or buds on the part to be planted, the end is carefully bent to avoid breaking it. If you don't need to bury them deeply, you can secure them in the ground with a wooden hook. 2. Marcottes by strangulation. - These differ from the previous ones in that the bark is tightened, without cutting it, with a wire on the part which is in the ground; the result is a bead from which new roots sprout. 3. Marcottes by incision. - We'll describe this poeration as it's used to propagate carnations. In July, stop watering for a while before layering, to make the branches more flexible, and choose stems long enough to be laid down. Cut off the lower leaves, so that the part in the ground is free of them; then lower each stem into a small trench made with a finger, and straighten the end of the branch above the bend. Using the blade of the grafting machine, make an incision about Om, 02 in length, so that the notched part forms a tongue, the end of which is cut cleanly below a node, taking care not to cut into the other half of the stem. Each marcotte is held in place by a hook or a piece of wicker passed underneath, the two ends of which are pressed into the earth; then the whole is covered with earth fine enough to penetrate everywhere, taking care to let a little of it penetrate between the separate parts, which usually remain apart due to the effect of the curvature. Once the operation is complete, we usually cut off the tips of the leaves to prevent them from withering; then we spread a light mulch of half-consumed manure and wet with a watering can with very fine holes, so as not to shake the marcottes, which usually take root after a short time. 4. Sprouting by cutting. - This method involves cutting a tree or shrub at ground level in spring and covering the stump with soil. It soon produces suckers, which are removed once they have taken root. This is how the coignassier is propagated, to provide subjects for grafting. 5. Root marcottes. - To make this type of marcotte, cut the end of a root and leave the wound open to the air; the sap forms a bead from which buds soon develop; from among these, choose the most vigorous, and remove the others; then, in autumn, weed it by cutting the root close to the stump. S XII. - Cuttings, Almost all serous plants can be propagated by cuttings. This operation, which is extremely simple, consists in cutting off any part of a plant, even a leaf for some species, and making it produce roots. Some plants are very easy to propagate, but others require a great deal of care, and can only be propagated by horticultural merchants, who have special tarpaulins for this operation. We will therefore limit ourselves to indicating which cuttings can be taken in the open air, and which need to be smothered, but which are very successful if you only have cloches and a frame. 1. Open air cuttings. - This is the way to propagate many ornamental trees and shrubs. In January, cut the year's twigs into trunks of Om, 10 to Om, 20 in length, depending on the species; cut the lower part cleanly below one eye; group them by species and bury them at half their length in sand or fine earth, but in a place sheltered from sun and frost; from the end of February to the beginning of April, they are planted in well-prepared soil and, as far as possible, in a shady spot; they are planted in such a way as to leave two or three eyes above ground; after planting, the soil is mulched, and when dryness begins to make itself felt, the humidity of the soil is maintained by watering. 2. Cuttings under cloches and frames. - Many orangery and temperate greenhouse plants can be propagated from cuttings in spring on a warm bed; this is done in February and March. For this purpose, prepare a thin layer, so as to obtain only gentle heat; surround it with a stove, and cover it with a bed of fine potting soil, to which a little heather may be added; then place cloches over it, or cover it with a frame; but in this case, the height of the layer should be calculated so that the cuttings are not far from the glass. When the layer has warmed up, cut the cuttings with or without heel on the most vigorous branches; top them by giving them Om, 08 to Om, 10 in length, always taking care to cut the lower part cleanly below one eye; then transplant them immediately onto the layer using a small planter, pushing them in from Om, 02 to Om, 03. These cuttings can also be transplanted into pots which are then pressed into the bed: this is how Heliotropes, Petunias, Verbena, etc. can be propagated. After covering the borders with clochies or frames, shade them when the sun is shining, and cover them at night with straw mats. They should be watered from time to time with a small watering can, as the cuttings can only take root if the soil is kept constantly fresh; when they start to grow, and you're sure they have roots, give them a little air during the day, by lifting the cloches or frames, and after a while pinch off the longest ends, then lift the cuttings, trying to keep each one a small root ball. They are then planted in pots, which can be replaced on the same layer after watering, and, if deemed necessary, the warmth of the layer is rekindled by making new warmers; other 4. care is limited to gradually airing them and watering them as needed. All plants treated in this way will be strong and hardy enough to be put in the ground at the same time as they are planted in beds and flowerbeds. The same operation can be carried out in summer, in a shady spot; only, at this time, there is no need for a bed: this is how Pelargoniums, etc., are propagated. The best time to take these cuttings is from the end of July to the end of August. After preparing them as described above, transplant them at Om, 03 or Om, 04 apart into pots filled with soil and heather mixed with a little potting compost, and after transplanting, water them lightly; then place the pots under a bell or under a frame, but away from the sun. In autumn, the cuttings will have taken root, and can be separated, which is done by dividing the pot into as many parts as there are cuttings; then they are potted separately; having been treated in this way, you are sure to have plants with the strength to flower the following spring. The following procedure can also be used for plants that are difficult to root: take an ordinary pot, then another pot, narrower, but as high as possible; invert it into this one, fill the space with soil appropriate to the needs of the cuttings you intend to make, put a small pinch of soil on the hole, then transplant the cuttings; finally, bury the whole on a layer, and place a bell on top, 3. Cuttings by root sections. - Some plants can be propagated by cutting a root into sections, which are planted either in the ground or on a bed, but leaving the end exposed to the air, from which buds soon emerge. CHAPTER IX. Grafting. We will not describe grafting at length, as this operation is too widely known for that; we will only say that its purpose is to multiply, conserve and perfect useful and pleasant varieties, and to make a wild trunk bear brilliant flowers or tasty fruits destined to embellish our gardens and increase the no less appreciable products of our orchards. This operation, which has been written about so many times and at such length, simply requires a little observation and a certain manual skill. It is based on three fundamental points: 1° an appreciation of the circumstances in which the grafting is to be carried out, i.e. the moment when the plants, watered by sap, are ready to vegetate; 2° the choice of the subject, which must be in a suitable state of vigour and health, and above all suitable for grafting, which can only be carried out on species united by close affinities, 3° the manual operation, which requires only a short apprenticeship and can be considered the least difficult of the three, since by observing the two preceding conditions, success can be achieved which cannot be attained, no matter how careful the grafter, if the circumstances in which he operates are unfavorable. There are various types of grafting, but most are purely for pleasure. We shall therefore limit ourselves to describing the main ones, which can be considered as the type of all the others that can be performed once you are familiar with those we have indicated. 1. escutcheon graft. This is the most commonly used graft, and can be carried out at several times of the year: firstly, from May to July, which is known as budding grafting; this name comes from the fact that these grafts start to grow as soon as the escutcheon is taken up; there are even some, on Roses for example, which in autumn of the same year already form a beautiful head. The second period is from August to September, when the sap begins to slow down, and is known as "dormant eye" grafting, because at this time the escutcheon only becomes attached to the subject, and doesn't grow until the following year. Fruit trees are best grafted at this time of year. Some time before the operation, you need to prepare the tree to receive the graft, i.e. select the branches on which you want to graft, and remove the others, especially those below the grafts. If the trees you want to graft start to lose their sap, you need to water them to revive their vegetation. When the time is right, select the best eyes of the species you wish to propagate, cut off the leaf above the eye without damaging the petiole, and remove any prickles from the bud. Then, using the blade of the grafting machine, surround the eye so that you can remove it with part of the surrounding bark, which will be shaped roughly like the bud (see A, fig. 3). To detach it, lift it slightly with the tip of the grafting tool, then with the spatula, taking care to remove all the woody parts adhering to the escutcheon, which would prevent it coming into contact with the wood of the subject, unless the branch is tender enough for this operation not to be necessary; And if the root of the eye were to be removed, as is easy to see from the resulting gap, the crest would have to be reformed, and its recovery would be doubtful. Another method can also be used to lift the escutcheon, and is particularly advantageous when the grafts are small. Fig. 3. - Escutcheon graft. consists in detaching the escutcheon with a silk thread or horsehair (see B, fig. 3), after lifting the upper part. 3), lift the edges of the wound by sliding the spatula under the bark, so as to easily place the escutcheon, which is inserted by holding it by the petiole and pressing lightly on the upper part; If it is not possible to insert the entire length of the escutcheon, its end must be cut off to ensure that it coincides with the subject; the edges of the notch are then brought together on the escutcheon, and the whole is wrapped with a wool ligature, taking particular care not to engage the eye (see D, fig. 3). The ligature is shown further apart than it should be, so that the position of the escutcheon can be seen. Early petiole fall is a sign of graft recovery, which usually takes place ten or fifteen days after the operation; the tree should then be cut back to a few centimetres above the graft. Be sure to remove any shoots that appear on the subject, and pinch the terminal bud of the grafts so as to encourage the development of the lower eyes. 2. Ring grafting. - The months of April and August are the best times for this type of grafting. Fig. 4 - Ring grafting. Suitable for hardwood trees, particularly walnut. A branch of the same size as the tree to be grafted is selected from the tree to be propagated; the bark is encircled circularly below and above an eye, so as to form a ring which is detached by splitting it perpendicularly on the part adjacent to the eye (see C. fig. 4); it is then removed using the spatula of the grafting machine. A ring of the same width is then removed from the subject (see B, fig. 4), and the section of bark removed is returned to its place on the tree to be propagated. To be sure of success, the bark must be joined at the top and bottom; then the grafts are secured with a wool ligature, taking particular care not to engage the eye. Do not cut back the branches or the head of the tree until the graft's recovery is assured. This grafting technique has the advantage of never mutilating the tree, because if the graft does not vegetate, the ring of bark remains and takes the place of the one that was removed. 3. Split grafting. - This graft can be done in spring or autumn; and, to be sure of success, there must be enough sap to make the graft grow, but still enough to weld it to the subject, so that it doesn't dry out in the winter. To graft in the spring, you need to take the precaution of cutting off the previous year's shoots from each species of tree you wish to propagate, and then bury them in sand in a northern exposure, so as to delay vegetation as long as possible. To be sure of the success of these grafts, the sap must have begun to rise in the subject, but not yet have swollen the buds of the shoot you wish to graft. The first fortnight of April is usually the most favourable time for this operation: we then cut the head of the tree horizontally, and split it in the middle of its diameter, so as to make a notch of Om,03 to Om,06, depending on the strength of the tree, and taking care that this notch is always a little deeper and wider than the graft to be inserted would appear to require. When the subject is large and vigorous, several notches can be made (see A, B, fig. 5); but they must be opposite each other, so that they do not meet. Once the tree is ready to be grafted, select a branch with good eyes, from Om, 06 to Om, 10 in length, so that after insertion in the notch, there are at least two or three eyes on the outside. The lower part of this branch is then pruned to form two bevels of Om, 03 to Om, 06 in length (see C, fig. 5), whatever the thickness of the outer part, care is taken to preserve its bark; then open the slit with the spatula of the grafting machine or with a wedge, and insert the graft so that its bark coincides exactly with that of the subject; then wrap the whole with a 14 M Fig. 5. - Grafting in slit. ligature, and cover the end of the subject with grafting wax (1). Finally, take care to remove any shoots that develop on the subject, as they will live at the expense of the graft. 4 - Slit graft on tuber. - This graft is particularly useful for multiplying tree peonies. In August, take a tuber of herbaceous Peony, cut the top transversely, make a slit on one side and insert a branch with the lower part bevelled, then plant the tuber in a pot, but in such a way that the whole graft is buried. The pots (1) The grafting wax is composed of two parts of pitch-resin, two parts of yellow wax and one part of tallow, melted together. are placed on a warm layer, and the grafts are covered with a bell which must be shaded for fifteen or twenty days. The following spring, each of these grafts can be planted in the ground. 5. Veneer grafting. - The branch to be grafted is pruned into an elongated flute-beak shape (see A, fig. 6), then a portion of bark is removed (see B, fig. 6) Fig. 6. - Veneer graft. exactly the same size as the pruned part of the graft; the two parts are joined together, and a ligature made. These grafts grow back easily, but for this to happen, they must be smothered under a bell. 6. Vine grafting. - In March, cut the subject at the root collar, at about Om, 08 to Om, 10 in the ground, and leave the wound to dry for a few days; if you grafted immediately, the sap would rise so abundantly that it could drown the graft. The branches are prepared as for other split grafts, then one or more notches are made, depending on the strength of the subject, and the grafts are placed, leaving two or three eyes above ground. The graft and the tip are then covered with grafting wax; some people even compress the soil around the graft, taking great care not to disturb the branches. In the south of France, modified split grafting is used in the following way: after removing the heel of the stock used as a subject, it is cut a little above the ground, split right through, and a knife-edge graft is inserted laterally about halfway along its length, with the lower end plunging into the ground at Om, 15 to Om, 20; this serves to feed the graft until it is perfectly fused with the subject. Herbaceous graft. - This graft differs from the split graft only in the time at which it is performed. It can be used to propagate almost all plants still in the herbaceous state, particularly resinous trees and some ornamental shrubs. The time for grafting varies according to the state of the season; usually, the best time is May. The tip of the bud is cut off above one or more leaves, which must be carefully spared to attract the sap to the graft. The tree is then split from about Om, 03 to Om, 06 in length, and the shoot is prepared as for split grafting, taking care not to thin it too much. For this operation, a sharp, well-sharpened instrument must be used, in order to make a clean cut. Once the graft has been prepared, it is inserted into the subject's cleft, and secured with a wool ligature. To avoid cutting the head of the tree, we can proceed in another way: we make an incision, as if to place a crest; then, after having cut the branch into a bevel elongated on one side, like a flute's beak, we introduce it between the wood and the bark, and as always, we maintain the graft with a ligature, which must be removed about a month after the operation. To ensure that the grafts take root again, they must be protected from the sun and the sun's rays; so, if you're working on potted plants, you'll need to put them under a frame, which you'll need to shade; and if you're working on plants in the ground, you'll need to protect them by wrapping them in a paper cone, a vine leaf, or some other shelter, which you can remove ten or fifteen days after the operation. Grafting can also be used to propagate tuberous plants: in spring, young shoots of the best Dahlia varieties are grafted onto tubers of inferior varieties. To do this, take a tuber, cut off the top horizontally and split it on one side, then choose a branch that is not yet hollow, which happens when they are already strong, and cut the lower part with a slightly sharp bevel, taking care to remove only the epidermis; then insert it into the slit in the tuber, which is planted in a pot, so that the whole graft is buried. The pots are placed on a warm surface and covered with a cloche, which must be shaded for several days. Herbaceous grafting is also performed on grapevines. This operation should be carried out in May or June, on buds of Om, 20 or Om, 25 in length. It differs in no way from ordinary slit grafting; the only difference is that, after wrapping the graft in wool or wax, it is placed in a wide-necked bottle (canning jar), attached to a stake or other support, and corked with fresh moss. After twelve or fifteen days, when recovery is certain, the bottle is uncorked to fortify the graft, and soon afterwards left to air. These grafts sometimes bear fruit in the very first year, which means that this process can be used with advantage, not only as a means of propagation, but also to judge the merit of new varieties. Crown grafting, known as Pliny grafting. - This graft is used when the subject is too strong to be split grafted; it must be done at the same time as the latter graft, and care must also be taken to cut off, during the winter, some of the raE Fig. 7. - Crown graft. meaux of the subject to be propagated, to prevent them from coming into vegetation too early. The head of the tree to be grafted should be cut horizontally (see fig. 7), and the tip should be wrapped with a ligature to hold the bark in place, lest it split when the cuts are made; a small wedge of iron or hardwood is then driven to a depth of about Om, 05 between the bark and the wood, then the shoot is bevelled, and after removing the wedge, the graft is driven in so that the entire bevel is hidden (see fig. 7). The number of grafts placed on the same subject should be proportionate to its size. They should be placed about Om, 05 apart; and, as soon as the operation is complete, the tip of the subject and the edges of the bark should be covered with grafting wax. 7. Ordinary approach grafting. - This operation consists in applying a branch of the variety to be grafted against a branch or stem of a tree of the same species; it can be performed while the trees are in vegetation. The procedure should be as follows: after bringing the two branches together in parallel (see fig. 8), remove part of the bark on each one, so as to form a longitudinal wound, the length of which should always be proportionate to the strength of the individuals; then apply them one Fig. 8 - Grafting by approach. It is often necessary to loosen the ligature as soon as the grafts have been taken up, so that the force of the vegetation does not cause strangulation, which not only forms bulges, but also hinders the taking up of the grafts. The grafts should not be detached until it is certain that they are solidly welded; in fact, it is often more prudent to start by cutting off the head of the subject, only half-cutting the part that is to be cut off, and not weaning it completely until some time later, when it should be cut as close as possible to the graft, so that the sap covers the wound more easily. This operation requires great care, so as not to cut the subject with the grafting blade. 8. Grafting by complicated approach. - This graft differs little from the previous one: it is specially used to give solidity to hedges. The branches are crossed so as to form a rhombus; at the point where they meet, a longitudinal wound is made on each branch, taking care to ensure that the bark coincides; the two parts are held together by means of a ligature, and the operation is repeated as the branches grow. CHAPTER X.. Plant Conservation. In this chapter, we will limit ourselves to saying that the northern situation of our country, the irregularity of the seasons, and the humidity of our springs and autumns would make the cultivation of certain exotic plants impracticable, if we did not have recourse to artificial means of conservation and multiplication. These means are of several kinds: starting with the simplest, they include the most complex: I. cloches; 2. frames; 3. the orangery or cold greenhouse; 4. the temperate greenhouse; 5. the hot greenhouse. 1. cloches. - Glass cloches are the simplest of all shelters; they serve to protect delicate plants and cuttings from cold and humidity, and to concentrate heat on those that need a higher temperature than that of the atmosphere. Choose cloches with the whitest glass, as they are prone to tarnishing after a few years. Take the precaution of washing them from time to time, and when they are no longer in use, place them one inside the other, taking care to separate them with a bit of straw to avoid breakage. 2. Chassis. - The purpose of frames is to activate the germination of certain seeds, to increase the heat of the layers, to enable the cultivation of vegetable plants that do not succeed in the open air, and to protect delicate plants from the insults of the air. They [consist of two parts: the trunk a, a (fig. 9), and the panels c. Each trunk is usually 4 meters long and Im, 33 wide; it is made of four boards nailed to four feet placed internally at the four corners. The back of the trunk should always be higher than the front, so that the panels are inclined to the south. The spacing is maintained by two bars b. b, dovetailed at the top and bottom, which support the panels. The glazed panels should be made of thick oak. They consist of a frame 1.33 m wide and of arbitrary length, divided by three small bars of equal thickness, which can be advantageously replaced by iron uprights, fixed to the crosspieces from top to bottom. A handle is placed at each end, so that they can be removed, and before glazing, they are painted with oil, an operation that should be repeated every year in autumn. Doormats. - Doormats are used to cover diapers, bells, frames, greenhouses and so on. With the gardeners' doormat loom, you can easily make your own doormats. This loom consists of a wooden frame 2 meters long by Im, 33 wide, with as many headless pegs at either end as you want to stretch strings through it, depending on the length you give the doormat. It is customary to make only three rows; however, it would be better to make four, for greater solidity. The strings are tied to the bottom pegs with a fixed loop, and to the top with a slipknot, allowing them to be stretched as much as necessary. Once each string has been tightened, it is left twice the length of the frame; this excess length is used to sew the mat, after which two layers of rye straw are laid across it, as evenly as possible, and laid head to tail; and, after rolling the middle row of string onto a kind of shuttle made from a piece of wood Om, 08 in length and hollowed out on the sides, we take a pinch of straw, and pass the shuttle from right to left under the straw and over the string, then return to the top to engage it in the loop formed by the string, and tighten by pulling straight ahead, taking care to press the straw between the thumb and forefinger of the left hand, so as to obtain a flat rather than round mesh; then continue with the same shuttle along the length of the mat, and when you've reached the end, tie a knot to stop the string. You then move on to the other rows, which you sew in the same way, guiding the edge stitches onto the middle ones; and, once the doormat is finished, you cut off the overhanging ears on either side. Although these doormats are intended to cover panels Im, 33 wide, they should be given a length of 2 meters, because when damp they shorten by about Om, 50, leaving them only as long as required. 3. Orangery. - The orangery, or cold greenhouse, is designed to keep out the cold for certain plants that require only a small amount of heat. Its shape is a long square, and its dimensions, both in height and width, must be in proportion to the number of plants it is designed to contain. The walls must be thick enough to prevent frost from easily penetrating them. The front will be fitted with windows as large as possible, and the entrance door, placed in the center, will be glazed and open in two parts. A stove will be built, with pipes running around the interior walls, but fire will only be needed in the event of extraordinary feoids. To preserve the health of the plants, it is sufficient to prevent frost from penetrating into the greenhouse; to this end, a thermometer should be placed in the greenhouse, to be consulted often, in order to maintain the necessary temperature for each plant. Water intended for watering orangery plants, and even those in adjoining greenhouses, should enter the building through underground pipes, and be collected in a basin or barrel to warm up a little. 5. §I. - On the entry of orangery plants and their treatment in winter. Plants should be brought into the orangery in the second half of October, rarely later. They should only be brought in when the weather is dry, and care should be taken to place the highest plants at the back, so that they form a tier, and all get as much light as possible. In addition to orange trees, laurel, pomegranate and many other hardy plants can spend the winter in the orangery; they can even be placed behind or between the orange trees, without any inconvenience; but this is not the case for myrtles, which can also be placed in the orangery; they must receive direct light, otherwise they will lose their leaves. Large Pelargoniums zonale (Red Geraniums) can be placed on the shelves: their hardiness is such that they are perfectly happy in the orangery; they should be watered very little (but not allowed to dry out), to prevent them from vegetating during their stay in the greenhouse, when the shoots are so tender that they have to be cut back as they come out. During the frosts, you can still place potted carnations and large wallflowers in the orangery, but you need to put them outside as soon as the temperature permits. At first, leave the orangery fully open day and night. When it starts to get cold, close it at night; then, when it's freezing, close it during the day. Then all the windows must be hermetically sealed and fitted with mats on the outside. Whenever the thermometer outside registers 3 or 4 degrees above zero, air is given in, unless the atmosphere is too humid or the wind too violent. Plants in the orangery should be watered only when they need it, and only as much water as is absolutely necessary for their maintenance. As winter is a time of rest for plants, it is important to avoid reviving vegetation at this time, which would exhaust them. §1I. - The plants can only be brought out in the first fortnight of May, and we always start with the hardiest; but to accustom them to atmospheric influences, we must give them as much air as possible a long time in advance, and wait for overcast or rainy weather before bringing them out. All plants should be placed (as is almost always the case) close to the house, but always with a good exposure and sheltered from the wind; double-flowered oleanders and pomegranates should be placed in the extreme south, if you want them to flower every year. As soon as they have been removed, any plants needing to be re-boxed, either because they require more space or because the boxes need replacing, should be re-boxed only after they have been placed in their proper positions, to prevent them from being shaken in transit. In all circumstances, we advise you to give larger crates only gradually, and with a great deal of reserve; because if they are too large, Laurel and Pomegranate trees grow a lot, but do not flower, and Orange trees languish. Up to the age of eight or ten, Orange trees should be pruned about every two or three years, and then every five or six years; but Laurel and Pomegranate trees should be pruned more frequently, as the development of branches and twigs is always related to that of the roots, and as these shrubs vegetate much more vigorously than Orange trees, they should be pruned more often. If, while waiting for the time for re-embedding, the leaves of the shrubs turn yellow, without this being due to excessive humidity, they should be given a half-embedding, which consists in cutting away Om, 05 to Om, 10 of the soil around the box and replacing it with new soil suited to the plant's needs. At the end of this chapter, we will indicate which soil is suitable for each plant. Since potting differs little from repotting, except in the way it is carried out, which must be modified, we refer you to this article for the details. After the operation, we cover the surface of the soil with a mulch of consumed manure, and give each plant a good watering. As with winter watering, the water used should be left in a barrel for some time; it would even be a good idea to water from time to time with water in which animal or vegetable substances have been left to decompose. Watering should take place at least once a day in summer. Finally, they should be more or less abundant, depending on the temperature; then gradually decrease as the temperature cools. Towards the end of August or the beginning of September, orange trees need to be pruned, which involves removing dead wood and all unnecessary or misplaced branches, such as those on the inside, which make the head too compact and hinder the circulation of sap. Finally, whether you're growing Orange trees in a rounded or cylindrical shape, you need to cut off the tips of all slender branches, so as to give each tree a regular shape; it's also at this time that you can reduce the head of those that are growing too much, or which, no longer growing, need to be rejuvenated, which is done by cutting back all branches more or less close to the trunk, depending on the tree's strength. Laurel trees can be subjected to the same treatment when they overgrow; but they must not be pruned annually, as this would deprive them of flowers. As soon as they are out of bloom, cut off the ends of the branches that used to bear the flowers, so as to obtain trees with well rounded heads. Myrtles need to be sheared regularly, as soon as they are defoliated; Grenadiers also need to be sheared every year, to keep their shape as graceful as possible; but this pruning should only take place at the time of re-entry. To complete our information on orangery plants as much as possible, we'd like to point out that Pelargoniums zonale should also be trimmed before being placed in the greenhouse. §111 - Soil composition for the following plants. ORANGERS. - One quarter free soil, one quarter good vegetable garden soil, one quarter heather soil, one quarter fat compost. MYRTS. - Pure heather soil. GRENADIERS and ROSE LAUREL. - Good vegetable garden soil mixed with fatty loam. 4. Temperate greenhouse. - This greenhouse differs from the orangery in that it is much brighter, an indispensable condition for the preservation of the plants we recommend placing there; it will be adjoining the orangery, and will communicate from one to the other: it will be 8 meters long by 3 wide, and, starting from the interior floor, it will be 2m, 45 high at the back; the front will be Om, 80 high and will be glazed. The small sashes that will close it will be hinged at the top, and will open horizontally from bottom to top; they will bear from below on a small support wall covered with a slab, and they will beat on the uprights that support the lower part of the rafters, which must be, like the rest, made of oak and placed at Im, 33 one from the other, so as to receive the glazed panels with which the greenhouse is to be covered. The first row of panels will be 2 m long by Im, 33 wide; they will bear from the bottom on a gutter board designed to discharge rainwater, and from the top on a crosspiece called a spacer, which must run from one rafter to the other. The panels of the second row will be only Im, 36 in length, and so that they can bear on the rafters, it is necessary to apply a sole on each of them, so as to form the thickness of the bottom panels, on which those of the second row will have to bear about Om, 03, and from the top on a crosspiece which, like that of the bottom, must go from one rafter to the other. The panels will be fixed at the top by hooks placed inside, and to give air, the bottom will be raised and held open by small iron racks. At the top of the greenhouse, a small roof will be placed over which you can walk to service the doormats. To prevent you from slipping on the panels, place a handrail along the entire length of the greenhouse. Inside, a pathway Om, 75 wide, supported by a supporting wall, will be provided at orangery floor level, as the rest of the greenhouse must be Om, 50 lower. The middle will be occupied by a tier Im, 25 wide, made up of six shelves; the height of the tier must be calculated so that the plants are no more than Om, 60 or Om, 80 from the glass; in front of the tier we'll make a path Om, 50 wide, so that we can circulate all around it, then we'll, establish a shelf against the back wall, and another path Om, 50 wide on the front of the greenhouse and under which will circulate the pipes of the stove, whose mouth must always be outside. An opening will be made in the gable of this greenhouse, and it will be fitted with a double door, which will serve as an entrance during frosts, thus avoiding having to open that of the orangery. §IV. - Temperate greenhouse plants and their winter treatment. The plants must be brought in during October, but it is impossible for us to determine the exact time; we will only say that it is important to avoid as much as possible that they remain exposed to the autumn humidity, and especially that they are not affected by the first frosts. By the beginning of the month, the panels should be ready to be placed on the greenhouse; the interior should be cleaned and any repairs made; finally, by this time, it should be ready to receive the plants, which should be placed in the following order, although this should only be done after cleaning the pots and lightly scraping the surface of the soil, so as not to leave any grass or moss. Pelargoniums, Calceolaria, Cinerea and Verbena are placed on the tier, while Hydrangea, Erythrina, Balisier or Dahlia tubers can be placed underneath. Succulents or plants requiring little care during winter are placed on the shelf against the back wall, but the front shelf is reserved for Camellias, which should always be placed in the brightest part of the greenhouse. All these plants should be placed on the shelves, in rows of the same size, spaced so that their heads do not touch each other; and during their stay in the greenhouse, they should be turned over from time to time, so that all their parts are successively exposed to the light; for without this precaution, they would all be inclined to the same side, leaving them with an unsightly shape. From the moment the plants are placed in the greenhouse until spring, watering should be moderate and only be carried out as and when the plants really need it. Watering should be done with a small watering can, to which an extension spout should be added to reach distant plants, and the water used should have been kept at greenhouse temperature for some time. Other care consists in keeping the house clean and renewing the air as often as possible, while avoiding opening the frames on overcast or rainy days, so as not to introduce humidity into the greenhouse; Then, as soon as the cold approaches, all openings should be hermetically sealed with moss, and when the weather clears in the evening and the outside thermometer registers only 3 or 4 degrees of heat, the greenhouse should be covered with mats, as it is likely to freeze overnight. In December, the small frames at the front of the greenhouse should be fitted with a stove made of dry manure; and, whatever the temperature, it is advisable to cover the greenhouse every night, taking care to remove the mats during the day, unless the weather is overcast and the cold is severe. Moreover, you can uncover the greenhouse without any inconvenience whenever the thermometer shows no more than 4 or 5 degrees of cold; however, you must be careful to replace the doormats before frost has formed on the windows; and if at that time you have to let in some air, you must always close the door before the sun disappears, in order to concentrate the heat in the greenhouse, which can often save you the trouble of making a fire at night; finally, either by doubling the mats, or by making a small fire (which should only be done with great reserve), care should be taken to ensure that the temperature of the greenhouse does not fall below 5 degrees of heat, and if it is necessary to make a fire, it should not be raised to more than 6 to 8 degrees, as the essential point is to maintain the plants in a state of rest from which they should only emerge towards the end of winter. As almost all the plants we have mentioned are prone to aphid attack, tobacco fumigation should be used as often as necessary, after closing everything (1). Once March arrives, there's no longer any need to light a fire in the greenhouse, as the sun usually heats the atmosphere sufficiently. Often, when the sun shines directly on the greenhouse, it's even necessary to spread a light-colored cloth over the panels, to prevent (1) This operation must be carried out using a cylindrical copper device called a fumigator. It consists of two parts: the upper part rubs against the lower part; a plate with fine holes is fixed internally to receive the tobacco and prevent it from passing through the pipes. To operate the unit, the end of a bellows is inserted into the pipe at the bottom of the unit, and as it blows, the smoke escapes through the tube attached to one side of the upper part. This prevents the foliage of the plants from being burnt. From this point onwards, watering should gradually become more frequent and more abundant; it is even necessary to syringe the plants from time to time, an operation which at this time should take place in the morning. But however beneficial these waterings may be, they should be suspended as soon as the first Pelargonium flowers bloom, as they will quickly dull their radiance. In the first days of April, gradually introduce more air into the greenhouse, depending on the temperature, to fortify the plants, which will soon be exposed to the open air. If you want Pelargoniums to bloom brilliantly, take them out of the greenhouse as soon as the temperature permits, and place them in a good position, taking care to arrange them so that they can be easily covered at night, should the temperature so require; then leave them there until the first flowers begin to bloom, at which point they can be returned to the greenhouse: In this way, the plants will be less slender, more robust, and the flowers more vividly colored. If some circumstance prevents the Pelargoniums from being put out as soon as indicated, air should be supplied through all the openings in the greenhouse to remedy the situation as far as possible. § V. - Removal of temperate greenhouse plants and their treatment in summer. In the first fortnight of May, and as much as possible on an overcast day, take the plants out of the greenhouse, except for Pelargoniums and Calceolaria, which should be taken out only after they have deflowered, in order to enjoy all the beauty of their flowering; and then treat them as we shall indicate when talking about the plants to be taken out. Place them in a shady spot for a few days, to strengthen them; and before putting them in place, repot those that need it, which must be done every year for those that grow a lot. But not all plants can be repotted at the same time; for this operation to be carried out properly, it must always take place some time before the plants come into vegetation, and it is wrong that many gardeners still indiscriminately repot all plants in autumn. It's easy to understand why we find this practice so reprehensible: repotting can hardly take place without damaging the roots; it often happens that, once the hair has completely covered the root ball, it becomes necessary to cut it back; it's certain then that this operation can be useless, if not harmful, when it takes place at a time when the plants are due to rest for several months. It is therefore preferable to repot plants in spring. However, plants like Pelargoniums, which vegetate towards the end of winter, should be repotted towards the end of August or the beginning of September, in a word, in time for them to put down new roots before winter. Now that we've come to the subject of Pelargoniums, we'd like to point out that they should always be pruned a fortnight before repotting; this operation consists in removing any thin or badly placed branches, and cutting back the current year's branches two or three eyes below their insertion, depending on their position and the vigour of the plants, but always so as to form a well-rounded head. Immediately after potting, details of which will be given in the next chapter, water the plants with the apple watering can, then place them in pruning rows in a well-ventilated spot, but in part-shade as far as possible; and, if there is no natural shelter, form palisades from small reeds attached at the top and bottom to gaulettes held in place by stakes; keep watering as required; you can even continue syringing, which, during the hot days of June, July and August, should only take place towards the end of the day. If heavy rainfall occurs shortly after potting, the pots should be temporarily laid to one side, to prevent excessive humidity from rotting the roots. Although we have given a general description of the care to be given to temperate greenhouse plants, it can be applied to all pot-grown plants, unless the plants are planted in the ground with their pots, which can only be done for Verbenas, Petunias, Hydrangeas, Pelargoniums and some varieties of Calceolaria, all plants with which you can form very graceful groups. There's no need to protect them from the sun's rays; they just need to be cut back and repotted in time for them to have recovered by the time they return to the greenhouse. SVI. - Repotting. Before repotting, the soil should have been prepared for each plant, as described at the end of this chapter. When everything is ready, take advantage of overcast weather as much as possible, or, failing that, move to a shady spot. We then take each plant in turn, carefully unstacking it by placing the left hand on the surface of the soil, so that the stem passes between the fingers, then we turn the plant upside down, and, supporting the pot with the right hand, we lightly tap its rim on a point of support, and once the rootball is out of the pot, we visit it. If, as is often the case, the lining of the rootball is made up of a tissue of dried-out roots, it is cut away cleanly, then, by scraping lightly, a greater or lesser part of the old soil is removed, depending on the degree of decomposition; broken or rotten roots are then removed. After preparing the clod in this way, if it happens to be very dry, we plunge it into water until it is well soaked. After draining, place it in the pot intended for it, which should always be proportionate to the volume of the roots and the vigor of the plant. However, this should only be done after placing a shard or a bed of gravel at the bottom of the pot, to facilitate drainage of the water used for watering. Next, a bed of soil is placed, the thickness of which should be calculated so that the surface of the rootball is 0.04m to 0.05m below the rim of the pot; then soil is poured between the rootball and the sides of the pot, taking care to keep the plant stem right in the middle, and, to ensure there are no voids, it is tamped down with a spatula; The bottom of the pot is lightly tapped, then filled with soil, this time compacted with the thumbs, leaving the surface of the soil about Om, Ol lower than the sides of the pot, to receive water for watering. This is all the care that temperate greenhouse plants require. Although given in a very succinct manner, this advice will always suffice for the cultivation of all plants that do not deviate from ordinary cultivation. §VII. - Composition of the soil to be used for the following plants. PELARGONIUMS. - One third heather soil, one third free-range soil, one third leaf compost, or, in the absence of manure, a little well-sieved poudrette (1). CALCÉOLAIRES. - Heather soil, free earth and leaf compost. VERVEINES. - Heather soil mixed with a portion of good vegetable garden soil. CINEMA. - Heather soil and compost. CAMELIAS. - Pure heather soil. HORTENSIAS. - Pure heather soil. SUCCULENTS. - Heather soil mixed with a little well-sieved powdered earth. 5. Warm greenhouse. - Like the temperate greenhouse, this greenhouse communicates with the orangery. As its structure is exactly the same as that of the temperate greenhouse, we refer to the latter for its construction, and will only discuss the interior layout. The inside path will be Om, 75 wide, and the bed 2m,25, including a small supporting wall Om, 45 high to support it; and another on the front of the (1) As in all circumstances the soil for potting must be very loose and well mixed, it must always be screened. The claie consists of a wooden frame, lined vertically with iron rods spaced about 0m,015 apart. These rods are supported by a crosspiece placed in the middle of the frame. To use it, you lean it (taking care to tilt it slightly) on a good stake. The sieve is a basket whose bottom is lined with wicker or wire mesh of varying widths, depending on the degree of loosening required. ANUEL DE JARDINAGE. greenhouse, where the pipes of the stove run, the mouth of which will always be on the outside. The shelf placed against the back wall is designed to hold potted strawberry plants. The manure layer can be replaced by a thermosiphon, with pipes running under a floor covered with a bed of tannin thick enough to bury the pots, and the inside temperature can be produced by the same device using pipes running above the layer. As the cultivation of greenhouse plants is very limited, we will limit ourselves to saying that during the winter, the temperature of the greenhouse should be maintained between 15 and 18 degrees centigrade. In April, we start to crimp the plants and give them a little air around mid-day. In the second half of May, we take out the hardiest plants, and bring them back in during September; finally, we can say that the general care to be given to hot-house plants is the same as that indicated for temperate greenhouse plants. CHAPTER XI. Vegetable garden. GARLIC (Allium sativum). - Multiplies from cloves planted in beds and borders towards the end of February and beginning of March. All soils are suitable, but it prefers those that are light and substantial. At the beginning of June, we tie a knot with the leaves and stem, in order to stop the sap flowing to the bulbs, which we pull off as soon as the leaves begin to dry out. Before putting them into bunches, we leave them on the ground for some time, where they finish ripening; then we hang them in a dry place to keep them until spring. Spanish garlic or Rocambole. - This species, less widespread than the previous one, differs in that it produces bulblets instead of seeds, which can be used for reproduction. Cultivation is the same. ANANAS (Ananassa sativa). - To raise pineapples and prepare them for fruiting, you need frames 1.65 m long by 1.33 m wide, and a well-exposed greenhouse with one or two gentle slopes, so that the plants are not too far from the glass. The first half of October is the best time to plant crowns and eyelets, because the young plants won't need any more care to overwinter in the ground than the old ones, and by spring the plants will already be strong and well rooted. Towards the end of September, prepare a good layer about Ou. 60 thick, made up of half new manure, half mixed leaves, or, failing that, part manure from old layers. The height of the layer should be calculated in such a way that, after being reloaded with Om, 20 or Om, 30. of tannin, or, failing that, moss, the plants are as close to the glass as possible. Eyelets for planting should preferably be taken from the leaf axils, where they are always strongest. After removing the eyelets, keep the old plants only if you are short of plants, and then only until they have produced the number of eyelets you need. Before planting the carnations, remove the leaves from the part to be planted (about Om, 05 to Om, 06); then cleanly cool the wound, and plant them immediately in pots Om, 10 to Om, 12 in diameter, depending on their strength. What we recommend for eyelets is, in all circumstances, applicable to crowns. If need be, crowns can be stored for at least a month in a shady, dry place. For planting, use pure heather soil or, failing that, a soil composed of one-fifth free earth, half heather soil and one-sixth compost, all prepared at least six months previously, stirred several times and put through a sieve. At the time of potting, this soil must not be damp, but must not be parched, although it is better to use it dry than damp. Immediately after planting, the pots are placed in the bed, starting with the top row and always choosing the highest plants. This must be observed each time they are replaced, because of the slope that must always be given to the frames. Take care to space them according to their strength. At night, cover the frames with straw mats; during the day, reduce the intensity of the sun's rays with canvas or mulch, which is spread over the frames; finally, for a month, the time needed for them to take root, care for them like cuttings. When they begin to vegetate, we give them a little air by lifting the frames when the sun is shining; then we water them at the base, but only as and when necessary. Around the beginning of November, i.e. when it's cold and wet, we surround the trunk with a good manure warmer, which should go down to the same depth as the bed, and from then until spring, it should be stirred at least every month, adding a portion of new manure each time. When the cold is severe, double the mats at night, spread a good layer of bedding over them, and keep the stoves at frame height; then uncover the panels every day, unless the thermometer falls below 4 or 5 degrees Celsius. In spring, watering should be more frequent and abundant, and more and more water is given as the sun gains strength. In the first days of May, a bed is laid, which must be much longer than the autumn bed, due to the development of the plants; but, as the temperature is milder, it need not be as warm as in autumn. The same applies to the stoves, which we don't make as deep and only remodel from time to time. This time, we replace the tan with a 32-centimeter-thick layer of soil, similar to that used for potting carnations; then we unstuff the pineapples, inspect the roots, and if any are rotten, we remove them; if not, we spare them all; After this, they are planted on the bed, taking care to press them in so that the old root ball is covered by a few centimetres of soil, to encourage new roots to emerge from the collar. Some time after planting, you begin to give the plants a little air; then you gradually increase the amount, depending on the temperature. At this point, it's preferable not to accustom pineapples to being shaded; in this way, you'll have much hardier plants, but you'll understand that you need to give them more air. During hot spells, they can be watered with a watering can, especially if planted on a good bed, as humidity is only really harmful in winter. Treated in this way, the pineapples will have developed by autumn to a degree that would scarcely be found in those grown constantly in pots for two years. Towards the end of September or the beginning of October, the Pineapples are lifted from the ground; all the eyelets are removed, followed by a few lower leaves; and as the Pineapple is one of the plants whose roots perish each year and are replaced by new ones, all the old ones are removed by cutting them close to the plant; after which the Pineapples are tied with a straw tie, so as to make them easier to repot, which should be done in pots of Om, 24 in diameter only. This operation is called bare-bottom potting. After potting, we place them on a new layer, and until they have new roots, we give them the same care as we would for first-age carnations. Around January, we place them in a greenhouse, where we've prepared a layer about 6.65 m thick and the full width of the recess, which must not be less than 2 meters wide. This layer should be filled with a good bed of tannin or moss, so that the pots can be easily buried in it, and placed about Om, 50 apart in all directions; finally, depending on the strength of the plants, they are left in this way until they set fruit, i.e. from April to July, when they are planted in the open ground on the same layer, after reshuffling it and replacing the tannin with a bed of soil. For as long as the pineapples remain in the greenhouse, the layer mentioned above can be replaced by thermosiphon heating; in this case, the tanning bed, and consequently the soil, is placed on a floor under which the heating pipes run. The heating is adjusted so as to maintain a temperature of around 25 to 30 degrees Celsius in the layer, which is quite sufficient for the needs of these plants. In spring, we start heating less, and stop completely in May, because from then until September, the sun's heat is sufficient. The greenhouse in which pineapples are placed is usually divided in two by a glass partition, so that there are two seasons. The strongest plants are placed in the first compartment, and we usually start heating them towards the end of January. From then on, the temperature of the greenhouse should be maintained at a constant 25 to 30 degrees; at night, until the end of April, the greenhouse is covered with mulch, which must be removed every day. For watering at the foot of the plants, use water in which animal or vegetable substances have been decomposed. In winter, watering should be subordinated to the warmth of the bed, and care should be taken to ensure that the water is at the temperature of the greenhouse; in summer, however, watering should be abundant, with occasional ponding. As we have already indicated, it is necessary to provide plenty of air, to avoid shading. First-season fruit usually ripens from July to September. Care should be taken not to raise the temperature on the side of the greenhouse where the second-season plants are placed to more than 12 degrees; but in March, when we usually start heating them, we should observe all that has been indicated for the first season. Second-season fruit usually ripens from September to December. As we can see, treating pineapples as we have just described will produce fruit that can be harvested twenty or twenty-six months after the eyelets have been planted, conclusively demonstrating the superiority of this method of cultivation over that used in the past. Varieties. - From Martinique. - Cayenne. - From Jamaica. - From Providence - From Mont-Serrat. - Duchesse-d'Orléans. - Comte-de-Paris. - Enville. - Poli blanc. - Charlotte Rothschild. ARROCHE DES JARDINS. Belle-Dame, Bonne-Dame (Atriplex horensis). - This plant is grown only to soften the acidity of sorrel. It is sown in spring and requires no care; it usually reseeds itself and, when you have a few plants, it is rarely necessary to sow them. Arroche seeds keep well for only one year. Varieties. - Arroche blonde. - Arroche rouge. artichoke (cynara scolymus). - To grow artichokes successfully, you need soft, substantial soil; they like warmth, and fear cold humidity. They can be propagated from seed sown on a bed in February and March, or immediately placed in April and May; but, as they rarely reproduce their varieties, it is preferable to propagate them by eyelet. This is done as follows: In April, the shoots born at the collar of old plants are broken off, taking care to remove them with the heel or portion of the root collar; then the strongest are selected, the leaf tips are shortened, and, after preparing the soil well, they are planted in a chessboard pattern, at about Om, 75 in slightly poor soils, and at I meter in those where vigorous vegetation is hoped for. If well hoeed and watered, a good proportion of these carnations will bear fruit in autumn, and all will bear abundant fruit the following spring. Each year, in the autumn, the old stems and the tips of the longest leaves must be cut off; then, in November, in a word, before the frosts, they must be "buttered", an operation which consists in raising the soil around each plant; and when the frosts begin to make themselves felt, they are completely covered with leaves or litter, which is removed every time the weather gets milder. In March, when frosts are no longer to be feared, we destroy the artichoke mounds and give them a good ploughing; then, in April, we grommet them, as previously indicated, so as to leave only the two or three most beautiful grommets on each plant. Artichoke plantations only produce abundantly after four years, so replant every three years to avoid any interruption in harvesting. As artichoke roots do not grow very deep, they do not exhaust the surrounding soil, and young artichokes can be counter-planted in advance between those to be destroyed, so that when the old artichokes are uprooted, the soil is covered with young plants in full ratio. It's easy to bring forward the artichoke production period. Whether you force them into place, or raise them in clods in November to plant them in a trunk, care consists in surrounding the trunk with a manure stove during frosts, covering the panels at night and providing air during the day. Artichokes treated in this way produce in April. Alternatively, and much more simply, Artichokes can be forced as follows: In the first fortnight of February, remove the soil from the paths around the bed to a depth of about Om.50, and replace it with a stove of new manure; then place hoops far and wide across the bed, to support the mats used to cover the artichokes at night and in bad weather; The soil is then covered with hot manure, to activate vegetation, and the stoves are changed every ten or fifteen days, each time adding more or less new manure, depending on the temperature. Artichoke seeds keep well for up to five years. Varieties. - Vert de Provence. - Vert de Laon. - Camus de Bretagne. - Violet. ASPARAGUS (Asparagus officinalis). - Two varieties are grown: the common or green asparagus, and the large purple or Dutch asparagus. Asparagus from Marchiennes, Ulm, Besançon and Vendôme are only varieties of the latter, the result of local influences. Asparagus is propagated from seed, which is sown in March, either in the field or in a nursery, in the ground or on a bed, for subsequent planting. Planting methods vary from country to country, but the one described here has produced excellent results in all the localities where it has been used, so we feel we should give it preference. After selecting a favorable site, in autumn Om, 25 to Om, 30 of soil is removed from the entire surface of the land to be planted. If, at this depth, the soil is not of good quality, we remove an extra spade shoe, which we replace with an equal quantity of good soil taken from the surface of the ground or from any other part of the garden. You could even mix in some old, consumed turf or the remains of old layers, if the soil is too damp or too compact and able to retain water; but, in either case, you spread a good bed of cow manure or other good fertilizer at the bottom of the trench; for Asparagus to do well, it needs soil that is not only light and sandy, but also well amended; Then, on top of all this, a bed of good soil is brought in, the thickness of which must be calculated so that the Asparagus claws are planted at Om, 15 deep, instead of Om, 35 which used to be indicated as a result of an error that experience has shown to be necessary to modify. In the course of March, a good ploughing is given, and a rake is passed over the whole area to remove clods and stones; the land is divided into plots Im, 33 wide, between each of which a path of about Om,50 is left; after which four rows are marked out, which must be equally spaced from each other, and in such a way that the two outer rows are Om, 16 from the edges of the plank. Then take one- or two-year-old seedlings, carefully pulled out with a fork, so that the roots are not broken; place the claws about Om, 40 apart on the line, and, after spreading the roots well, cover them with loose soil; it would even have to be put through the rake, if it were mixed with stones from clods or badly broken soil. Once the beds are evenly covered, we spread a good mulch of half-consumed fnmier over each one. Each year, in autumn, old stems are cut off, lightly hoeed and then mulched with half-consumed manure. If you proceed in this way, the asparagus will be in full ratio by the third shoot, and the larger ones will be cut off with an asparagus knife. This can be done at this point without affecting the following year's harvest. Sowing can also be done in situ, after preparing the beds as described above. You sow in rows in February or March, and when the plant is strong enough, you thin it out, leaving only the most beautiful plants at a distance equal to the one we have indicated; after which the other care to be given is the same as for planted Asparagus. We would like to add another procedure, communicated to the Cercle Général d'Horticulture by Mr. Lenormand, who has been practicing it successfully for many years. We'll leave it to this skilled horticulturist to explain how he establishes his asparagus plantations: "In April 1834, I made trenches Im, 30 wide by Om, 33 deep, in which I made layers, which, trodden and wetted, were from Om, 38 to Om, 40 thick; after covering them with well-levelled earth, I placed the boxes intended to receive the frames, and in each frame I planted twelve claws of Asparagus of one year's sowing. After planting, I covered the soil with a good mulch and planted two vines of Melons, which came up perfectly without harming the Asparagus. When the melon plants were three-quarters strong, I added four cauliflowers per frame, and after the melon harvest, in September, I sowed lamb's lettuce for the winter; the whole thing was a complete success. "The following February, the layer having settled, I reseeded my claws with soil from the paths, then planted Lettuces and Romaines on top, with two rows of Cauliflowers per bed, which made all traces of the layer disappear. The whole thing grew with amazing speed and strength, as I got Asparagus of Om, 07 in circumference. By improving the soil in this way, we can obtain two harvests a year, independently of the Asparagus, which we can force from the second year, and continue in this way, leaving them one year of rest out of three. This plant, established in 1834, still exists today, which proves that the claws lose none of their vigor, even though they are brought into relationship three years earlier than they could have been by the old process." Argenteuil growers, whose market garden produce enjoys a justly-deserved reputation, cultivate asparagus in rows, usually between vines, of which there are large tracts in this locality. Planted shallowly, I meters apart in all directions, Argenteuil asparagus is regularly smoked every two years. They are hoeed as often as necessary, and dethatched every year in November, which involves removing a few centimetres of the soil surface; they are then ridged in February or March. If practised intelligently, this method of cultivation gives such remarkable results that it can be adopted with confidence by all those who wish to harvest very large quantities of asparagus. If you want to have early Asparagus, you can start forcing a bed in the first days of November, and continue successively until February, which is done in the following way: after placing the trunks on the beds you wish to force, spread a bed of potting soil over the Asparagus, then remove the soil from the paths to a depth of Om, 50, and place it on the beds in such a way as to reload them with Om, 33 approximately, in order to obtain much longer Asparagus; then replace the soil in the paths with a new manure stove, which should be raised to the height of the panels with which you cover the boxes; but before placing the panels, spread a bed of manure on the boards, to activate vegetation; take care, however, to remove this fu nier as soon as the Asparagus begin to emerge from the ground. Whatever the state of the weather, don't give the asparagus any air. During the night and in bad weather, we cover the panels with good straw mats, to concentrate the heat. The stoves are changed every ten or fifteen days or so, adding more or less new manure each time, depending on the temperature, so as to obtain a heat under the panels of not less than 15 degrees, and no more than 25. These Asparagus are usually good to cut twenty or twenty-five days (depending on the temperature) after you start forcing them. Old asparagus or asparagus to be destroyed can be planted on a bed, where they will produce, once only, thin, green asparagus to be eaten as peas. To do this, prepare a layer of Om, 60 to Om, 80 thick, with a heat of 20 to 25 degrees; lay down boxes, fill the layer with a few centimetres of potting soil, then fill the paths, but only halfway. When the layer has caught its first fire, we take the asparagus claws and, without subtracting anything from the length of the roots, we place them on the layer one beside the other; we leave them in this state for a few days, after which we pour a little potting soil between the claws, so as to cover them slightly, then we finish filling the paths, and reshape them if necessary. At night, we cover the panels with straw mats, and as soon as the asparagus start to grow, we give them air during the day, unless the temperature is too unfavorable. After twelve or fifteen days, the Asparagus starts to produce, and we cut for as long as it lasts, which is about three months. Asparagus seeds ripen towards the end of October, and are good for four years. AUBERGINE or MÉLONGÈNE (Solanum Melongena). - In the Paris climate, Aubergine is sown towards the end of December or at the beginning of January, on a bed with a temperature of 20 to 25 degrees. Two or three weeks after sowing, the seedling is transplanted into the nursery, but on a layer less warm than the first; after a while, it is lifted and transplanted a second time before being put in place. When the plant has recovered and the temperature permits, we start to give it some air.
