In the course of March, a final layer is prepared, the length of which should be proportionate to the number of plants to be grown; the boxes are placed, the layer is filled with potting soil, and when the layer is warm enough (15 to 20 degrees), four eggplants are planted under each 1m, 33. panel; After that, we start to give a little air, either from the top or from the bottom of the panels; then we gradually increase the amount as the season progresses, so as to remove the panels and trunks in the course of May. Other care consists of watering as needed, and cleaning leaves attacked by kermes. Eggplant seeds are good for seven years, Varieties. - Violet, - white, - variegated, - giant. BASELLE (Basella). - Climbing plant whose leaves replace spinach. Sow in March on a bed, and when there are no more frosts to worry about, plant out in the open ground, at the foot of a wall, with good exposure. Baselle seeds germinate for three years. Varieties. - Red Baselle, - White Baselle. COMMON BASILICO (Ocimum Basilicum). - An annual plant used, along with its varieties, as a seasoning. All are sown in March, in the soil, to be replanted in May in a shady spot. Basilie seeds can be stored for six years. BETTERAVE (Beta vulgaris). - It is sown at the end of April or beginning of May, in rows or broadcast, in deeply ploughed and smoked soil from the previous year; then, when the plants have five or six leaves, they are lit so that they are about Om, 35 apart, and replanted in places where they are lacking, an operation that should only be carried out in rainy weather. During the summer, they are hoeed several times, and towards the end of October or the beginning of November, the roots are harvested after the leaves have been cut off. Put them in the greenhouse or in a healthy cellar, where they can be stored until May. Beet seeds ripen in September and keep well for five or six years. Varieties. - Rouge, - rouge de Castelnaudary, - rouge foncé de Whyte, - Turnep, - jaune, - jaune de Castelnaudary. BOURRACHE (Borrago officinalis). - A plant whose flowers are used to decorate salads; it grows in all types of soil, and is planted in spring and autumn. Borage seeds germinate in three years. LARGE CAPUCIN (Tropœolum majus). - Sow in April at the foot of a wall, in a well-exposed spot. It can also be sown on its own, but must be raked. The flowers are used to dress salads; the seeds, picked while still green, can be preserved in vinegar and used in place of capers. Nasturtium seeds last five years. CARDON (Cynara Cardunculus). - Cardoons need soft, deep soil and frequent watering in summer. They propagate from seed sown in April on a bed, or better still in May, immediately in the field. Holes are made about a meter apart, filled with potting soil, and two or three seeds are sown in each. When the seeds have sprouted, the most vigorous plant is chosen and the others removed. If you have to fear the ravages of grubs or crickets, you should sow them in pots at the same time, so that you can replenish the empty spaces. Around September, when they are strong enough to be blanched, they are stuffed by attaching a tie made of litter to the plant's collar, then rolling it up and down so that only the tips of the leaves are visible. After a fortnight or three weeks, the ribs are white and must be eaten immediately, otherwise they will rot; they must therefore be stuffed one after the other. Before heavy frosts, we pull up the Cardoon clods to replant them close together in the vegetable greenhouse, where they will blanch without covering; but we must visit them often and remove all rotten leaves. In this way, they can be kept until March. Cardoon seeds ripen in the first half of September, and are good for seven years. Varieties. - Cardon de Tours, - d'Espagne, - Puvis, - plein inerme, - à côtés rouges. CARROT (Daucus carota). - The first seedlings are sown on a bed in December. A layer of Om, 35 to Om, 40 thick, with a heat of 15 to 20 degrees, is prepared; the boxes are placed, the Om, 15 layer is filled with potting soil, and, unless it's very cold, the paths are only half-filled. When the layer is warm enough, sow the variety known as Early Short Carrot or Dutch Carrot. Among the carrots, you can transplant a few small black lettuces, or sow a few pink radishes. During the night, we cover the panels with straw mats; when the sowing is well under way, we rework the warmers, which we then make the full height of the trunks. These carrots are usually ready for harvesting in April. If, in the second half of March, the weather is mild and the panels covering the carrots are needed, they can be removed, along with the trunks, but harvesting then takes longer. In the open ground, the first sowings can be made as early as February, and continued until July. However, this can only be done for the Early Short Carrot; for the other varieties, sowing should not go beyond April, so that they can reach full development before winter. Whatever the time of sowing, the soil must be well prepared, after which it must be broadcast. The soil is lightly harrowed with a fork, tilled and a layer of compost is spread over each bed. Rake lightly over the whole area, and water whenever necessary. When the carrots have emerged, we thin out the plant, which is almost always thick if the sowing has been successful. In November, we cut off the collar of each carrot; we put them in gauge, then we cover them with large manure during the frosts, or we deposit them in the vegetable greenhouse, in order to have some during the winter. In light, healthy soils, there's no need to uproot them; simply cover the carrot plants during the frosts. Carrot seeds are harvested in August; their germination period is four years. Varieties. - Rouge courte de Hollande, - demi-longue, - longue, - d'Altringham, - jaune longue, - rouge pâle de Flandre, - blanche, - violette d'Espagne. CULTIVATED CELERY. - A variety of Apium graveolens, it is sown on a bed in the open air as early as February; the seed should be very lightly covered. In April, plant in the open ground at a distance of about Om, 33 cm. From April to June, sow in the ground in a shady spot and plant out immediately. We encourage seed germination by frequent watering, and if the plant is too thick, it should be thinned out to prevent it withering away. In June and July, the seedlings are transplanted into place. Four rows of Om, 33 wide are drawn per bed, then planted at Om, 33 apart along the line. Immediately after planting, water to facilitate recovery, and continue until the Celery is strong enough to be blanched: a trench 1 m wide is opened, with the soil thrown in to the right and left, after which the Celery is lifted in clods and planted in the trench; eight clods are placed in each row, then potting soil is poured in between each row, so that it is completely buried, except for the tips of the leaves. After a fortnight, it's usually white enough to be harvested; but as it doesn't keep for long after that, you should only bleach it successively, so as to prolong the harvest as long as possible. During frosts, it is covered with litter, which is removed whenever the temperature permits. With care, it can be kept until the end of February. Varieties. - Plein blanc, - Turc, - Cole's superb red, - Plein rose, - violet, - for cutting. Celeriac. - Sowing can take place in April, in the open ground, in a shady spot; but it's better to sow in February on a bed, transplant the seedling on a bed; then put it in the open ground, after cutting off the large leaves and all the lateral roots. Water abundantly during the summer; then remove all unnecessary leaves, taking care to spare those in the heart, an operation which should be repeated as often as necessary to encourage the development of the tuber. Celeriac is pulled out at the beginning of winter to put it in gauge, and covered during the frosts; or it is put into the vegetable greenhouse after the leaves have been cut off. Treated in this way, it can easily be stored until March. CERFEUIL (Scandix cerefolium). - Sow almost year-round; in spring and autumn, at the foot of a wall and with good exposure, and in hot weather, in a northern exposure. Curly chervil. - This variety requires nothing more than ordinary Chervil; it is used for the same purposes, and has the advantage over the latter of not being confused with Hemlock. Chervil seeds ripen in June, and keep for two years. Bulbous chervil (hœrophyllum bulbosum). - Bulbous chervil is an edible plant whose roots do not extend beyond the base of the plant.

proportions of a small Dutch Carrot. It is very starchy and has a pleasant flavor. Bulbous Chervil is sown in September, i.e. as soon as the seeds have been harvested; otherwise, they won't germinate until the second year, unless you take the precaution of storing them in sand until sowing time, which may not be until spring. After sowing, the seed is covered with a good layer of potting soil; once this is done, bulbous chervil requires no special care other than the weeding and watering that all garden produce requires. Regardless of when it is sown, bulbous chervil is good for harvesting in July. Bulbous Chervil seed is harvested in July. It is only good for one year. COMESTIBLE MUSHROOM (Agaricus edulis). - The success of mushroom beds depends on the choice and preparation of the manure and the care given to the beds. To set up a Mushroom wheel, you need to use manure from horses that do hard work; as it is renewed less often, it is softer, i.e. more impregnated with urine, and contains more dung than that of luxury horses. The manure is first deposited in a heap, to allow it to ferment; then, about a month later, it is taken up with a fork to form a layer (called a planchée) about Om, 65 thick by Im, 33 wide. We spread a first bed, taking care to remove the longest straws, ties and hay, then mix the dry parts well with those most impregnated with urine; and to form the edges of the layer we turn the manure on its sides, so that the ends are inside. Once a bed of manure has been formed, it is wetted with the watering can, then trodden down with the feet. We then make a second bed, which we treat in the same way, and so on until we have reached the height indicated. Eight or ten days later, the bed is reshuffled, starting at one end, then turned over in the same way as the first time, but taking care to return what was on the edges and on top to the center. After leaving it to ferment for a further eight or ten days, the manure should finally be ready for grinding, i.e. it should be fat but not too wet, and should have only the right amount of heat for the operation. As summer thunderstorms often abort the spawn, we don't start cultivating mushrooms in the open air until September, and then continue successively until December. After ensuring that the manure is in good condition, we start erecting the millstones, which should be Om.50 wide at the base and the same height. The manure is tamped down as the wheel is raised, to ensure that it is compacted as little as possible. It is mounted in the shape of a humpback, so that it is only Om, 10 wide at the top. During the operation, the sides of the grinding wheel are carefully firmed by lightly tapping with the back of the shovel, then the long straws protruding from each side are removed with the rake. If, after the millstones have been set up, heavy rain should fall, they should be wrapped in a shirt (a large litter blanket), which, in favorable weather, should only take place after the millstones have been gobbled up, an operation we'll talk about later. After eight to ten days, check the degree of heat with a diaper thermometer, and if it doesn't mark more than 15 to 18 degrees, you can larder the millstone, i.e. cut a row of small openings on both sides, at Om, 10 or Om, 15 from the ground, depending on whether it's dry or damp, a row of small openings to be made by hand, at Om, 33 from each other, in which to place the spawn (1) flush with the side of the wheel, then ap(1) Mushroom spawn is the name given to small white filaments, rather like mold, that form in manure. They can be found either in manure that has been in piles for a long time, where very good ones are often formed, or in old melon beds; failing that, they can be taken from a millstone that is already in use, but where only one harvest has yet been made. Mushroom spawn can be kept in a dry place for up to two years. lightly stink to bring the spawn into perfect contact with the manure; but if you fear that there is still too much heat, bring the manure closer only after a few days. If, eight or ten days after larding the millstone, small whitish filaments begin to spread over the whole surface, take light, lean earth, salped as much as possible, pass it over the claie and spread it out to a thickness of about Om, 03, which you press down lightly with the back of the shovel, which is called gobetering. If you don't notice the traces we've mentioned, you'll have to repeat the operation, putting new white into openings next to the old ones. If the weather is mild and dry, the grinding stone can be cooled by lightly dabbing it with water, but care must be taken not to give it too much water at once, as excess humidity will destroy the nascent mushrooms. After gobbling, cover the wheel with a liner of Om, 05 to Om, 06 of large bedding (a thicker liner could cause the manure to ferment again, which would destroy all hope of harvesting), increasing it during frosts and according to the severity of the cold. About six weeks later, we'll start picking the first mushrooms. To find them, carefully lift the bedding, and after picking them, fill the holes they occupied with soil of the same type as that used to gobet the millstone. If you find a few small places where the young Mushrooms have perished, remove all the destroyed part and replace with new soil. At all times, even after one side of the wheel has been used up, it should be carefully covered with bedding. A millstone can produce for three to five months at any time, but 7. best in summer. You can set up your millstones in a dimly-lit cellar, and then, given the equality of temperature that prevails in these localities, it becomes unnecessary to cover the millstones with litter. CHENILLETTE (Scorpiurus vermiculata), VeRS (Astragalus hamosus). LIMAÇON (Medicago turbinata). - Native annual plants of the Papilionaceae family, whose fruits imitate caterpillars, worms or limacons, and which should be sown in place, in April and May, about Om, 30 apart. CHERVIS or CHIROUIS (Sium sisarum). - A plant whose fleshy, very sweet roots can be eaten like Scorsonera. Sow in spring or September, in loose open soil, then hoe and water frequently. Chervis seeds germinate in two years. CHICORICE. Wild chicory (Cichorium intubus). - The emerging leaves of this species are used to make a highly prized salad, which can be obtained almost all year round by sowing in beds from March onwards, then in the open ground from April until autumn. If the roots are to be used to make the salad known as Barbe-de-capucin, they must be sown in rays in April, with hoeing and watering being all that's required; then, as frost approaches, the roots are pulled out, lifted with a fork to avoid breaking them. In October, when this work is usually started, a layer about Om40 thick is prepared, at a temperature of 15 to 20 degrees Celsius. The best place for this operation is a low cellar with no air or light. Once the bed has caught its first fire, the roots are put together in bunches, but only after careful removal of old leaves and any parts likely to cause mildew; they are then placed upright on the bed, and watered frequently with the apple watering can; but, as always, watering must be in proportion to the heat of the bed; as soon as the Chicory starts to grow, watering must be done very sparingly to avoid rotting the inside of the bunches. Chicory is usually long enough to be harvested after fifteen to eighteen days. Chicory can be successively blanched from October or November to April. Chicory can also be blanched by burying roots on a bed covered with solid-framed panels, to intercept light, or with ordinary panels, which are kept constantly covered with straw mats. Wild chicory seeds ripen in September, and keep well for eight to ten years. Curly chicory (Cichorium endivia). - The first seedlings can be sown in September under cloches, but cold. From then until June, curly or Italian chicory is sown. In the first days of October, the seedling is transplanted, also under cloches, and towards the end of October or the beginning of November, Chicory is planted on the ground, but under panels; air is given as often as possible, and during frosts, the panels are covered at night. In January or February, sow on a warm bed under panels. The bed must not be less than 20 to 25 degrees hot, because to obtain a plant that doesn't bolt, the seeds must germinate within twenty-four hours, regardless of the time of year; it's better to start sowing again 'than to transplant a plant that has languished. Twelve or fifteen days after sowing, the seedling is transplanted into the nursery, to be planted a fortnight or three weeks later, still under panels, but on a softer layer. In the second half of March, you can start transplanting Chicory in the open ground, but under cloches or panels, which you remove as soon as the weather is favorable. In April, May and June, Chicory is still sown on beds, but then the plant can be transplanted immediately into the ground. Draw four rows per bed, Im, 33 wide, and plant Om, 40 apart along the line. In June and July, Chicorée de Meaux is sown in the ground in a shady spot. However, in many areas, it would be better to continue sowing in the soil, but in the open air. When the plant is strong enough to be transplanted, spread a good mulch on each bed, then plant at the distance indicated above, and give it a good watering to help it take root; other care consists of pulling out weeds and wetting if necessary. When the Chicory is sufficiently covered, take advantage of dry weather to lift the leaves, which are then tied with rushes or straw to whiten the inside; but as they whiten in a short time, they should only be tied in proportion to consumption. As soon as they are bound, they should be watered only at the neck, to avoid wetting them, which could cause them to rot, and, as soon as the first frosts set in, they should be covered with straw mats or bedding, which should be removed whenever the weather permits; then, as the frosts increase, they should be uprooted and returned to the vegetable greenhouse, where they should be half-buried in sand: in this way, they can be kept until January. Chicorée frisée de la Passion. - This interesting variety, which we owe to the kindness of M. Charvet, owner at La Cellette, near Blois, is just as hardy as the Lettuce whose name it bears. Sown in August, transplanted in September and planted out in October, Passion Chicory is good for harvesting in March and April, a time of year when garden produce is generally scarce. As such, Curly Passion Chicory can be considered a good buy. Ever-white chicory. - This variety is not as widely grown as it deserves, as it can be a good substitute for spinach, especially in summer, when spinach is often hard to come by. It can be sown in the field or in the ground, from February to August. Scarole. - Scarole is a variety of Chicory whose cultivation is very similar to that of Chicorée de Meaux. The seeds are good for harvesting at the end of September, and keep for five or six years. Varieties. - Frisée de Meaux, - fine d'été or d'Italie, - de Rouen, or corne-de-cerf, - de la Passion, - always white, - sauvage, - panachée, - améliorée, - Scarole ordinaire, - blonde or à feuilles de laitue. CABBAGE (Brassica). - Cabbages need cool, well-smoked soil, because the more fertilizer they receive, the fatter they become. They come in many varieties, but all can be divided into five main races: 1° cabbages, 2° savoy cabbages, 3° green cabbages, 4° kohlrabi, and 5° cauliflowers and broccoli. N. 1. head cabbage. - Towards the end of August or in the first days of September, early cabbages are sown. In October, transplant the seedlings in a nursery along a wall with good exposure, for planting in late November or early December, and in February or March in cold or damp soils. If winter is harsh, it will be necessary to secure the plant, either with bedding or with mats laid on top of gaulettes. If you run out of plants in spring, you can sow them in February on a bed and in March on a well-soiled bed. Second-season and late-season cabbages are usually only sown in February or March; however, they can also be sown in August. Whatever the time of sowing, these cabbages should be transplanted immediately into place, 50, 60 or 80 centimetres apart, depending on the proportions of the varieties to be grown. The soil should be well fertilized, as it should not be forgotten that it is only by dint of manure and water that we can obtain all that this plant should produce. Varieties. - Cabus hâtifs, d'York, - cœur-de-bœuf. - Pain-desucre. Cabus 2nd season, Pomerania, - Winigstadt, - Joannet, - Schweinfurt. Cabus tardifs, from Saint-Denis, - from Holland, - vert de Vaugirard, - from Germany dit Quintal, - dark red. Cabbage preservation. - As heavy frosts are very detrimental to cabbages, in November, all cabbages with apples should be uprooted and placed on a plank, close to each other and with their heads slightly inclined. When there is a heavy frost, cover them with litter or leaves, and remove them as soon as the weather is mild. In light soils, you can bury the head instead of the roots: in this way, cabbages can be kept until May without covering; it is a good idea, however, to cover part of them, so as to be able to pull them out during frosts. N. 2 Savoy cabbage. - They are sown from the end of February to June, and transplanted immediately into the ground like Savoy cabbages. More hardy than the latter, Savoy cabbages usually withstand winter without shelter. Varieties. - Hâtif d'Ulm, - pied-court, - Victoria, - ordinaire, - doré, - pancalier, - des Vertus, - de Bruxelles. N. 3 Green cabbages. - We divide them into the only ones grown in the kitchen garden; the others are grown extensively as fodder, and some as plants are even more pleasant to eat when frost has softened them. Varieties. - Big-ribbed cabbage, - Fringed big-ribbed cabbage, - Strawberry cabbage. Cabbages of the second category are sown in July and August, or better still in March and April, and the seedling is transplanted immediately; but they can also be sown in place, either in rows or broadcast. These cabbages produce leaves throughout the winter and into their second year. Varieties. - Cavalier or Tree cabbage. - Caulet de Flandre. - branchu de Poitou. - de grand Jouan. N. 4 Kohlrabi. - The first sowings take place towards the end of February, and can continue until June; they are sown on a composted bed, and later put in place. These cabbages differ from the others by their neck, which is swollen and fleshy, and which is used in cooking like turnips; but, to keep them tender, they must be watered frequently in summer. Varieties - White or Siam, - early white. - violet. - early purple. Chou-navet, quite similar to the preceding variety, produces a fleshy tuber with the same flavor as kohlrabi. It is sown in the field in May and June, either in rows or as a broadcast crop. The next step is to thin out the plant so that the cabbages are about Om.40 apart. They have little fear of frost, and, barring a severe winter, can be pulled up only as and when needed. Cabbage seeds ripen in July; they keep well for five or six years. Varieties. - White turnip cabbage, - Rutabaga or Swedish turnip. N. 5 Cauliflower. - These are much more delicate than the other cabbage varieties, and like light, well-smoked soil, with plenty of watering in summer. Several varieties are grown, but it is impossible to recommend one over another, as results depend solely on local causes. The first seedlings are sown in September, in a well-soiled bed or on an old layer, to be transplanted in October, in a nursery, on a bed; and when it freezes, cloches or panels are placed over the plant. Care must be taken to provide air every day, as long as the temperature permits. If, in spite of this precaution, the plant should advance too far, it should be uprooted and replanted to delay vegetation; then, when the cold becomes severe, surround the bells with litter, and cover the whole with straw mats; uncover whenever the weather permits, and provide air. In December, you can plant some of your cauliflowers on beds and under panels, with a few lettuces in between. During the night, cover the panels with straw mats, water as needed, and provide air whenever the temperature permits; then, when the cauliflowers reach the stained-glass windows, take care to raise the boxes; if the weather is favorable in the second half of March, remove the panels. These cauliflowers will produce in April and May; in March, we plant the other part in the ground, and they will give from the end of May to July. They can also be sown on a bed in February and March, and shortly afterwards transplanted on a bed and planted out in late March or early April, producing in June and July. The last cauliflowers are sown in June: this is the time when most cauliflowers are sown, and when cultivation is easiest: it's the same as for ordinary cabbages, and all success depends on abundant watering, which must be very frequent, especially during the first few months. Sow in a shady spot in a well-soiled bed; when the plant is strong enough, transplant immediately. These cauliflowers produce from the end of August to November; they can even be kept until February, and sometimes until April. To do this, cut them as late as possible, and especially when the weather is dry, so as not to bring them in until they are well dried out, as this will determine how long they can be kept. Preserving cauliflowers. - After removing all the leaves from cauliflowers, place them on the shelves of the vegetable greenhouse, or, which is even better, hang them upside down. As they shrink a lot as they dry out, the day before you want to eat them, refresh the tips of the cores and soak them in fresh water for a few hours, taking care not to get the heads wet; they soon regain their original shape, without losing any of their quality. Cauliflower seed is harvested in September. They germinate for five years. Varieties. - Tender or petit Salomon, - demi-dur or gros Salomon, - Lenormand, - dur de Paris, - d'Angleterre, - de Hollande. Broccoli. - Broccoli is sown in March, April and May, starting with the earliest varieties. When the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted to the nursery, and later planted in rows like cauliflower. Early varieties sown in March are good for harvesting in September. Others produce successively during winter and spring. More hardy than cauliflower, Broccoli can withstand a few degrees of cold without suffering. However, it's a good idea to remove the clods as frost approaches, and replant them in a trench over which frames or straw mats have been placed. Broccoli seeds germinate for five years. Varieties. - Early white, - Late white, - Early violet. - Sprouting (this last variety produces small apples like Brussels sprouts). CHOU MARIN (Crambe maritima). - Crambe is a very good vegetable, whose emerging leaves are eaten and blanched by ridding the stalk; it is hardy and easy to grow. Under favorable conditions (i.e. sandy, well-smoked soil), it produces for a very long time. We have seen a plantation of Crambés in full report, which for fifteen years has given several harvests each year. It can be propagated from seed sown in the field or in the nursery soon after harvest, as autumn sowings are generally more successful than spring ones. Crambes can also be propagated by root cuttings, but sowing always produces, as is easy to understand, more vigorous plants. Crambes are planted in autumn or spring. Two rows are drawn in an Im, 33-wide bed, and planted at Om, 50 apart along the line. Each year, in autumn, the dead leaves are removed, the plants are hoeed, and a good bed of half-consumed manure is spread over the beds. You could start cutting the leaves off the Crambes as early as the second flush, but it's better to wait until the third, by which time they'll be in full vegetative growth, and you'll be able to keep them much longer. We usually start buttering Crambes towards the end of January or the beginning of February; but, so that they don't all give together, we only butter part of them, and the rest a fortnight later, which is done in the following way: we place on each plant a heap of compost (or light earth) of about Om, 20, and cover the whole with a good bed of manure or leaves, in order to activate vegetation; about a month later, when the tips of the leaves begin to appear, we cut them back to the ground, but taking care to spare the eyes at the neck of the plant, as without this precaution it would no longer grow. After harvesting, they are ridged again, and produce a second crop that is often as abundant as the first. After the second cut, destroy the mounds, spread some of the compost on the beds, and remove the rest. You can also force Crambé under panels, like Asparagus: this simply involves placing boxes and panels over the Crambés in January or February, after having ridged them; if you don't have solid-frame panels, you can use ordinary panels, provided you keep them constantly covered with straw mats. To stimulate Crambé vegetation, you can surround the boxes with a manure stove, as heat does nothing to harm this plant. You can even, as is done in England, place large clumps of Crambes in the greenhouse to be forced during the winter, so as to have good ones to harvest ahead of time, or give the forced ones away on the spot. Crambé seeds are harvested in August; they keep well for three years. CIBOULE COMMUNE (Allium fistulosum). - The first seedlings are sown in February, in the field and broadcast; from then on, sowing can continue successively until July. After sowing, the seeds are covered with a light layer of compost, and watered whenever necessary. If you don't want to run out of Ciboule in winter, pull it out in November, put it in gauges, then cover it with dry litter during the frosts. The seeds ripen in August, and keep for two years. Perennial Ciboule - Multiplies by splinters in spring or autumn. CIBOULETTE. Civet (Allium Schœnoprasum). - This plant multiplies by its cloves, which are separated in February and March for planting in borders. The more often it is cut, the more tender it becomes and the better it grows. To overwinter, cut at ground level and cover with compost. CUCUMBER (Cucumis sativus). - Cucumbers are sown in January, February and March, on a warm bed and under panels. When the cotyledons and first leaves are well-developed, they are transplanted into small pots, sown on a warm bed and planted some time later, also on a bed and under panels. Later, when frosts are no longer a threat, cucumbers can be planted in the ground. In light soils that are easy to warm up, cucumbers can even be sown in place, in small pits filled with potting soil. Like melons, cucumbers need to be pruned to produce good fruit, as described in the Melon article. Small green gherkin cucumber. - Sow in early May on beds and under panels. Shortly afterwards, the seedling is transplanted to the nursery, also on beds and under panels. As soon as it has taken root, we start to give it a little air, to strengthen it, and towards the end of May or the beginning of June, we raise it in clods to put it in the ground in good exposure, Om, 60 apart. Gherkins can also be sown in the ground; they are even hardier than other cucumbers, and don't need pruning. In naturally damp soils, Cucumbers and Gherkins should be rowed like Peas and Beans, so that they do not lie on the ground, in order to harvest good fruit. Cucumber seeds germinate for five years. Varieties. - Early white, - large white, - long yellow, - English long green, - Russian, - snake. PUMPKIN (Cucurbita maxima). - Pumpkins are sown in March, on a warm bed and under panels; in April, they are transplanted in the nursery, also on a bed and under panels. A few days after transplanting, we start to give them a little air, in order to strengthen the plant; and, in May, we prepare holes which we arrange so that the Pumpkins are at least 1m, 65 from each other. The holes are filled with manure and covered with about Om, 15 of compost. If, after planting, there are small white frosts during the night, cover the Pumpkins with cloches, or, failing that, with litter. While they are growing, they should be watered abundantly, and other care consists in pinching the first stem above the second eye, to encourage the development of one or two branches on each. When they are about Im,50 in length, we marcotte them, which consists in laying the branches in the ground, so that they produce roots; in this way, we obtain much more vigorous vegetation. As soon as a fruit is knotted and deemed worthy of preservation, the branch bearing it should be pinched off two or three eyes above the fruit; and if you want to obtain large fruits, you should leave only one or two on each plant, except for the Dutch variety, whose fruits are smaller but of excellent quality; In fact, this is the variety best reserved for winter provisions: picked before the frosts and placed on the shelves of the fruit tree or orangery, it often keeps well into March and April. Marrow squash, full squash from Naples, Ohio, Valparaiso, Barbary, Madeira, Patagonia, Giraumont turban and Patisson, are grown in exactly the same way as Pumpkins. The germination period of pumpkin seeds is five years. Varieties: - large yellow, - Spanish, - Dutch, - white, - Corfu. FONTAINE CRESSON (Nasturtium officinale). - This plant, hitherto used in cooking only as a salad and supply, can also be prepared in the manner of spinach, and in this form has a very pleasant flavor. The consumption of watercress has become so considerable that, in a radius very close to Paris, large areas of land are devoted to this crop. These cressonnières are fed by natural or artificial springs, and arranged so as to be submerged at will. The land is divided into pits, each about 3 meters wide by Om, 40 or so deep, separated by raised beds for various types of crops, such as artichokes, cabbage and so on. Cress can be propagated from seeds sown in spring, or even better from cuttings taken in August. Before planting, the soil should be well consolidated, and if it is not sufficiently moist, a little water should be left to run off. Once the soil is well prepared, watercress is taken and placed at the bottom of the pits in small pinches, about Om, 12 to Om, 15 apart. After a short time, it has taken root and completely covers the soil; then a light layer of well-consumed cow manure is spread over the whole surface; by means of a board with an obliquely placed handle, the whole is slightly compressed; after which, Om, 10 to Om, 12 of water is introduced, a quantity quite sufficient for this crop. In summer, watercress is picked every two or three weeks. To make harvesting easier, a plank is laid across the pit. As soon as a pit has been harvested, we dry it out and spread a little cow manure over it, pressing it down with the above-mentioned instrument, an operation that must be repeated immediately after each cut. When a pit has been in production for a year, it is destroyed and replanted as described above, but only after removing the old roots and manure debris, which form a fairly considerable thickness at the bottom. It can also be sown or planted on the banks of watercourses, as is often the case in pleasure gardens. The stems spread quickly, and can be cut back every year until the frosts, provided this is done often enough to prevent them from going to seed. Watercress seeds germinate in four years. Cresson de terre, Cresson vivace (Erysimum prœcox). - It can be used in place of watercress, and has all the flavor of watercress. Sow in spring, in rays, in clean, light, moist soil. The germination period of watercress seeds is three years. Watercress (Lepidium sativum). - As this plant goes to seed very quickly, it is necessary to sow it very often; sowing is done in rays, in spring on a bed, and in summer in a shady spot. Cresson alénois seeds ripen in June, and keep for five years. Varieties. - Cressou alénois frisé. - Golden cress. ECHALOTE (Allium Ascalonicum). - Successfully grown only in light, substantial soil smoked from the previous year. It multiplies from cloves planted in February and March, at Om, 08 or Om, 10 apart, and almost flush with the ground, to avoid humidity, which is very harmful. For replanting, choose the thinnest and most elongated shallots, as they produce the most beautiful bulbs. Pull them up in July or August, when the leaves are dry, and leave them for two or three days in the sun, then store them in a dry place. SPINACH (Spinacia oleracea). - They are sown in rows or broadcast from March to the end of October, and as they remain in the ground for only a short time, they are often sown among newly transplanted plants or to line beds to be used for another crop a month or so later. Summer seedlings should be sown in a shady spot, and watered frequently to prevent them from bolting. Spinach seeds ripen in July, and keep for five years. Varieties. - Spinach from Holland, - England, - Flanders, - Esquermes, with lettuce leaves. ESTRAGON (Artemisia dracunculus). - It is propagated from seed, but more frequently by splintering plants and replanting them in spring in good exposure. Cut the stems at the onset of winter, and cover the clumps with a few centimetres of compost. FENOUH, (Anethum feniculum). - Several varieties are grown, but as a vegetable, sweet fennel is the most highly regarded. It is propagated from seeds taken from Italy every year; those harvested in our gardens degenerate quickly. Sow from March to June, either in the field or in a nursery, and transplant to Om, 40 cm apart; then hoe and water frequently during drought. When Fennel is strong enough, it is blanched in the same way as Celery. The roots and young shoots are eaten. Fennel seeds germinate for five years. BEANS (Faba vulgaris). - The first broad beans are sown in January, under panels (to sow at this time, early dwarf broad beans are preferred); in February, they are transplanted in slightly deep rays, which are traced at Om, 35 from each other; During bad weather, they are covered with litter, and when they are a few centimeters high, they are hoeed, then the rays are filled in, which increases the vigor of the plants and the produce; when they are deflowered, all the ends are pinched off, to force the sap to 135 bear towards the fruit. In February, we sow in the open ground, in clumps or in rays; from this time onwards, sowing can be continued successively until the end of May; finally, whatever the time of sowing, care consists in giving a few hoeings and pinching the ends of the stems, as previously indicated. Bean seeds germinate for six years. Varieties. - Early dwarf, - Julienne, - Marsh. - de Windsor, - evergreen, - violet, - purple flower, - long pod. STRAWBERRY (Fragaria vesca). - We propagate Strawberry plants from seed or from nets, which should only be taken from one-year-old plants; those from old clumps produce much less, and the fruit is less beautiful and of poorer quality. Sow in March, in a shady spot; cover the seeds with a light layer of fine soil mixed with compost, and keep the soil cool by watering. As soon as the plant has four or five leaves, it is transplanted, two by two, onto an old bed. Immediately after transplanting, we baste with an apple watering can, and continue to do so as needed. For a few days, we protect the young plants from the sun with a little litter, which we spread lightly. At the beginning of July, the clods are lifted and planted out in the ground, about Om, 15 apart, and, as after the first transplanting, the new growth is protected by frequent watering. The result of these transplantings is to encourage the development of a large number of young roots; and the more the Strawberry plants are furnished with them, the more productive they become. From this time onwards, until they are ready to be planted, we take care to remove all the flowers and netting that develop on the young plant, and to pull out those that appear to be degenerating, which can be easily recognized by their vigor and the absence of flowers. Towards the end of September, the beds in which the Strawberry plants are to be planted are given a good ploughing; and if the soil is not of good quality, only well-consumed fertilizers should be used to improve it: for when the Strawberry roots reach the unconsumed manure, the leaves dry up successively, and often the clumps perish. After preparing the soil well, draw five rows per bed, Om, 33 wide; then plant your Strawberry plants at Om, 35 apart on the line, which however should only be done for Four Seasons Strawberries, as for those with very large fruits, such as Keen's seedling Strawberry, draw four rows only, and plant at Om, 50 apart on the line; after which, we continue to cut the flowers and netting from each clump before they are rooted, in order to concentrate the reproductive power they are endowed with on each plant. In spring, we hoe each bed; as soon as the flowers begin to appear, we cover the soil with a slightly long mulch, which on the one hand has the advantage of conserving soil moisture, and on the other prevents the fruit from bearing on the soil. Watering should be done with apple watering cans, in the morning in spring and in the evening in summer. The following year, the same care is continued; but, as the produce degenerates after a few years, Strawberry plants should not be kept for more than two years. However, in good soil, they can be kept for three or four years, taking care to replant them each spring with new soil. Strawberry plants propagated from nets should be planted in July, and as what we have just said about Strawberry plants from seed is entirely applicable to the latter, we believe it unnecessary to deal with this subject at greater length. Forced strawberry plants. - Strawberries grown for forcing are: Fraisier des Quatre-Saisons, Keen's seedling, Princesse Royale, Marguerite Lebreton, Victoria Trolopp, Elton. In January or the first few days of February, boxes, then panels, are placed on the boards of the strawberry plants to be forced; the soil is removed from the paths surrounding the boxes to a depth of about Om.45, after which the paths are filled with manure, but only up to ground level, and in the first fortnight of February the filling is completed. From then on, care must be taken to maintain them to the height of the panels: for this, manure is brought in as and when it is needed. We cover the panels at night with straw mats, and give them air when the sun is shining. Towards the end of April, we start giving a few basins, if the temperature requires it, which we continue to do as needed. Strawberry plants treated in this way will begin to ripen in April. After harvesting, we remove the panels (which can still be used to cover the melons), which will not prevent the strawberry plants, especially those from Quatre-Saisons, from bearing fruit until the frosts. However, Keen's seedling and other large-fruited varieties can also be harvested a second time. To do this, they must be deprived of water for 8. some time, in order to stop their vegetation; and when the clumps are almost wilted, remove most of the leaves, hoe them lightly, then encourage their vegetation with good watering. In the first days of August, a second fruiting will take place, just as abundant as the first. Instead of destroying potted strawberry plants after harvest, as is usually done, you can remove the old roots and renew the soil in the pots in autumn. After this operation, treated like the young plants that are renewed each year, these strawberry plants produce fruit just as beautiful and abundant as the young plants. In Tours, at the home of Mr. Bellanger, a friend of ours, we have seen strawberry plants that have been subjected to this treatment for fifteen years, and whose vigor leaves nothing to be desired. Since the adoption of thermosiphon heating, the forced cultivation of strawberry plants has been modified. Thus, after having treated the strawberry plants as we have indicated, towards the end of September or the beginning of October, they are lifted in clods and planted in Om, 15 pots. For potting, we use good, soft soil, which has been raked, and immediately after planting, we place the pots next to each other in a trunk, so as to protect them from heavy rain and frosts, by placing frames or straw mats over them; then we water them to make it easier for them to recover, and, as for plants grown in the open ground, we remove the netting and flowers as they appear. In January, we prepare the boxes to receive the strawberry plants; then we place them on the ground, all next to each other, or on a tier under which we run the thermosiphon pipes. Once everything is in place, we hoe the soil in the pots, remove any dead leaves, and lay the panels, which we cover with straw matting overnight. At this point, we start heating them, which should be done only moderately and in such a way as to maintain a temperature of 12 to 15 degrees under the panels, and, as we indicated for Strawberry plants forced into the ground, we baste and give air whenever the temperature is favorable. In this way, you can have ripe fruit as early as the first days of March. Like those grown in the open ground, Strawberry plants forced into pots are likely to produce a second crop; all you need to do is remove the soil, plant them in the open ground and give them the care indicated above. Strawberry plants are divided into several sections, each containing a large number of varieties. We will only mention those most commonly grown. Strawberry seeds germinate for three years. Varieties: - Des Quatre-Saisons, - Ambroisia, - Amiral Dundas. - British Queen, - Eleonor Myatt, - Elton. - Jacunda, - Keen's sedling, - Lucas, - Marguerite Lebreton, - Napoléon III, - Princesse royale, - Sir Harry, - Victoria Trolopp, - Vicomtesse Héricart de Thury. BEANS (Phaseolus vulgaris). - The first Beans are sown in December, on beds and under panels; but, as at this time there is often a complete absence of sun, which is very unfavorable to this type of cultivation, it is preferable not to start this work until January, and from then on you can continue until the end of March. Sow on beds and under panels, and as soon as the cotyledons have developed, transplant the beans in the nursery, always preparing a layer about Om, 50 thick with a heat of 20 to 25 degrees; lay the boxes, load the layer with Om, 12 to Om, 15 of light soil, and plant the beans. Four rows are marked out per box, and the beans are planted at about Om, 15 on the line; after which, the care to be given consists in re-stoking from time to time, in order to maintain the necessary heat in the layer; covering the panels during the night; giving air whenever the temperature allows it; When the beans are about Om.25 in height, they are placed on top of the trunk, and held in this position by means of small wooden rods placed on the stems. A few days later, the ends of the stems are raised (the rods can then be removed), but the lower part remains lying on the ground. Treated in this way, the first beans are usually picked six weeks after sowing. It is often a mistake to destroy beans as soon as they have been harvested, because if you clean them carefully, removing dead leaves and any fruit found too small to be picked, after a while they will produce a second harvest as abundant as the first. Thermosiphon heating can also be used to grow beans under panels. All that's needed is to prepare a very thin layer, with the sole aim of protecting the beans from the dampness of the soil; then the pipes of the appliance are wired above the layer; the heat is maintained at 15 to 20 degrees under the panels; and as the heating can be regulated at will, the beans are uncovered every day, regardless of the state of the temperature, and air is given as often as necessary, which contributes greatly to the success of the operation. In April, we still sow on beds, but we transplant in the open ground and under cloches. Three beans are transplanted under each cloche; after a few days, air begins to circulate, and the cloches are removed when frosts are no longer to be feared and the temperature is favorable. It goes without saying that either cloches or panels can be used. Sow in the open ground in May; in light soil, sow in the first fortnight of the month; in heavy soil, only in the second fortnight, in clumps, or better still, in rays, as this produces much more vigorous vegetation, and consequently more abundant produce. Rays are traced about Om, 05 deep, Om, 40 apart, after which the beans are sown one by one, Om, 15 or Om, 20 apart on the line, then covered with about Om, 02 of soil. To sow in clumps, holes are made Om, 05 to Om, 06 deep, arranged in a checkerboard pattern, Om, 40 apart; five or six Beans are sown in each, then covered with the same amount of soil as those sown in rays. Some time later, we hoe to facilitate seed emergence; but it's only when the beans are well up that we finish filling the holes or combs. From the time indicated above, beans can be sown in the open ground until mid-August, to be eaten green (the beans grown particularly for this purpose are: Nain de Hollande, Flageolet and Bagnolet); but if you want to harvest dry, don't sow after May, except for a few early dwarf varieties, which can still be sown in the first fortnight of June. We still grow a large number of bean varieties, which can be divided into two categories. Bean seeds have a germination period of three years, and can be eaten green or shelled. Om, 30, - Belgian black, h. Om, 35, - Soissons dwarf, h. Om, 50 - Chinese, h. Om, 35, - Flageolet, h. Om,35, - Fl. yellow, h. Om, 40,- Swiss white, Om,45, - S. gray, h. Om45, - de Chartres, h. Im,40,- de Soissons, h. 2m, - Sabre, h. 2m, - Rice, h. Om,60. Beans without parchment or Mange-tout. - White dwarf, h. Om, 50, - Sabre dwarf, h. Om, 50, - marbled Prague dwarf, h. Om,45. - Princess dwarf, h. Om, 40, - Canadian yellow, h. Om, 40, - Butter dwarf, h. Om, 35, - Predomme, h. 1m,50, - Princess, h. 2m. - Butter 2m,50, - White butter, h. 2m. - Prague red, h. 2m,50, - marbled P., h. 2m, - P. bicolor, h. 2m,50, - Villetaneuse, h. 2m. CHINESE IGNAMUS (Dioscorea Batatas). - Introduced to France in 1848, this plant has stood the test of time, under which many new plants have succumbed. It increasingly justifies the hopes founded on the services it renders in its native country, and we can now say that it is worthy in every respect of first place on the list of our vegetable plants. The taste of the tuberous roots of the Chinese Yam differs little from that of the Potato; they are also rich in starch, and can, like the Potato, be used for all kinds of seasonings. The roots of the Chinese Yam grow every year, but only after having given birth to new roots, which grow from the collar of the plant. The Chinese Yam is propagated by planting, in March or April, with no more care than is required for the well understood cultivation of the Potato, either the bulbils which are born in the leaf axils, or the young roots produced by the bulbils, or finally the collar of the roots destined for consumption. Chinese yams are planted in rows, Om, 20 or O,, 25 apart, in all directions. In siliceous soils, which are better suited than all others to the cultivation of this plant, Chinese yams can be harvested the same year they are planted. Harvesting costs are not significantly higher than those for long carrots, for example. Nevertheless, to get the most out of this plant, the roots must be left in the ground for two years. From what we have been able to observe in our own cultivations, the root yield of Chinese Yam always far exceeds in the second year what the same area of land could have produced of Potatoes. The result is that, despite two years of cultivation and the cost of uprooting, this operation still offers definite advantages. Although the stems of Chinese yam are climbing, they don't need to be raked, and can be left to crawl along the ground. If they grow too much in the second year, they can be fed to cattle, which are happy to eat them as fresh fodder. Chinese yam is not very sensitive to the cold; in the climate of Paris, it survives ordinary winters very well in the open ground. However, it is advisable to uproot Chinese yams as soon as the stems are completely dry. Placed in the same conditions as potatoes, 3 Chinese yams can easily be kept for five or six months out of the ground. LATTUE (Lactuca sativa). - Two main breeds are grown: head lettuces (Lactuca capitata) and romaine lettuces (Lactuca romana). Lettuces. - These are divided into head, spring, summer, winter and cutting lettuces. Spring lettuces. - In the first fortnight of October, we sow the variety known as petite noire, on a south-facing bed; when the cotyledons are well developed and the first small leaves begin to appear, we place three rows of bells on the bed, and transplant around thirty plants under each one; then we raise these Lettuces without ever giving them air. But this is not the case for the other varieties, which are sown in the second half of the month: when the plant is well established, as can be seen when it begins to vegetate, we give it a little air by lifting the bells by about Om, 03 on the opposite side to the wind. After a few days, the amount of air is gradually increased, depending on the temperature and to strengthen the plant. The bells should only be lowered when it freezes at 2 or 3 degrees. When the frost becomes more severe, the bells are lined with dry manure, which is increased according to the intensity of the cold. The bells are uncovered as soon as the sun comes out, but first check that the plant is not suffering from frost, because then, instead of uncovering, you'd have to increase the cover and let it thaw gradually. In the first fortnight of November, we plant on beds (under panels or cloches), and from then on, we continue successively until the end of February. In March, we plant in the open ground, in good exposure, always with plants taken from the same bed. Towards the end of February or at the beginning of March, we sow on beds and under panels; and when the plant is strong enough, we transplant it into beds that we have carefully mulched before planting. Varieties. - Crepe or little black. - Gotte. - Georges. - Red-edged. - Bigotte. These varieties are also sown in August and September; they are planted on old beds, topped up with compost, so that they are close to the glass; but only when it freezes are they covered with panels, taking care to give them as much air as possible. They are often kept until December. Summer lettuces. - They are sown from the first days of April through to July, successively every two weeks, so as to have a succession of lettuces throughout the season. Care is the same as above, but because of the time of year, watering must be much more frequent. Varieties. - Blonde de Versailles. - Blonde d'été. - Berlin blond. - Batavia blonde. - Bl. brown. - Maltese. - Turkish. - Imperial. - Green of Genoa. - Big lazy brown - Palatine. - Chartreuse red. - Red with yellow seeds. - Bloody or flagellated. Winter lettuces. - Start sowing in August, and continue until mid-September. In October, they are planted in a bed with good exposure, and are protected from heavy frost and snow by covering with litter, which is removed whenever the weather permits. Varieties. - Passion. - Morine. - Winter brown. Cutting lettuces. - You can easily have them almost all year round, starting with bedding in January and February, then in the open ground from March successively until November. Varieties. - Pinch. - Chicory. - Spinach. Romaine lettuces. - Several varieties are grown which, like head lettuces, can be classified as spring, summer and winter Romaine. Spring Romaine. - In the first fortnight of October, we sow the early green Romaine, and in the second, the blond and grey Romaines maraîchère; we treat the plant like that of the head lettuces grown at that time; only, in the course of November, we raise the plant of the green Romaines to replant it immediately; but then we only place twelve or fifteen per bell. Towards the end of December or the beginning of January, we start planting under panels or cloches, and from then on we continue successively until the end of February; in March, we plant in the open ground, with good exposure. Summer Romaine. - They are grown in exactly the same way as summer lettuces, with the only difference being that varieties that do not cover themselves are tied with one or two straw ties to make them blanch. This operation should only be carried out when the weather is dry, and the leaves should not be allowed to get wet when watering. Varieties. - Alphange, - blonde de Brunoy, - Monstrueuse. - Panachée. - Golden. - Oak-leaved. Winter Romaine. - They are treated like winter Lettuces, and when sowing at this time of year, preference is generally given to the red winter Romaine, a very hardy variety that withstands frost the best. Lettuce and Romaine seeds ripen in August, and keep well for five years. LENTILLES (Ervum lens). - This plant is grown more in open fields than in gardens; it is weeded into rays in March and April. In dry, sandy soil, it produces much more than in greasy, damp soil, because it vegetates a lot, but produces very few seeds. The Gallardon variety is generally preferred. Lentil seeds germinate in three years. HOLLAND MACHE (Valerianella olitoria). - It likes soft, well-smoked soil. Sowing begins in August and continues until the end of October. It is sown broadcast, and after harrowing with a fork, lightly covered with a rake, and watered if the weather is dry. If sown in October, it will do well in spring. There is a new variety of Lamb's lettuce with variegated leaves, which can be grown in exactly the same way. Italian or Regency Lamb's lettuce. - This is a broader-leafed but later variety, often sown among the Dutch variety to extend the sowing period. Lamb's lettuce seeds are good for harvesting in June, and keep for four or five years. Corn (Zea maïs). - In the garden, Corn is particularly cultivated for its young ears, which can be pickled in vinegar like gherkins, or eaten boiled like potatoes. We sow it in May in the open ground, or in April on a bed, and then transplant it Om, 60 cm apart. As the plants gain strength, they are ridged, and the buds at the base are cut off. Corn seeds germinate in two years. Varieties. - Early white. - Quarantain, - for chickens. - Sweet. MELONS (Cucumis Melo). - The cultivation of melons, on which many treatises have already appeared, rarely written by practitioners, could not be easier; however, in our countries it requires assiduous and intelligent care, especially for those in the first season: Good cultivation has such an influence on the quality of the fruit that, despite being grown in far less favorable conditions, our Cantaloupe melons are usually superior in quality to those grown in southern regions, where they are sown in open fields and left to their own devices. They can be grown in three ways: under panels, under cloches or in the open ground. Panel-grown melons. - Only Cantaloup Prescott fond blanc and its varieties are grown under panels. The first melons are sown in the early days of January, but in ordinary cultivation, they are only sown in the early days of February. A layer about Om, 75 thick is prepared, made up of half new manure, half annealed manure. This is filled with Om, 10 of potting soil, so that the seedling is not far from the glass. Surround the trunk with a good manure stove, and when the heat of the layer is favorable (25 to 30 degrees), trace out rays, sow seeds, which are lightly covered; keep the panels covered with straw mats for two or three days, until the seeds have risen; then uncover every day, taking care to cover before dark. A few days after the seeds have emerged, we start to give a little air through the top of the panels whenever the weather permits, in order to strengthen the plant. When the cotyledons are well developed, we prepare another layer, of the same thickness as the previous one, but whose length should be proportional to the number of plants we want to transplant; then we load it with potting soil. The boxes are placed, the potting soil spread evenly, and when the layer is warm enough, the most vigorous seedling is chosen and transplanted with the finger, as you would with a planter. We usually make ten rows per trunk, and transplant our Melons at Om, 12 apart on the line, taking care to press them down to the cotyledons; or we press Om, 08 diameter pots onto the bed; These are filled with good soft soil mixed with loam, lightly treaded, and when the heat is favorable, a melon plant is transplanted into each pot (1); in this case as in the other, the panels are kept covered with straw mats for three or four days, to make it easier for the plant to grow. (1) These two transplanting methods are still used in market gardening. However, many market gardeners have abandoned pot transplanting, as experience has shown that a melon whose roots are twisted (which necessarily happens to pot-transplanted plants if planting is delayed by a few days) should vegetate less vigorously than one transplanted on a bed, and whose roots have not been hindered in their development. the plant's recovery, after which it is uncovered every day and given a little air when the sun comes out. First pruning. - When the primitive stem has three or four leaves, it is cut above the second leaf (fig. Il); then the cotyledons are removed, lest the humidity rot these organs and spoil the stem. In the second half of February, layers of Om, 60 thick and Im, 33 wide, made up of new manure, tree leaves and grape marc; or new manure and a third of manure from old layers, are prepared. We load them with about Om, 15 of good vegetable garden soil mixed with loam; we place the trunks, and after spreading the soil well in the trunks, we place the panels, fill the paths halfway, and, when the layer has thrown its first fire, we plant two feet of Melon under each panel. Before planting, we make a row of holes in the middle of the bed; then, if we've transplanted onto the bed, we lift the plants with a good clod, and plant a melon plant in each hole, taking care to press it in up to the first leaves. If you've transplanted into a pot, carefully remove the plant from the pot. To do this, take the pot with your right hand, place your left hand on the surface of the soil, so that the stem is between two fingers. Turn the pot upside down, tap lightly on the edge of the trunk, and when the rootball is out of the pot, plant the melon as described. Immediately after planting, water the root ball a little; when the sun is shining, shade the panels with a little bedding, and for a few days refrain from letting in air. A few days after planting, we surround the trunks with a good manure stove, and finish filling in the paths. During the night and in bad weather, we cover the panels with mats, then give air whenever the temperature permits. Second pruning. - The first pruning, i.e. the pinching of the primitive stem, having determined the development of two lateral branches, we direct one towards the top of the trunk and the other towards the bottom; and when they Fig. 12. - Melon, second pruning. are about Om, 33 in length, we prune them above the third or fourth leaf (fig. 12), depending on the vigor of the plants. At this point, and before any new branches develop, spread a good mulch of half-consumed manure over the entire layer. Third pruning. - The second pruning determines the development of three or four branches on each side branch. While they are growing, they are directed so that they do not cross each other; and when they are about Om, 33 in length, they are pruned above the third leaf (fig. 13), without taking into account the flowers, which are removed, because the first flowers of the melon are usually male flowers, called false flowers; if by chance there are some female flowers called meshes, we also remove the branches where they are, because then the plants are not yet strong enough, and the fruits would be much inferior to those obtained later. After the third pruning, we carefully monitor the development of the new branches; and when we have young knotted fruits, we choose the best one; we pinch the branch that bears it two eyes above the fruit, which we secure with the surrounding leaves, so as to& E Fig. 13. - Melon, third pruning, so that it is not affected by the direct rays of the sun, which would harden it; then immediately remove all the other fruit on each plant, to encourage the development of the one left, and pinch off all the other branches above the second leaf (fig. 13). As it sometimes happens that the young fruit does not have a regular shape, or that it elongates too much, in this case it is removed, and another mesh is chosen. Finally, when the fruit has just about reached its full size, if the plants are vigorous, a second fruit is selected from among the newly-knotted ones on each plant, but always under the same conditions as for the first, after which all the others are removed, making one or two melons on each plant. Other care is limited to cutting off all new branches above their first leaves, and removing the tips of any branches that may have grown out of the trunk. For all operations requiring removal of the panels, choose the time of day when the temperature is mildest, so that the cold does not seize the Melons, which are excessively tender. When watering becomes necessary, use the apple watering can; but at this time, the water used must be at the same temperature as the atmosphere in which it is poured, so as not to delay vegetation. If melons are growing very vigorously, it's a good idea not to water them, or to give them very little water, until they have knotted fruit, because the more vigorous they are, the less likely they are to bear fruit. Every day, when the sun is shining, give the panels some air, taking care to lift them away from the wind. As far as possible, you shouldn't get them used to being shaded; it's better to give them more air as the sun gets stronger. Indeed, once you've started, you need to keep going, and with great precision, because often it only takes a ray of sunlight to burn the leaves. We continue to cover the panels every night; and from the time of planting, we must maintain the stoves at the height of the panels and reshape them every month or so, adding at least half as much new manure each time, in order to maintain the warmth of the layer; but we must not reshape the stoves to their full depth once the Melons are growing vigorously, as they have roots that crawl almost to the surface of the soil; and as they develop rapidly9. This is why it's important to refrain from any operation that might stop their development. With this treatment, first-season fruits begin to ripen in the first fortnight of April, and those sown in February produce in May (1). Early melons are among the plants that benefit from thermosiphon heating, as one of the most unfavorable conditions for this crop is the absence of sunshine, which often occurs in January and February; and since, despite the rigorous temperature, it is necessary to baste the melons, because of the heat of the layer, it often happens that the atmosphere of the frame is loaded with moisture and that numerous droplets of water form on all the interior surface of the panels; now, if the temperature does not make it possible to give air, this excess of moisture causes the flowers to drop. This is where the beneficial effect of the thermosiphon can be appreciated. As you can adjust this heating at will, you (1) A fact that's quite important to know is the precise point at which melons ripen. On this subject, we'd like to say that it's not always essential to wait for a melon to reach full ripeness before harvesting it; it's enough for it to be struck, i.e. to begin to change color or hue; when it has reached this point, it can be picked and placed in a cool place, where it will finish ripening without losing any of its quality; in this way, the harvest can easily be prolonged. Although it's not always easy to tell when a melon is ripe, we'd say that it's ready to be eaten when it takes on a yellow color, which becomes quite intense in light-colored varieties; when the tail becomes encircled at its point of insertion, as if it were about to detach; and finally when the fruit gives off a pleasant odor, and when, by gently squeezing the umbilicus (the point opposite the tail), you feel it flex under your finger. Thin-peeled varieties are the easiest to distinguish, while Cantaloupes are more uncertain. can give air whenever it's needed. In this way, care is exactly the same as above, except that the layer is much thinner, and the pipes of the machine are run underneath. A second season of melons is sown in the second half of February. At the time when these melons are ready to be planted, the temperature starts to become more favorable, so the layers are not as thick, and the reheaters do not need to be repeated as often. A fortnight after transplanting, we choose a site with good exposure to the south, but where we haven't grown melons the previous year; for this crop to be a complete success, we mustn't plant two years in a row on the same plot. A first trench is made, one metre wide and Om, 33 deep; the soil is deposited at the end of the square, i.e. where the last trench is to be made; then a good layer is prepared, about Om, 66 thick, made up, as for first-season Melons, of manure, leaves or grape marc. Then open a trench Om, 66 from the first, and with soil, if it's not too compact, load the Om, 15 layer; make a layer in the second, and so on to the end of the square, where you'll find the soil from the first trench to load the last layer. After that, we plow the paths, place the chests, spread the soil inside the chests, lay the panels, then surround the chests with a good manure stove, and fill the paths. When the layer is warm enough, plant two melon plants under each panel and give them the same care as first-season melons. Melon under cloches. - For planting under cloches, you can still sow Prescott cantaloupe melons, but many gardeners prefer embroidered melons, which fruit much better. Towards the end of March or the beginning of April, sow in beds and under panels, taking care to observe all that has been indicated for the education of the first-season plant. Some time before planting, we make a trench Om, 65 wide by Om, 40 deep, then prepare a layer about Om, 75 thick. This is lightly crowned in the middle, and covered with a bed of good soil mixed with loam. When the layer is warm enough, plant the melons in a row, Om, 66 apart. Immediately after planting, each Melon is covered with a bell, which is wrapped in litter for two or three days, to encourage the young plant to take root; during the night, the bells are covered with straw mats. As soon as the melons start to vegetate, the cloches are lifted during the day to give them a little air, then gradually increased until the time comes to remove them, which takes place when they can no longer hold the branches, but which should only be done in fine weather, as it would be better to delay this operation than to remove them in wet weather. From the above-mentioned time to Midsummer's Day (June 20-25), you can successively plant several seasons of Melons sous cloches. Plant training, pruning and other care are all the same as for melons grown under panels. Melons grown on mounds. - We will now describe a simple and inexpensive method recently indicated by M. Loisel. In May, cone-shaped mounds of half-consumed manure, leaves or moss are raised on the ground. They should be about Om 50 or Om 60 in diameter at the base, Om 60 in height, and set about I meter apart. The manures should be prepared in the same way as for the other layers, i.e. they should be mixed well, and wetted if too dry; then, as they are used, they should be trodden down so that the mounds are compacted as little as possible; and, whatever the nature of the substances used to build these mounds, they should be covered with about Om, 15 of good soil, prepared as previously indicated. At the top of each mound, make a small hole of about Om, 10 and sow three or four seeds in each one, leaving only the two most vigorous plants, or, even better, plant them high up. In both cases, they should be immediately covered with a bell, which should be removed when it can no longer contain the branches. For the care to be given and the first pruning, we refer you to what was said about Melons under frame. Before removing the bells, lightly hoe the mounds, taking care to preserve their rounded shape, as well as the surrounding soil, then cover them completely with a mulch of manure, which can be spread about 1 m around. This cover has the advantage of keeping the watering fresh. Visit the mounds from time to time. The care to be given consists in pulling down any branches that are going in the wrong direction, pulling out weeds as they appear, and when the branches have reached about the middle of the mound, pinching off the ends. This operation will give rise to new branches, which will soon be loaded with flowers and fruit; and as they quickly reach the bottom of the mound, all the ends should be cut off one last time, when they begin to crawl along the ground. Once you've reached this point, all you need to do is water them as needed, and place a tile or a piece of plank under each fruit when it's about half its size. In addition to saving money, these mounds offer the advantage of being more easily penetrated by the sun's rays, which is an important point in crops of this kind. On the other hand, the inclination of the branches is so favorable to fruiting, that each mound can easily produce 10 or 12 good Melons in the course of the summer; the first ones usually begin to ripen in the second half of July, and continue to give fruit until September. Melons are divided into three breeds, whose best varieties we will describe below: Melons brodés. - Maraîcher. - Sucrin de Tour. - S. with white flesh. - Ananas d'Amérique, - d'Arkangel, - de Grammont. - de Honfleur, - de Cavaillon. Cantaloupe melons. - Orange. - Noir des Carmes, - de VingtHuit Jours. - Prescott white background, - Pr. grey background. - Galeux green background, - de Portugal. - Holland black. Smooth-peel melons. - Maltese, - M. red-fleshed. - Winter, - Persian (1). Watermelons (Cucurbita Citrullus). - They are grown in the same way as bell melons, with the difference that (1) These melons, stored in a dry place, can be kept unaltered until January, and sometimes longer. The germination period of melon seeds is five years. NAVET (Brassio Napus). - Turnips do well in all types of soil, but prefer soft, sandy soil; only in this type of soil do they acquire good quality. They are broadcast sown from May to the beginning of September, whenever possible in wet weather. When the plant is strong enough, it is lighted more or less, depending on the size of the variety. Those intended for winter consumption should be sown in July and August. As cold weather approaches, we pull them up and cut off the tops, so that they don't grow back; then we gauge them and cover them with straw during the frosts; in this way, we can keep them until April. A large number of varieties are grown, divided into tender, semi-tender and dry turnips; for the first and last sowings, it's the tender ones that should be chosen, as they are the earliest. Turnip seeds ripen in June, and keep well for five years. Tender turnips. - Des Vertus. - Early flat white. - Early flat red. - Snowball. Semi-tender turnips. - Holland yellow. - Scottish yellow. - Malta yellow. - J. of Finland. - Rose du Palatinat. - Rond de Croissy. - Gris de Morigny. Turnip sces. - De Freneuse, - de Meaux, - de Berlin petit. - Long yellow. ONION (Allium cepa). - Onions like soft, substantial soil, smoked from the previous year; they are broadcast in the second half of January, if the weather is favorable, or even better in February and March in strong soils. After sowing, the ground is harrowed and trodden; in light soils, the soil should even be firmed up before sowing; then the seeds are raked and covered with a light layer of compost. If the weather is dry, water from time to time, to encourage germination; then, when the seeds have sprouted, thin out where the plant is too thick, and transplant in places where it is lacking. During their growth, onions require no care other than hoeing and watering, which should be stopped as soon as they begin to turn. Quite often, when they have reached their full size, the tops are cut off with the back of a rake, to stop the flow of sap to the onion. Onions are harvested towards the end of August or the beginning of September; after being pulled, they are left on the ground for two weeks to ripen, after which they are placed in an attic. If you take care to spread them out and remove anything that could cause rot, you can keep them until the end of May. Varieties. - Dark red. - Pale red. - Vertus yellow. - Spanish soufflé. - Pyriform, - Madeira. They can also be sown in August: in this way, onions are even produced two months earlier, but many often go to seed in spring. White onion. - Sow in nurseries and broadcast in the first fortnight of August, for transplanting in October, and towards the end of the same month for transplanting in March. In October in light soils, and in March in heavy soils, we prepare the ground for planting the white onion. Mark out 10 or 12 rows per bed, Im, 33 wide, and transplant the onions Om, 10 apart along the line. In harsh winters, it's a good idea to cover the plant with bedding. White onions can be harvested towards the end of April or the beginning of May. If, due to unforeseen circumstances, there is a shortage of plants or the quantity is insufficient, they can be sown in January or February on beds and under panels; they can also be sown in the open ground in February and March; these onions will produce much earlier than other varieties sown at the same time. Varieties. - Early white onion. - Large white onion, Egyptian onion or Rocambole. - It differs from the others in that it bears bulblets on its stem, which serve to multiply it. They are planted in March, at Om, 12 from each other, and each of these bulblets produces a good, large onion, which is pulled off when the leaves turn yellow; they are placed in a very dry place to be used for consumption, taking care to reserve the necessary number for planting, which must take place in the following March.
