Each of these onions grows up the stem, producing bulblets which are kept for replanting at the time indicated above. Onion Potato. - It is planted in February or earlier, weather permitting, at Om, 30 or Om, 40 apart; during its vegetation, it is ridged several times, to encourage the development of the bulbs which grow around the mother Onion. Onion seeds are harvested in August, and keep for two years. GARLIC (Rumex acetosa). - This plant is propagated in the spring by splitting plants or sowing seeds, which are sown in rays from March to July. After sowing, cover the seeds with a light layer of potting soil, and water frequently: it's usually used as a border plant, but to make sure you don't run out of it in summer, it should also be grown in beds, with copious watering during drought. The last crop is harvested towards the end of October, after which it is hoeed and a good mulch of half-consumed manure is spread over each bed, or, at the same time, the sorrel clumps are lifted to make them more manageable for winter heating. For this purpose, prepare a layer of Om, 35 to Om, 40 thick, with a heat of 10 to 12 degrees; place the boxes, and load the layer of Om, 15 to Om, 20 with potting soil; then plant 10 to 12 rows of sorrel per box. During frosts, we cover the panels with straw mats; we give air as often as possible. You can also force sorrel into place. To do this, place boxes on the boards, then panels. The paths around the chests are dug out, and a dung stove is raised and remodeled from time to time. We start heating sorrel towards the end of November or the beginning of December, and can continue successively until the end of February. Sorrel seeds ripen in July, and keep for two years. Varieties. - Vierge, - de Belleville, - de Frévent. Sorrel Spinach. - Garden Patience, Rumex Patientia, is grown under this name. This plant has large, elongated leaves; its flavor is milder than that of sorrel, and it multiplies easily, either from seeds sown in spring, in the field or in a nursery, to be transplanted, or by sprouting. Place them about 1 m apart. Patience seeds germinate for three years. OXALIS CRENELÉE (Oxalis crenata). - This plant is very easy to propagate and grow; it produces a large number of small tubers, but the flavor is generally not very appealing. The leaves and tops of the shoots can be eaten like spinach. It is propagated by tubers or cuttings, which are planted in April and May, 1 metre apart; as soon as they have grown about Om, 12, we begin to: butter them in the center, to force each shoot to take a horizontal direction; then, as they lengthen, we successively load them with soil until September, when the tubers begin to form. As frost approaches, a layer of manure or leaves is spread on the ground, so that harvesting doesn't take place until as late as possible, as the tubers grow until quite late in the season. PANAIS (Pastinaca sativa). - They need deep, substantial soil. They can be grown in the same way as carrots, but they need to be thinned out more, as the tops are much wider. They can be left in the ground over winter, as they have no fear of frost. Parsnips ripen towards the end of August and are good for only one year. Varieties. - Long parsnip. - Round parsnip. SWEET PATATO (Convolvulus Batatas). - Sweet potatoes are grown on beds and under frames, or on bare beds and in the open ground. They are propagated from seed, which is sown in March on a bed and under a frame; but as they are rarely harvested, they are most often propagated in the following way: in the early days of January, a few of the best-preserved tubers are selected; they are placed on a warm bed and covered with a frame, over which straw mats are spread overnight; shortly afterwards, they begin to grow. They are then covered with Om, 05 or Om, 06 light soil, and at planting time, the seedling is detached from the mother tuber and planted immediately in place; or, if you want to have seedlings that are easier to take up, the young shoots are removed as they reach Om, 06 or Om, 08 in length, and transplanted into pots of around Om, 06 which are buried on a bed; When the cuttings have taken root, which happens fairly quickly, we start to lift the bell a little, and gradually increase the pressure to remove it completely when they can stand the air without wilting. 1. Potatoes on beds and under frames. - In the first fortnight of February, prepare a layer of Om, 60 to Om, 70 thick, half manure and half leaves. The height of the layer should be calculated so that after it has been loaded with about Om, 25 of good soil mixed with loam, the whole is no more than Om, 10 from the glass. After placing the boxes, the panels are laid, and when the warmth of the layer is favorable, the Potatoes are planted in two rows and Om,60 apart on the line. When planting them, care must be taken to spread the roots well; for if they were contorted, this would essentially harm tuber production. During the night, the panels are covered with straw mats, then the stoves are rearranged from time to time, to maintain the heat of the layer; the soil is basked if necessary, and air is given whenever the weather permits. As the tubers grow, they often emerge from the ground, and care must be taken to cover them with a few centimetres of earth. Potatoes treated in this way can be harvested in May or June at the latest; the larger ones are removed, and if the roots are carefully covered, they will continue to vegetate until autumn. Watering should be suspended in September, so as not to prolong vegetation, which would be essentially detrimental to tuber maturity. 2. Potatoes on a deaf layer. - In the course of April, prepare a deaf layer about 1 m wide and Om, 50 thick; cover with Om, 20 to Om, 25 of good, light, substantial soil, and towards the end of April or beginning of May, plant the potatoes in a row and Om, 65 apart; After a few days, you begin to give them air, lifting the bells during the day, and removing them when they can no longer hold the branches. During vegetation, care is limited to watering whenever necessary. Towards the end of August or the beginning of September, tubers are ready to be eaten, but it's only in October that the harvest is complete. This must be done with great care, as potatoes that are broken or crumpled rot quickly. 3. Potatoes in the ground. - In May, make holes from Om,60 to Om, 60, Om, 50 wide and Om, 35 to Om, 40 deep; fill the bottom with manure, cover with about Om, 20 of light, substantial soil, and plant three Potatoes in each hole, arranging them so that they are about Om, 8 apart. Water, cover with a bell, and shade as needed. In light, healthy soils, in the Paris climate, potatoes can be grown simply in the ground, on mounds 0.80 m high, or on planks about 1 m wide, with a ridge in the middle, on the crest of which a row of potatoes is planted. After planting, they are protected from the sun's scorching rays until they recover perfectly, after which all care consists of watering them as needed. 4. Preserving potatoes. - The simplest way to preserve potatoes is to store them in a dry place where the temperature, being as even as possible, does not fall below 12 degrees. In this way, potatoes can easily be kept unaltered until February and March. Potatoes can also be preserved in situ, as M. Souchet, gardener at the Château de Fontainebleau, does, by covering them with glass panels and doormats, so as to prevent the cold from penetrating under the panels. Varieties. - White - Yellow - Yellow - Malaga pink - Violet. PERCE-PIERRE. Sea fennel (Crithmum maritimum). - We sow it as soon as the seeds are ripe or in March, on a bed, to transplant it at the foot of a wall, facing south or east; we cover it with litter during the winter, because it is delicate and sensitive to frosts. Perce-pierre seed retains its germinative quality for only one year. PARSLEY (Petroselinum sativum). - It is sown in rays from February to May and June, and to avoid running out in winter, it should be covered with leaves or litter as soon as the frosts approach. Finally, in January or February, you can sow on the ground, but under panels; in this way, you'll have young Parsley in the second half of March. Parsley seeds ripen in August, and keep for three years. Varieties. - Curly. - Very curly dwarf with large root. PEPPER (Capsicum annuum). - Sow in a bed in February or March, in April in an earthen bed, and plant out at the end of April or beginning of May in the open ground, in good exposure. Pimento seeds germinate for four years. Varieties: -Long,-round,-Chile,-Spanish sweet,-Tomato. PRIPRENELLE (PETITE) (Poterium Sanquisora). - Sown in spring or autumn, it is usually used for borders, which are extremely hardy. Pimprenelle seeds ripen in September, and keep for two years. DANDELION. Lion's tooth (Taraxacum dens leonis). - This plant is not widely cultivated, as it is found in abundance in meadows; however, when dandelions are sown in spring, they are more beautiful and of better quality, especially if the seeds are harvested from the individuals with the broadest leaves. In addition to the salad they produce towards the end of winter, they can be blanched in autumn. Simply cover them with Om, 12 to Om, 15 of well-consumed compost, and as soon as they begin to break through the layer of compost, cut them off at the root collar. Dandelion seeds germinate for two years. PEAR (Allium Porrum). - Leeks are sown at the end of December or beginning of January, on beds and under panels; at the end of February or beginning of March, the plant is transplanted into the ground and broadcast; at the end of April, i.e. when the plant is strong enough, eight or ten rows are laid out in beds 1m, 33 wide, and the leek is transplanted at Om, 15 apart on the line. We pull out the plant needed for planting by thinning out the seedling, and we start to eat the plants left in place, which gives the new ones time to form. It can also be sown in July and transplanted at the beginning of September; then, in the second half of September, a final sowing is carried out, and, as always, the seed is broadcast, but very lightly, as it is not then transplanted. This leek is good for harvesting in June. Leek seeds are good for harvesting in August, and keep for only two years. Varieties. - Short, - long, - monstrous from Rouen, - yellow from Poitou. BLONDE PEAR (Beta vulgaris). - It is dried in rays, from May to August; and to keep the leaves 169 tender, they must be cut often and watered frequently during the drought. To make sure you don't run out of leaves in winter, as soon as the frosts are approaching, you can lift the roots and plant them on a bed; or you can place boxes and panels on boards laid out for this purpose. Lift the soil from the paths, then surround the boxes with a stove made of old manure, and give them air as often as possible. Poirée blonde can be eaten as spinach if required. Poirée à cardes. - Sow in nurseries in May and June. When the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted immediately. Three rows per bed, 1m 33 wide, are planted out at Om, 50 apart along the line. During drought, water abundantly to ensure large, tender chard. During frosts, we cover them with litter, and only in spring do we start to harvest them. Poirée seeds ripen in September, and keep well for five or six years. Varieties. - White chard. - Red chard. - Yellow chard. - From Chile. The most remarkable of all chard pears for the development and rich coloration of its ribs. PEAS (Pisum sativum). - At the beginning of November, we sow the first Peas on the ground, but under panels, to transplant the plant also under panels. In the course of December, the boxes for planting are placed, and a good spade-head is removed from each one, so as to have Om, 45 to Om, 50 deep under the panels; the soil is deposited in the paths, which serve to support the boxes; after which the ground is levelled, the rake passed, and four rays about Om,08 deep are traced in 10 each trunk, taking care to space them equally, but in such a way as to leave more space towards the bottom of the trunk, which is naturally the wettest part. Once the site has been prepared, and as soon as the plant is Om, 08 or Om, 10 high, it is lifted, so as not to break the roots by pulling it out, then it is transplanted by 3 or 4 together and about Om 20 apart on the line. When the Peas are Om, 20 to Om25 high, all the stems are laid down towards the top of the trunk; and to keep them in this position, they are covered with a little earth. A few days later, the ends of the stems rise and continue to grow; they soon flower; then we pinch all the stems above the third or fourth flower, to make them bear fruit more quickly. During the night, we cover the panels with straw mats; we give air whenever the temperature allows it, and we make some basins, which must take place with a lot of care, in order not to determine a too vigorous vegetation, which would essentially harm the harvest. When you have a good coastline and are short of panels, you can sow peas under cloches towards the end of January and during February; then, depending on the temperature, you transplant them into slightly deep rays; then you cover them with litter during bad weather. These peas bear fruit after those grown under panels, but much earlier than Peas.

sown in November and December. In-ground. - In the open ground, the first seedlings are sown in the second half of November, in a coastal location in the south of France. Rays are planted slightly deep and at Om, 25 from each other. After sowing, cover the peas with a few centimeters of compost, and when they are Om, 15 or Om, 20 high, hoe them and fill in the rays. If the winter is harsh, we cover the peas with litter, which we remove as soon as the temperature permits; but we must first check whether the peas have suffered from frost, because then, to avoid losing them, we must let them thaw gradually and uncover them only if the weather becomes milder. From the above-mentioned time, you can sow successively until July to eat green; to harvest dry, sow in March. Whatever the time of sowing, care consists of a few hoeings, pinching the tips of early species above the third or fourth flower to hasten maturity, and putting oars on the larger varieties. A large number of varieties of Pea are grown; we will only mention the most widespread, which we will place in their order of earliness; however, we will say that for sowings that will take place in the open ground before February, the ordinary Michaux should be used, as the earliest Pea is less hardy and could suffer from the winter; but the latter, sown in February, produces just as early as the Michaux sown in autumn. Pea seeds germinate for four to five years. Peas for shelling. - Carter, h. Om, 80 - Prince Albert, h. Om,45. - Early dwarf wrinkled, h. Om, 25 - Michaux de Hollande, h.
