POTATOES (Solanum tuberosum). - Only varieties that are not widely grown, or that are recommended for their qualities and ripening time, are usually cultivated in gardens, as the others belong mainly to field cultivation. Potatoes can be planted for the first time, on beds and under panels, towards the end of January or the beginning of February. To this end, prepare a layer Om, 40 thick, surround it with a stove, then load it with Om, 20 of good soil; mark out four rows per trunk, after which the potatoes are planted Om, 33 apart on the line (the variety known as Marjolin is the most advantageous for planting at this time); during the night, cover the panels with straw mats, and give air as often as possible. In this way, you can have new potatoes in the first fortnight of March. The larger ones are removed and the others covered, so that they can be harvested some time later. Full soil. - The first potatoes are planted in the open ground in February. The land to be planted must be ploughed and smoked in advance. If smoking is necessary during planting, only half-used fertilizers should be used. Potatoes are usually planted in holes similar to those in which beans are sown, only deeper and closer together than for beans. In damp, cold soils, instead of making holes for planting Potatoes, the seed is laid out in rows on the ground, then a trench is made between each row, and the soil from the trench is used to cover the Potatoes. Whatever the method of cultivation, the seed must be chosen with care, i.e. the tubers must be healthy and perfectly ripe. Larger tubers can be divided. To bring the harvest forward, potatoes can be germinated before planting, as many growers around Paris do, by placing them in a room in the house. During the summer, potatoes should be hoeed several times, then ridged when they have reached the middle stage of their development. This operation, considered unnecessary by some and recommended by others, is as necessary in light soils as it can be harmful in wet ones. In recent years, trials of autumn planting have been attempted with the aim of harvesting potatoes much earlier. Despite all the care taken in this operation, we have so far found no appreciable difference in the results of this crop, compared with those obtained by planting in spring, and, in our opinion at least, it would be preferable, rather than planting in autumn, to look for early varieties that can be harvested before the time when potato disease begins to run rife. Early Potatoes planted in February are harvested in the first fortnight of June; and, as we have said with regard to bed-grown plants, only the largest are detached, and the roots are carefully covered, in order to prolong the harvest. From the 10. time indicated above, successive plantings can be made until the end of June. Varieties. - Round yellows, early dwarf, Schaw, Cordilleran regent. - Long yellows. Marjolin, lapston kidney, second-season marjolin. - Round reds. August truffle, Strasbourg truffle, Montreuil truffle. - Long red. Grows upright. Vitelotte. Xavier. - Violets. Plate hâtive, - de Vincennes. - Smith secdling. POURPIER (Portulaca oleracea). - It is sown on a bed in February and March, and in the open ground in May and throughout the summer; the seeds should hardly be covered, but they should be assiduously drenched until they have sprouted. Purslane seeds germinate for eight years. Varieties. - Green purslane. - Golden purslane. RADISH (Raphanus sativue). - The first seedlings are sown around September 15, on teens. If it gets cold at night, cover with straw mats. In December, we sow on beds and under panels; in February, we sow again on beds, but in the open air; then in March, we start sowing in the open ground, which can continue until autumn. As a result, they are usually only sown among other plants, both on beds and in the ground. Summer sowings should, as far as possible, be made in the shade and watered in the breeze, and all varieties can be sown indiscriminately at this time of year; but early or half-long pink radishes are preferred for bedding. Varieties. - Early white from Holland. - Early violet. - Early round pink. - Half-long pink. - Half-long rose with white tip. - Summer gray. - Yellow or red. RAVES. - Cultivated in exactly the same way as pink radishes. Winter radish, horseradish. - They are broadcast from June 1 to July 10; as they are almost always sown too thickly, the plant must be thinned out. These radishes can be stored all winter long by placing them in gauges and covering them during frosts, or by placing them in the vegetable greenhouse. Varieties. - Black, - Chinese rose, - Gournay violet. Snake radish, Rat-tail radish (Raphanus caudatus). - Sown in June, July and August, I meter apart in all directions, these Radishes develop rapidly and produce a prodigious quantity of siliques that can be eaten like ordinary Radishes, with exactly the same flavor. Radish seeds are good for harvesting in August, and can be stored for up to five years. RAIFORT SAUVAGE (Cranson, Cochlearia armoracia). - The roots of this plant have an extremely pungent flavor; when grated, they can replace Mustard. It is propagated from root sections in the autumn, but it is not until the third year that the roots are large enough to be used. Rapunzel (Campanula rapunculus). - Rapunzel is broadcast in June and July. As the seed is extremely fine, it must be mixed with sand or fine, very dry soil, otherwise the sowing will be uneven or too thick. The seed is not covered, but simply raked over and lightly trodden, after which a small amount of bedding is spread over the whole, which is removed as soon as the seeds have sprouted, and the germination of the seeds is encouraged by frequent watering; a small amount of spinach or radish is usually sown among the Rapunzel to protect the young plant. We don't start harvesting Rapunzel until February, and harvesting can continue until it goes to seed. Rapunzel seeds ripen in July and August, and keep well for up to five years. RHUBARBE (Rheum). - Rhubarb is grown in gardens for its leaf stalks, which are used to make excellent jams, or to replace the fruit sometimes used in pastries. It can be propagated by sowing seeds immediately after maturity, or even better, by separating the plants and dividing them in spring, taking care that each shoot has at least one reproductive germ. Whatever the method of propagation, they are planted about 1 m apart, and all care consists in cutting off the old leaves and hoeing them every year in spring. We usually start cutting the petioles at the end of May or beginning of June. Rhubarb seeds are harvested in June, and keep well for three years. Varieties. - Myatt's linnaus. - Myatt's Victoria, - Prince Albert. WHITE SALSIFIS (Tragopogon porrifolium). - Sow in March, April and May, in rows or broadcast, in deep, substantial soil, smoked from the previous year. If the weather is dry, the seedbed is thoroughly basked to encourage seed emergence; if the plant is too thick, it is thinned out, then hoeed a few times. We start harvesting the roots in October, then successively until spring. To make sure you don't run out of them in winter, put them in gauges towards the end of November, or cover them in place during the frosts. Salsify seeds ripen in July, and are only good for one year. GARDEN WILLOW (Satureia hortensis). - It is sown in spring, after which it resows every year on its own, without needing any care. Perennial savory (S. montana). - It can be propagated from seed sown in spring, or by splitting plants at the same time. Savory seeds keep well for three years. SCORSONÈRE D'ESPAGNE or SALSIFIS NOIR (Scorzonera Hispanica) - Sown either in August, or in March and April, in rows or broadcast: care is exactly the same as for white salsify; only, as it goes to seed in the same year, the stems are cut in July; and as the roots rarely grow large enough to be eaten in the first year, this operation must be repeated the following year. Harvesting begins in October, and continues successively until spring. Scorsonera seeds are harvested towards the end of July from two-year-old individuals, and keep well for two years. SUMMER SPRINGS (Tetragonia exponsa). - This plant is an excellent summer substitute for spinach, as it has all the flavor of spinach. After soaking the seeds, sow them in a bed in February and March. When frosts are no longer a concern, transplant the seedlings into the ground, about Om, 60 apart in all directions. As soon as the stems begin to cover the ground, the leaves and tips of the young shoots are cut off. Seeds ripen in autumn, and keep well for up to five years. TOMATO or LOVE APPLE (Solanum lycopersicum). - The first tomatoes are sown as early as September, and placed in pots in the pineapple greenhouse, or on beds and under panels, for planting on beds in January. Planted at this time, the fruit begins to ripen in the first days of May. In January, we sow on beds and under panels, and when the plant is strong enough, we transplant it to the nursery, also on beds and under panels. A few days after planting, we start to give the plant a little air, in order to strengthen it. In February or March, we prepare a second layer of Om, 50 thick, with a heat of 20 to 25 degrees; we fill it with Om, 25 of compost, after which we plant four tomato plants under each panel. During the night, the panels are covered with mats, basked if necessary, and given air when the sun is shining; and when the plants begin to develop, two branches are selected on each, then lowered so as to prevent them from touching the inner surface of the panels. To keep them in this position, we tie them to small stakes which we drive into the bed at a certain distance from the base; then we remove the other branches; and when the plants are sufficiently full of flowers, we pinch off the ends of all the branches. From this time onwards, all new buds are carefully removed, and when the tomatoes start to turn red, the leaves are completely removed from the fruit, to bring forward ripening. Tomatoes are sown again in February and March, and when the plant is ready for planting, a layer of Om, 40 thick and Om,80 wide is prepared. Fill it with Om, 25 of potting soil, draw two rows, and plant the tomatoes Om, 80 apart along the line. Put a bell on each one, and give it air whenever the weather permits; then remove the bells as soon as frosts are no longer to be feared. When the plants begin to develop, select three or four branches from each, tie them to a stile and remove the others; Then, when they have reached Om, 75 to I mêtre in height, we pinch off all the ends, if the plants have flowers, otherwise we only cut them back when they are higher, and, as we said earlier, we take care to remove all the new buds; we remove a few leaves, and when the tomatoes start to turn red, we remove all the leaves from the fruit. To plant in the open ground, sow in February and March, equally on beds and under panels; transplant the plant in the nursery, and when frosts are no longer to be feared, lift the clodded plant to put it in the open ground. Mark out two rows per bed, and plant the tomatoes Om, 80 apart along the line. Water abundantly during hot spells, after which pruning and other care is similar to that described above. Tomato seeds keep well for five years. Varieties. - Early red. - Large red. - Stiff or monstrous stem. - Yellow. - Pear. - Cherry. Vegetable greenhouse. - We don't always have a suitable place to store our vegetables, and are often obliged to accept an inconvenient shed or a damp, poorly ventilated caye, where vegetable plants cannot be kept without deterioration. In such circumstances, there's nothing to be done, and you have to resign yourself to keeping only small provisions; but when you have access to a spacious cellar, deprived of light, and whose air can be renewed at will through doors or window wells, you find yourself in the most favorable conditions for preserving vegetables. The vegetable greenhouse should be divided into several compartments, into which the plants to be preserved are placed in beds, with a little sand or dry earth between each one. This method is suitable for plants with roots; as for cabbages, cauliflowers, cardoons, chicory, etc., they should be pulled out with their roots and planted in the sand at a sufficient distance apart to avoid contact which would cause rotting. In this way, vegetables from the previous year can be kept until April and May. CHAPTER XII Diseases of Vegetables. Knowledge of the diseases that attack vegetable plants is of little importance, especially as they can rarely be cured, and nature alone can bring about the cure. Whenever a plant finds itself in a pathological state as a result of unfavorable environmental influences that have developed a morbid state within it, and its tissues no longer have sufficient vital energy to fight the evil, disorganization begins, and the only means of recovery is a redoubling of care to restore the plant to its original vigor. The parasites that grow on diseased plants are not the cause of the disease; they are simply its effect. So why bother knowing that Puccinia asparagus grows on asparagus, Sclerotium varium on cabbage, and several species of Uredo on celery, beans, salad burnet and leeks? Botrytis effusa on spinach; Fusisporium on melon; Acrosporium monitioides on onion; Erysiphe communis on peas; Botrytis infestans on potato, etc.? These are, we repeat, effects, not causes. In cold, wet seasons, in unfavorable exposures, due to lack of care and precautions, plants suffer and fall ill; but with water, manure and shelter, we can prevent all this evil, which we could not repair once it existed. CHAPTER XIII Fruit garden. As the people for whom we are writing this book rarely have a fruit garden as distinct from a vegetable garden, we have not thought it necessary to give any special provisions for the orchard; we have limited ourselves to bringing together all 11 the notions that it is important to possess in order to make the most of one's fruit trees. This chapter contains the general principles of planting, pruning, disbudding and trellising, without taking into account the differences that exist between trees of different kinds. Special articles will deal with the care to be given to each particular species. § 1 - Planting. Successful planting depends on several conditions which, unfortunately, are not sufficiently observed. The first is to be aware of the nature of the soil, which should guide the choice of trees; for a tree grafted onto one subject will languish in a soil where it would, on the contrary, have thrived, had it been grafted onto another. The choice of trees is also very important, but presents many difficulties; for nurserymen are so little concerned with the future of the trees they raise, that they too often neglect their initial education and thus prepare many disappointments for the planters. They must be young and vigorous, the bark must be smooth, the subject must always be well proportioned to the graft, and above all, they must have been uprooted with great care. The best time to plant is immediately after leaf fall, in light soils; but in heavy, damp soils, plant only in February and March. Before planting, the roots should be inspected, and the ends of any broken ones cut off cleanly, without cutting any off, and taking care to preserve all the hair. If, at this time, the land is not ready for planting, or if planting requires several days' work, the plants should be placed in rows in such a way that they can be removed one by one during planting. If some circum stance prevents planting at the times indicated, from the end of March to the time when they can be put in place, they should be lifted every fortnight, and immediately put back in gauge, then watered. The more the season progresses, the more precautions need to be taken: they will have grown a lot of young roots, which you must take care not to break. If you follow our advice, you won't be able to plant them definitively until June. These late plantings should be watered during hot spells; it's even a good idea to put one or two watering cans in the hole before planting. For planting at ordinary times, the holes should always be made in autumn, even in soils where planting is not due until March. They should be wide and deep, in proportion to the volume of the roots, so that they can spread out at their ease. In very light soils, a good result will always be obtained if, after having made holes at least one meter deep and of a proportionate width, we put, at the bottom, sods placed in such a way that the roots are on top. In damp soils prone to retaining water, very wide and expensive holes should also be made, and small plaster placed at the bottom; they should then be filled to the necessary height with good soil mixed with consumed manure; finally, it would be better to delay planting for a few days than to plant in the rain or in overly damp soil. When it's time to plant, place the tree in the middle of the hole, as plumb as possible, while another person sinks the loose, fine soil between the roots; then, to leave no gaps, gently lift the tree, keeping it upright. Trees should only be buried up to the collar, i.e. about Om.10 above the roots; and those grafted on the ground should not be covered over, so that the roots do not become too deeply buried as the soil settles. When the tree is at the right height, cover all the roots with fine earth, then fill the rest of the hole with earth mixed with spent manure. To secure the tree, lightly tamp down the soil with your feet, pressing harder on the edges. Then add more soil to complete the hole. Some people sow fruit trees intended for grafting, in order to have more vigorous trees that are not delayed by transplanting. To do this successfully, you need to know your subsoil well, because if you don't have good soil, you'll get very poor results. As these trees always have a pivot that descends very deeply, when they reach bad soil, they turn yellow and have only languid vegetation. § 2 - Pruning. Pruning fruit trees is a very important operation: 1° Its purpose is to distribute sap evenly throughout the tree, and to give it a pleasing shape; 2° It prepares trees to bear more beautiful fruit of better quality; 3° If a tree were not pruned, its superfluous branches would infallibly exhaust its strength, and it would not last as long; thus, when pruning is well reasoned, it prolongs the life of trees. Of all gardening operations, tree pruning is the least advanced. In this respect, it would be desirable for practitioners to devote themselves to the study of plant physiology; indeed, how can such an important operation be carried out, if we do not know the functions of each part of a tree? The instruments used for pruning are the pruning knife and the pruning shears. Although the latter greatly shortens the work, it cannot completely replace the pruning knife, as its use requires a great deal of practice, and the pressure it exerts often bruises the branch below the cut. But it is very useful for cutting back branches that would be difficult to remove with the pruning knife, and for pruning vines and roses. In addition to these two instruments, it is sometimes necessary to use the handsaw or egohine to cut large branches. We usually start pruning at the end of January and into March, and sometimes even at the beginning of April; but it's impossible to give a precise indication of the most favorable time to start this operation, as it varies according to exposure and temperature differences from year to year. It would be much more natural to carry out pruning in the order of vegetation, starting with apricot trees, followed by peach, plum, pear, cherry and apple trees. However, to save time, it is customary to start with pear and apple trees, as they have little fear of frost, and are almost always finished by the time you start pruning peach trees. As a general rule, you should start with the weakest trees and finish with the most vigorous, in order to slow down their vigour a little; however, you should always prune before the sap is in motion, because later on you will greatly damage the health of these trees, and you will only obtain very weak shoots. There are also a few general principles from which we must never deviate: 1° When pruning, always make a clean, slightly oblique cut, opposite the eye on which you are pruning, and at Om, 03 or so above, so that the sap can easily cover the wound; it is also for this reason that whenever it is necessary to cut back a branch, you should cut it as close as possible to its insertion, and make a clean wound, which is always easier to cover. 2° Trees should not be pruned too short, as they will then grow too vigorously and yield little fruit. 3° Pruning too long exhausts the trees, because they set too much fruit, and there is really no advantage; because the fruit is less beautiful, the trees are tired, and they usually remain several years without bearing fruit. We will now describe the different parts of a tree, which it is essential to know how to recognize before starting to prune. 1. Stone fruit trees. The trunk or stem is the part of the tree that rises from the root to the base of the branches. The mother branches are so called because they are the ones that give birth to all the others; they grow directly from the trunk. The limbs are the branches that grow on the side of the mother branches, and whose development is encouraged to form the framework of the tree. Bifurcation branches are limbs designed to fill the gaps left by the extension of mother branches and limbs; they should only ever be established on the third or fourth bud above the previous pruning. Wood branches are those which form the framework of the tree and the extension of each limb; they are easy to recognize on all trees, by their size and the eyes with which they are furnished, eyes which are always thin and pointed. Fruiting branches are generally thin and elongated; in Peach trees, the bark is green on the wall side and reddish on the sunny side. These branches must be renewed annually, as they only bear fruit once. Replacement branches. - Fruiting branches of. Peach tree only produce in the second year, and only bear fruit once, as we've just said. It is therefore essential to replace them every year, which is very easy, as each fruiting branch has several eyes at its base; once these eyes have developed, it is sufficient to choose the most vigorous bud as close as possible to the insertion of the fruiting branch, and to remove the others. These new branches are called replacement branches; once they have borne fruit, they must be replaced in their turn, and so on. Greedy branches. - On espaliered trees, gourmands are generally placed at the top of the limbs. They are easily recognized by their wide spread and their vigour, which is so detrimental to the other branches that their development must be stopped by all possible means; they should never be seen on a well-treated tree. Buds are young shoots of the year; in the second year, the bud becomes a woody or fruit-bearing branch, depending on its position. False or early buds are those born between the leaves of the current year's shoots. 2. Fruit-bearing trees. Since the woody branches on all fruit trees have more or less the same characteristics, we'll only talk about the buds they bear: those on Pear and Apple trees are wrapped in a scaly membrane, but are always thin and elongated, as on Peach trees. Flower buds are much larger than wood buds, rounded in shape and enveloped by a large number of scales. Twigs are small, slender, elongated branches ending in a leaf or flower bud. Their eyes are very tightly closed, and easily transform into flower buds. They should be kept, as they can bear fruit for several years. The lambourdes are essentially productive; they grow on twigs, and often on wood branches too. They almost always end in a flower bud, which often doesn't blossom until the second year. The eyes with which they are furnished are much closer together than on other twigs, and always very ready to bear fruit. They remain several years before reaching full development, are much larger at their base than at their tip, and are covered with a circularly wrinkled bark whose folds become deeper as they age. §3 - Disbudding. This operation begins in May, and continues throughout the vegetation period; it consists of removing any poorly placed buds that need to be removed at the next pruning. Disbudding takes place on branches from previous years, and can be carried out using the disbudding tool. As for disbudding the current year's buds, as this involves removing the false buds, it is done with the fingernail. In both cases, on espaliered trees, buds on the front and back of the branches, and those on the sides that are too close together, are removed; on other trees, buds on the top and bottom of the limbs, and those that are too close together, are removed. This operation should be started as soon as the buds to be removed are from Om, 02 to Om, 03 in length, so that the sap which would be needed for their vegetation, if we waited until later, immediately turns to the benefit of those to be kept. § 4 - Trellising. Trellising consists in securing the buds of espaliered trees to trellises or walls, using wicker and rushes for trellises, and rags and nails for walls, which are soft enough to be driven in easily (1). (1) As a ladder is always essential for this operation, we recommend Mr. Forest's ladder. Butt-trees describe an arc of a circle which, when the feet of the ladder enter the ground, prevents damage to the branches on which it rests. 11. The time to start trellising is indicated by the development of buds; it is usually in June that it is essential to start, to finish only towards the end of the season. The aim of this operation is not only to fix the buds, lest they be broken by the wind, but also to slow down the vigor of the most advanced by training them earlier than the others. When trellising, always place the buds in a straight line, at equal distances and without ever crossing them. It's during the first trellising that you should remove any poorly placed fruit, and thin out any that are too tightly packed, which could harm each other. § 5 - Fruit trees. Most people who cultivate fruit trees choose the healthiest room in their home, and sometimes even the first one they see. Nothing is more difficult than preserving fruit. There are certain conditions that are not generally observed, but which are nonetheless indispensable. To preserve fruit for as long as possible, and with the best possible chances of success, a special room must be set aside for the purpose, half-buried, in an area where the temperature is least likely to vary, and where air and light can be renewed or intercepted at will. Shelves of Om, 50 to Om, 60 in width, with a rim to prevent the fruit from falling off, are laid out and covered with a bed of new, fine, dry, odorless straw. It is in this room that we place, by species, the fruits that the advanced season prevents us from leaving on the trees, and which must ripen at more or less distant times. A few days before placing them in the orchard, they should be carefully sorted to separate those not worth keeping, and left to dry out. When the fruit box is full and dry, close it to the air and light, and do nothing more than visit the fruit once or twice a week. Grapes can be stored on shelves like other fruits, or rather hung from the ceiling; but they require scrupulous supervision, and are generally quite difficult to preserve. People who attach great importance to preserving their fruit can line all the walls of their orchard with wood, and increase the chances of preservation. You could also place quicklime on the last combs, taking care to renew it each time it is extinguished. Because of its drying properties, this lime will keep the atmosphere always dry. The method we have described is the only one used by orange-tree growers; all other recipes for preservation are either unsafe or completely impractical, and we advise you to make do with an orange-tree grower, observing the preservation conditions we have described here. § 6 - Cultivation of the best fruit species, ABRICOTIER (Armeniaca vulgaris). - All soils are suitable for apricot trees, provided they are not too damp. They are usually grafted onto the Saint-Julien plum tree, but as they grow very vigorously and bear little fruit in strong soils, they should be grafted onto the Cerisette plum tree, which grows much less than the Saint-Julien. As the fruit of open-air apricot trees is much more fragrant than that of espaliered trees, only a few of the latter are usually planted, in order to obtain fruit that ripens a little earlier, or in localities where it does not ripen well in the open air. High-stemmed apricot trees are planted about 6 mềtres apart. Once the young trees have been given the right direction, it will still be necessary to prune them every year; otherwise, the branches would easily recede from the bottom; but by a reasoned and timely pruning we will easily force the sap to flow back into the lower parts; we will treat the high-stemmed trees in the same way, and in addition we will cut back all the branches that would point inwards, so that the air can circulate easily. Apricot trees can be rejuvenated by cutting back the larger branches, choosing the most vigorous and well-disposed young shoots to replace them. It is difficult to advance the ripening of apricot trees; nevertheless, if you want to try it, you should give them very little heat and only start heating them in February. Varieties. - Early or Apricot, - Angoumois, - Common, - Dutch, - Alberge, - Peach. AMANDIER (Amygdalus communis). - Cultivated almond trees are grafted onto the common variety, and are rarely planted except in the open air, where they require no care. However, in the North, they need to be planted in espaliers, with good exposure, and pruned accordingly. The almond tree often flowers as early as February, which is why it is so often planted in pleasure gardens. Varieties. - Amandier à gros fruits, - Amandier de Tours, - Amandier Princesse ou des Dames, à coque tendre. CHERRY (Cerasus). - Cherry trees are not difficult when it comes to choosing a site: high-stemmed cherry trees are grafted on to wild cherry, and other forms are grafted on to Sainte-Lucie. To obtain fruit a little earlier, you can plant a few English Cherry trees in espaliers or form cattails, which produce a lot; but you're more likely to plant high-stemmed trees, which always produce more. If planted in rows, they should be spaced about 6 meters apart. It is only necessary to prune them during the first few years, to form the framework of the tree; and for espaliers and cattails, once formed, remove as few branches as possible. The only thing to be done is to give good direction to each limb, as they grow in size. When Cherry trees cease to bear fruit, they can easily be rejuvenated by cutting back the large branches near their insertion; they promptly provide new ones, with which to form another té̂te. De la culture forcée du Cerisier. - To bring forward the maturity of fruit trees, we must take into account the average temperature at the time when each species begins to vegetate, to enter into flower, and finally to that which ordinarily reigns at the time of ripening of the fruit, so that, in a space of time which must always be less long than in the natural state, we subject the trees to the various modifications of heat through which they ordinarily pass; for, in one case as in the other, they can only bear fruit after having completed all the phases of vegetation. We can bring forward the maturity of espaliered cherry trees by placing glass frames in front of them, or, better still, by planting dwarf English or Royal cherry trees in pots in autumn, which are the most successful. They should be as well-branched as possible. Bury the pots in a well-exposed spot, and the following year, in January, place them in a glass greenhouse, where the temperature need only be maintained at 12 or 14 degrees. Air can be supplied when the sun is shining. You could even combine them with plum trees and give them the same care. If you put them in the greenhouse at the right time, the fruit is usually ripe by the beginning of April. In this way, they can be heated for several years running. Varieties. - English. - Royal. - Reine-Hortense. - Belle de Choisy, - de Portugal, - Belle magnifique, - du Nord tardive, - Grosse, - Guigne noire, - Guigne ambrée, - Gros bigarreau noir. COIGNASSIER (Cydonia communis). - Coignassiers are generally grown as grafted pear trees. They should be planted at the same time as pear trees. They are rarely grown as fruit trees, although their fruit is very beautiful, but the smell they give off when they begin to ripen is generally unpleasant and forces us to relegate these trees to areas far from our homes. In this case, there's no need to choose the land as you would when grafting Pear trees, because they grow well everywhere, even in damp places. You don't need to prune them; just remove the dead wood. The only cultivated variety is the Portuguese variety, grafted on the common Coignassier, whose fruit ripens in October. VINE THORN (Berberis). - It grows in the most arid soils, and in autumn produces fruit that makes excellent jams. Fig tree (Ficus Carica). - All soils are suitable for Fig trees, provided they are not too damp. These trees should only be planted at the end of March or in April, and as they are difficult to get going again, they should be planted in rootballs or raised in pots. They are best planted close to a wall and in the warmest location; in some places, it would even be preferable to espalier and trellis them like other trees. However, whatever form they take, fig trees need to be covered in winter to protect them from frost. Towards the end of November, all the branches are gathered together and wrapped in straw held together with ties. When the stems are young and not very high, we lower them to the ground and hold them there with wooden hooks; then we cover them with Om, 15 earth or straw, until the end of March. Whichever way you shelter them, you must take great care to secure the base, and, should the stems be affected by frost, you cut them off at the collar, an operation you can also perform when they have become too strong. They quickly regrow new stems, which bear fruit in the second year. Fig trees usually produce two harvests, but in our climate it's extremely rare for the autumn crop to ripen, so we'll only talk about the first, which ripens in July and August. To encourage fruit development and bring forward ripening, in June we pinch off the terminal buds of fruit-bearing branches, preventing them from falling off before ripening. Fig trees cannot be pruned, as amputations are very harmful to them, due to the large amount of sap they lose each time. In spring, simply cut off dead branches and cut back those too thin to produce fruit. DE JARDINAGE. fruit. However, if they are growing too vigorously, you can pinch the ends of the branches, a method often used with advantage to make them bear fruit. A large number of varieties are grown in the south of France, but only two are successfully cultivated in Paris: the white rondo and the violet. Forced cultivation of the Fiquier. - In January, we cover the Fig trees with a small mobile greenhouse, and start heating them to 15 degrees; then we gradually raise the heat to 25 degrees without inconvenience, and in the first days of May, we obtain ripe fruit. We will only briefly mention a process that has fallen into disrepute in our country, but is now being revived: caprification. Recent facts seem to prove that this operation is not as useless as has been claimed, although it is not essential for the fertilization of Fig trees. . It increases the number of fruits, which ripen more reliably. In the absence of the fertilizing insects found in the fruit of the wild Fig tree, a branch of which is hung on the Fig tree you wish to caprify, you can simply prick the eye of the Fig with a needle dipped in olive oil, and wait for the result. This operation, which we offer to our readers for what it may be worth, at least has the advantage of not compromising the fruit on which the test has been carried out. RASPBERRY (Rubus Idœeus). - Raspberry bushes grow everywhere, but they prefer fresh, light, well-amended soil, as they considerably deplete the earth, and it is necessary, in order to obtain good fruit, to plant them in autumn with new soil or used fertilizer. They are planted in autumn, or in February and March, depending on the variety, about 1 m apart. After planting, they are cut back to a height of about Om, 15. Each year, in June, select the five or six best buds from each clump, and cut off the others. This removal will benefit the stems left behind, and the fruit they produce will be much more beautiful; they ripen in July. In March, cut down to the ground the stems that have borne fruit, and prune the others back more or less, depending on their vigor. Depending on the soil and the care they have received, they should be moved every four, five or six years. Varieties. - Red raspberry, - Fr. with flesh-colored fruit, - Fr. white. - Four seasons. GRAPEBERRY (Ribes rubrum). - Although not very fussy about the choice of soil, redcurrants will produce more beautiful fruit and of better quality in soft, cool soils, without excess humidity, than in other soils. They can be given any shape you like, but because of the pruning they have to undergo, it is preferable to grow them in clumps. Currants can be espaliered, and usually ripen from June to July; but ripening can easily be brought forward by placing frames in front of them. They are planted about 1.30 m apart, in autumn or February, depending on the soil. They are pruned in February: the first year, they are pruned short, to encourage the development of the lower eyes; but for subsequent prunings, they should be pruned longer, and it should always be remembered that redcurrants only bear abundant fruit on two-year-old wood. Each year, let the branches necessary to form a good clump develop successively, and take care to remove the buds that start at the base, then cut back the large branches as they reach their sixth year (which is easy to see by counting each year's shoots), as they then become too high, receding at the bottom and giving only mediocre-quality fruit; then replace each branch cut back with a young bud. Forced cultivation of redcurrants. - Currant bushes can easily be heated on site, if planted in counter-trellises; if planted in pots, they can be treated in the same way as cherry trees. Varieties. - Red currant. - Gr. white. - Flesh-colored, - Cherry, - Gondouin, - Queen Victoria. GROSEILLIER A FRUIT NOIR, CASSIS, POIVRIER (R. nigrum). - Treated exactly like the ordinary currant tree, it can be cut back more often, as the wood bears fruit. SPINY OR CURRANT (R. uva crispa). - A considerable number of Gooseberry varieties have been sown, many of which are remarkable for the size of their fruit. To maintain good fruit, branches that have been producing for three years should be removed. MURRY (Morus). - Mulberries are very hardy trees, adapting well to almost all soils, even those of mediocre quality, except those that are constantly damp. Whatever the species, it should not be planted before February, nor should any branches be cut off; after which, all care consists of a few hoeings. As a fruit tree, only the black Mürier is grown, with fruit ripening from July to September. It cannot be pruned, and only the dead wood is removed. When these trees are too old and produce only small fruits, they should be cut back, i.e. the branches should be cut back to within a few centimetres of the trunk; they will produce very vigorous young shoots which will soon bear fruit. The White Mulberry is grown as an ornamental tree, but more particularly as a grafting plant for the broad-leaved species grown to feed silkworms. The most favorable time for grafting is the end of April, and the most advantageous variety, among those grown for the above-mentioned purpose, is the Moretti, whose leaves are very wide and much preferable to those of the Multicaule. Its hardiness is at least equal to that of the ordinary White Mulberry. It reproduces very well from seeds sown in spring. Mulberries can be grown as spindles or clumps, to form hedges which, if well trained, will produce many leaves. It is preferable to cut off the branches whose leaves you want to feed the worms; but you must take care to leave all the small branches, and not to detach the leaves, so as not to completely block the circulation of the sap. At the end of June or the beginning of July, or as soon as the worms have been fed, the Mulberries should be pruned immediately, so that the shoots that develop during the August sap are strong enough to withstand the frosts. NEFLIER (Mespilus Germanica). - Néfliers do very well everywhere, even in very poor soils. They should be planted in autumn, unless the nature of the soil does not allow it; as the fruit comes at the end of the branches, these trees should not be pruned, and it would be impossible to give them a regular shape. Preference should be given to large-fruited medlar trees. The fruits should be picked in October and November, and laid out on straw or on shelves, where they ripen. HAWTHORN (Corylus). - Hazelnut trees are very hardy, and should be planted in autumn; they grow in all types of soil and in all types of exposure. They can be grown in clumps or on stems, and fruit equally well in either case. Fruit ripens in August and September, and falls as soon as ripe. Several species are grown solely for ornamental purposes in pleasure gardens. Varieties. - Red-fruited hazelnut. - N. grosse aveline de Provence, - N. avelinier rouge, - N. à fruits en grappes. WALNUT (Juglans regia). - Walnut trees deserve to be cultivated in many respects; however, they are rarely given a place in gardens, because of the space they cover (at least 20 or 30 meters are needed between plants) and the extent of their roots, which exhaust the soil and are very harmful to surrounding crops. They like soft, substantial, deep soil. They tolerate transplanting fairly well when they're young and cared for, but it's especially important to avoid cutting them back when planting, which would be very detrimental to their growth. It is better to sow them in situ. In this case, choose the most beautiful and ripest nuts of the variety you want to sow, and either put them in the ground in autumn, or stratify them in sand and sow them in spring. They will bear fruit after six to eight years of sowing. If you wish to change the variety you have sown, or if you fear that it will not reproduce identically once the trees have reached a height of around 1 metre and a diameter of Om, 03, you will need to graft them in spring, either in a cleft or in a ring; they will then grow a little less. Walnut trees do not need pruning; however, when they are old, it often happens that the end of the branches dies; they must then be cut back to about Om, 60 from the trunk, and a new head is formed. Varieties. - Soft-shelled walnut, - Late walnut, - Jewel walnut (for fruit size). There is a very interesting new species, known as Juglans prœparturiens. It doesn't grow very tall and produces good-quality fruit from the second year of sowing. Walnut trees ripen towards the end of September or the beginning of October, but can be eaten in kernels as early as the end of July. Several species of American walnut are also grown as ornamental trees. PEACH (Amygdalus Persica) (1). - In deep soils, we prefer to plant Peach trees grafted on the Almond tree; but in those with only a shallow layer of good soil, and with a tuff or clay base, trees grafted on the Plum tree should be planted, as they have tracer roots that are content with shallower soil. Peach trees can be planted in espaliers at all exposures; only in the north and west, early varieties should be planted, with slightly less spacing than at other exposures. The usual distance is 8 to 10 meters, depending on their shape and the nature of the soil. A pear tree can be planted in between, to provide fruit until the peach trees are removed as they develop. It is almost always preferable to plant eighteen-month-old grafted peach trees; given the right circumstances, they are more likely to succeed than those that have already been pruned several times. When planting, care should be taken to place the strongest roots at the front, and the collar of the tree should be about Om.15 from the wall on which the stem is inclined. (For planting times and other precautions, see Planting, page 182). To maintain the vigor of the trees, it is necessary to fertilize the bed where the peach trees are planted; but it is not possible to determine the time that should elapse between two fertilizations, as this depends on the nature of the soil. Preference should be given to new soil, turf or half-used manure. In years when it is necessary to fertilize peach trees, they should be pruned longer, and every year, after pruning, a hoeing operation should be carried out at the foot of the trees. In July and August, water the leaves of the peach trees using a hand pump. This operation is very useful, but should only be carried out when the sun is no longer shining on the espalier. The high temperatures of this period often make it necessary to water the base of the trees, so hoe them and form a basin around each tree, filling it with short manure, which retains moisture longer. As soon as the trees have been planted, it is important to determine the form in which they will be grown. Without going into the advantages and disadvantages of other methods of cultivation, we will limit ourselves to indicating the one used in Montreuil as the simplest and one of the most advantageous. To achieve a good result, we recommend tracing a quarter-circle on the wall (fig. 17), where we will indicate each year the place the main branches should occupy according to their development. First year. After planting, cut the stems of the young peach trees we recommend at Om, 15 or Om, 20 above the graft. This determines the development of several buds. Disbudding. - When the buds are Om, 25 to Om, 30 in length, select the two most vigorous, one on each side, to form the two mother branches b (fig. 14), then remove the others. Trellising. - In case of breakage, they should be tied, but in such a way as not to hinder their development. If one of the two buds is more vigorous than the other, it should be tilted more to restore the balance of the sap, a principle which must never be deviated from, as the whole future of the tree depends on it. Second year. - In February, i.e. when the sap begins to swell the buds, and not when they are in flower, as some people advise, after the tree has been stripped, the wall should be cleaned, as well as any limbs on which gallinsects may be found, which should be done every year; after which the snag a (sig. 14) should be cut, and the wound covered with swelling wax. Mother branches 6 will be pruned to Om, 35 or Om, 40 in length, according to the shape of the tree immediately above the eye intended to extend the branches. In the absence of the front eye, the top eye can be used, which should be observed at each pruning. At this point, the development of the mother branches makes it possible to establish a lower sub-mother branch on each side of the tree, by means of lateral eyes placed at the base of the mother branches.  Fig. 14 - Peach tree, 2nd year. When attaching the two mother branches b, give them about 10 degrees of opening. Disbudding. - In May, remove with the fingernail, or with the tip of a pruning knife, all buds which are too close together, those which have double or triple use due to the development of double or triple eyes, which are so numerous on peach trees; those placed on the front or back of the branches; all those which should be removed during pruning in order to encourage the development of buds which will provide the second lower mother branches. Forced to make a choice, we prefer to remove the bud in the middle of the triple eyes, which, always more vigorous than the others, could later be a source of embarrassment. As for the other buds, in either case, only the best placed of the two should be kept. The same reason for removing the most vigorous bud from the ordinary eyes means that this same bud should be kept when disbudding the terminal eye of each branch; since it is intended to prolong the branch, this bud must always dominate the others. Later on, false buds should also be removed, and those that should be kept should be pinched off above the seventh or eighth leaf. From the time indicated above, disbudding continues successively until July, when the tips of any buds whose development needs to be moderated are pinched off with the fingernail. Trellising. - As the buds develop, they are trellised; but this operation requires a great deal of care, as the buds are so tender that they will break cleanly unless great care is taken to bring them into the position they should occupy. Always give them the most direct position possible, so that the circulation of the sap is not slowed down by any obstacle, and always avoid crossing the buds one on top of the other. Third year. - At the time of pruning, and before untrimming the tree, we examine the vegetation of each limb, and judge whether it would not be necessary, by pruning, to re-establish the balance of the sap in the event of one limb being much more vigorous than the other. 12 Cut the mother branches b (fig. 15) at about Om, 40 or Om, 50 in length, depending on their vigor, taking care that the eye on which you are pruning is placed so as to extend them as directly as possible, which must be observed at each pruning and for each branch. Fig. 15 - Peach tree, 3rd year. All buds from the previous year, which cover the woody branches, should be pruned back two or three eyes from their insertion, so as to have as many fruit-bearing branches the following year as the tree's vigor will allow; then remove any buds that are poorly placed. If the buds at the base of the mother branches are very vigorous, they should be pruned short, to determine the development of the fruiting branches; otherwise, they should be pruned to five or six eyes. When reattaching them, give the mother branches about 25 to 30 degrees of opening, if they are of equal strength; if not, the least vigorous should be given a more upright position, which should be observed every year. Disbudding. - We remove buds and false buds that are in the wrong position, taking the same precautions as the previous year. The buds at the base of the mother branches should be encouraged to develop, as they should provide the first upper sub-mother branches; this should also be done for the buds of the fruit-bearing branches closest to the main branch, as these are the ones which should replace the fruit-bearing branches at the next pruning; for the rest of the disbudding, observe what has already been said. Trellising. - When the buds are about Om.25 to Om.30 in length, they are trained, always starting with the most vigorous. Fourth year. - After stripping the tree, as in every year, we prune the tips of the mother branches b (fig. 16), at point 3 for example, to varying lengths, depending on their vigour. We also prune the lower sub-mother branches according to their strength, and always on the eye most favorable to their extension; we cut back the fruit-bearing branches on the replacement branches, which should always be as close as possible to the main branches, so that the latter always seem to be rejuvenated by new shoots; then we prune the replacement branches, to bear fruit, at five or six eyes, according to their strength and the tree's vigor, but always with the aim of obtaining a replacement bud as close as possible to their insertion. As always, false buds should be pruned back to two or three eyes.... Reattach the mother branches, giving them about 35 to 40 degrees of opening, always taking care to observe what was said on this subject for the previous year. Disbudding. - Watch out for woody branches that tend to establish themselves where there should only ever be fruit-bearing branches. Pay particular attention to encouraging the development of buds, which should provide the second upper sub-mother branches and Fig. 16. Peach tree, 4c year. =- bifurcation branches, as well as buds destined to form replacement branches. Care should be taken to pinch off the fruiting buds; if there are no fruits, or if they have fallen before maturity, these buds should be cut back immediately onto the replacement bud, unless one of them is too vigorous, in which case it would be preferable to bring it closer only at pruning. For the rest of this operation, please refer to what has already been said about disbudding in the second year. It is especially important to pinch off buds which, because of their vigor, threaten to become "gourmands". Trellising. - Trellising should be carried out according to the same principles, and successively, as in previous years. As the tree is expected to bear fruit, it should be uncovered gradually as it approaches maturity, a precaution that should always be taken. Fifth year. - The aim of this year's pruning is to extend and strengthen all parts of the tree (fig. 17). We will shorten the terminal branches according to the same principles as for previous prunings, and, when pruning the fruit-bearing branches, we will leave fruit on them according to their vigor and the health of the tree; we will prune the false buds to two or three eyes, as in previous years, and we will give the mother branches about 45 to 50 degrees of opening. Bifurcation branches e and f should be encouraged to grow longer, as should buds g, which can then be used to make new bifurcation branches. Finally, by disbudding young shoots and removing misplaced false buds, pinching and trellising, all parts of the tree will be maintained or brought back to a perfect balance of vegetation. As the tree advances in age, pruning and other operations will become more complicated, but the principles will always be the same; bifurcation branches will be established successively, to fill the gaps, and care must always be taken to keep the mother branches, as well as all the others, in the proportions relative to their various functions. 12 IOVNIdAVT ad TANV LAE Fig. 17 - Peach tree, 5th year. = 211 Peach trees grown in this form are particularly suitable for people who want to have their hearts quickly filled. Planted one meter apart, they can bear abundant fruit from the third year onwards, without any special care.  - 2 Fig. 18 - U-shaped peach trees = e Like all espalier trees, U-shaped peach trees should be cut back after planting to Om, 15 or Om, 20 above the graft, to encourage the development of buds destined to supply the two mother branches. During the course of their growth, these two buds should be directed so as to form a single or double U (fig. 18), taking care to leave the ends free so that they are not hindered in their development. At this point, U-shaped peach trees are just as easy to manage as oblique peach trees, over which they have a distinct advantage. Forced peach tree cultivation. - We can easily bring forward the ripening of espaliered peach trees, especially the early varieties; and, to be more certain of the success of the operation, we prefer to bring forward those placed to the east or west. In January, place a small mobile greenhouse in front of the peach trees, covered by 2-meter-long frames supported by rafters, the top of which will be Fig. 19. - Greenhouse to be forced. sealed into the wall, and which will bear at the bottom on a plank base Om 85 high, which will produce Om, 90 internally, sufficient space to provide the necessary care, which, incidentally, is absolutely the same as that indicated above. After pruning, start with a temperature of 12 degrees; then gradually increase to 18 degrees, but no higher; and as the heat will be much higher when the sun is shining, provide air; cover the greenhouse at night with straw mats, syringe the leaves if necessary; and as the fruits are usually more numerous than outdoors, it is often necessary to remove some of them, so as not to exhaust the trees. After ripening, which takes place in April, the frames are removed. Usually, peach trees that have been forced into frames are allowed to rest for a year or two, but we can safely say that this operation can be repeated the following year. Varieties. - Avant-Pêche blanche. - Petite Mignonne, - Grosse Mignonne, - Malte, - Madeleine, - Chevreuse, - Chevreuse tardive, - Admirable Belle de Vitry, - Brugnon musqué, - Madeleine rouge tardive, - Alberge jaune. - Galande, - Vineuse pourpre hâtive, - Madeleine de Courson, - Violette hâtive. - Veloutée tardive, - Teton de Venus, - Bourdine, - Bon ouvrier, - Pourprée tardive, - Belle de Doué, - Reine des vergers. PEAR (Pyrus). - Planting. - Pear trees grafted on Coignassier succeed in almost all soils, even those with little depth, provided they are not loamy or damp; otherwise, despite all care, they would perish after a few years. Whenever you have to plant in deep soil, it's best to plant pear trees grafted on franc, as they are much more robust. It is wrong to say that these trees are too slow to set fruit, because they grow too vigorously; for if, by well-reasoned pruning in proportion to the trees' strength, we establish an equal distribution of sap in all the limbs, we will often succeed in making them bear fruit from the very first years; once these trees have set fruit, they give abundantly, and live to be very old. If you are planting Pear trees grafted on Coignassier, you will need trees that have been grafted for eighteen or twenty months; but if they are grafted on Franc, as they grow much more vigorously, they can sometimes be planted grafted this year. For planting in flowerbeds, we recommend taking pear trees raised in cattails; in this position, this is really the most advantageous form, as it takes up little space and produces a lot of fruit. A dwarf apple or pear tree, pruned into a goblet shape, should be placed between two vines. They should then be planted about 4 or 5 meters apart. 1. Cattail pear trees. Pruning. - In the first year, prune the terminal branch to seven or eight eyes, depending on the tree's vigor, Fig. 20 - Cattail pear tree. in order to obtain three or four new limbs. Care should be taken to prune on the most favourably placed eye, to extend the stem as vertically as possible (fig. 20). The terminal branch of each branch should also be pruned on an eye positioned so as to extend the arm horizontally. As the lower arms are the oldest, they will be more elongated than those above them, and care must be taken at each pruning to maintain the same proportions. On each limb, we remove the twigs that are placed above and below, then we prune to about Om, 03 in length those that are not necessary to the shape of the tree. It is the lower eyes of these twigs that will give rise to the twigs. If there are a few twigs ending in a flowering bud, they should not be pruned, as this would deprive you of some fruit. If, in the place where a limb is needed to complete the regularity of the tree, there is no bud to form it, one can easily be obtained, either by placing a crest, or by circling the eye closest to the place where a limb is needed, which should be done in the following way. At pruning time, make a transverse incision immediately above, then a second one at Om, 02 or Om, 03 above the first, more or less, depending on the strength you want to give the bud. Then, starting from the second incision, a longitudinal incision is made at Om, 02 or Om, 03 on each side of the eye, and the portion of bark between the two transverse incisions is removed. The eye is thus encircled, and develops as vigorously as if the entire part above it had been removed. This operation can be performed successfully not only on the stem, but also on all the limbs of a tree where there are gaps to be filled. Disbudding. - To encourage the development of the terminal bud, the two or three buds closest to it should be pinched very short, unless the first bud has produced too weak a shoot, in which case it should be replaced by the most vigorous and closest to the tip. From the others, select the best-placed ones to form members for the next pruning, always observing that they must never be placed just above those of the previous year; for the members of a cattail must be arranged in such a way that, starting from the insertion of the first member, the others spiral around the stem. Pinch off the two or three buds closest to the terminal branch of each arm, and remove all the buds born above and below each limb. On the previous year's limbs, any buds growing too vigorously should be pinched off; then twigs should be pruned to Om, 16 or Om, 18 in length. Prune terminal shoots in proportion to the tree's vigor, and always on the eye best placed to extend them according to their position. Other twigs should be pruned to about Om, 05, even those that have been pinched off during disbudding; twigs that have borne fruit should be pruned to Om, 12 or Om, 15, and if those that have been broken off during disbudding have grown, they should be pruned above the shoot. Fruit-bearing branches will be protected successively, and those that grow too vigorously will be watched over, so that gourmands cannot form on any part of the tree; finally, the cattails will be treated in the second year as they were in the first, and so on; only, with each pruning, the operations will become more complicated. As it often happens that the limbs of a cattail tend to rise vertically, which is not only contrary to the principles, but also detrimental to the subsequent development of the tree (as these limbs then grow with such vigor that they absorb a large part of the sap necessary for the vegetation of the other limbs), we must, in such circumstances, seek a way to remedy such a state of affairs. We often resort to the use of small buttresses, but as this method presents many difficulties and serious inconveniences, we will share with you a procedure communicated to the Société centrale d'horticulture by Mr. Chevalier Gérolme: "I have placed four stakes at the foot of each pyramid, with a notch of Om, 028 at the head of each; I have attached a circle of wire at Om, 081 above ground level, in order to be able to hoe and clean. With this simple device, which I call a horizontal trellis, and which offers me the resources of a Montreuil wall, I become master of my tree. Equipped with a large number of leather straps pierced at both ends, and a bundle of wicker, I take the branch to be tilted, whether small or large, short or long, it doesn't matter; at the appropriate point, I encircle it with my strap, which I close with one end of a wicker strand, and stop the other on the circle at the place required by the inflection of the branch or the emptiness of the tree. With this procedure, I've been able to rectify a thousand irregularities independent of size, like proportioning spaces, destroying the confusion that always exists in pyramids, facilitating the circulation of air, the movement of light; in a word, satisfying all the conditions of development and equilibrium that until now had not been fulfilled." 2. Espaliered pear trees. To form an espalier of Pear trees, take young, vigorous dwarf trees, and plant them in a pre13 3 218 GARDENING MANUAL. ference to the east or west. They can be grown in several forms, but we consider fan-shaped (fig. 21) and palmette-shaped (fig. 22 and fig. 23) to be the easiest to manage and the most advantageous. Fan-shaped pear trees should be planted 5, 33, 6, 66 or 8 meters apart, depending on the nature of the soil and the height of the walls. ~~ 9th of the walls, with preference given to grafted trees of the year. They should be cut back so as to obtain five buds on each side to form the framework of the tree. If, in the first year, the number of buds required to form the tree is not obtained, the terminal bud should be directed vertically to extend the stem, and the following year it should be cut back to obtain limbs a, b, c, d, e. As the buds develop, they should be trellised, placing them as parallel as possible and at equal distance from each other. Pinch the tips of those that grow more vigorously than the others, so that each limb has roughly equal growth. The buds in front and behind the branches should also be pinched very short, to dismantle them as a result of pruning. By disbudding, we encourage the extension of the terminal bud of each member, and we pinch very short all those which are badly placed. In subsequent prunings, bifurcation branches, f, g, h, i (fig. 21), are established on each limb successively, as needed, to fill in the gaps. To complete the regularity of espalier-grown Pear trees, we can, independently of what we have indicated for those grown in cattails, resort to approach grafting, which should be done in the following way. When the buds are still in the herbaceous state, choose the one closest to the place where you need to establish a limb, lower it carefully so as not to break it, and, after making a longitudinal wound on each part, apply them one on top of the other, then hold them in this position by means of a ligature which is loosened as soon as the grafting is resumed; when the bud is woody, a notch is made at mid-wood, just below the graft, but the graft is not completely weeded until pruning time. As many grafts as necessary can be placed on the same tree, as we have been able to observe on an espalier of Pear trees entrusted to the care of Mr. Fourquet, a skilled horticulturist, who, with the help of approach grafting, succeeded in a very short time in giving a regular shape to Pear trees whose limbs were in complete disorder. If trees are planted which already have the number of branches needed to form them, all branches should be pruned back to about Om, 04 and over an eye arranged so as to extend the limb in the direction required by the shape of the tree. The others should be dismantled, taking care to preserve the joists and twigs. - e hu Fig. 22 - Palmette pear trees. ~~ ~ ~ ~ ~~ ua e Those destined to be grown as palms should be planted 5 or 6 meters apart; the stem should be bent back so as to obtain a few buds on the right and left, from which the first limbs a (fig. 22) should be formed, setting them at around Om, 30 to Om, 35 one above the other. Those on the right should alternate, as far as possible, with those on the left; when trellising, they should be inclined more or less, depending on whether you wish to encourage the development of one or restrict that of the other. In subsequent years, they should be lowered further, but never placed horizontally. In subsequent years, we prune the vertical branch so as to obtain two lateral branches each year, until the tree has reached the full height of the wall. Prune the arms hcab L M ~~ Fig. 23 - Double-stemmed palmette pear tree. to around Om, 15 to Om, 18 in length, depending on their strength and position, but always on an eye placed so as to extend them as directly as possible. If one extension bud is much more vigorous than the other, it should be inclined more than the others when trellised, to restore the balance. Pear trees trained as double-stemmed palms (fig. 23) should be trained according to the same principles as single-stemmed palms. This form is now adopted by the greatest number of Pear growers, who prefer it to all others. M- - The. Stemmed pear trees should be planted 5 or 6 meters apart, and for the first few years, they should be pruned so that the branches that form the framework of the tree are equally spaced. Their development should be monitored to encourage the least vigorous ones, until such time as the trees can be left to their own devices, i.e. about two years later. When Pear trees are very old, or so exhausted that they no longer produce fruit, they can still be put to good use by cutting them down and grafting them into the crown (see article on Grafting, p. 67). More or fewer grafts are placed, depending on the tree's strength; then, when they are well established, the most vigorous are chosen to form the new framework.
