APPLE (Pyrus malus). - All soils, even those that are a little cool, are suitable for the Apple tree; all exposures, except south, are favorable. High-stemmed trees are grafted onto egrin, while dwarf trees are grafted onto doucin and paradis. What we have said about growing and pruning Pear trees can, in all circumstances, be applied equally to Apple trees. They can easily be made to take on any shape. High-stemmed trees are the most durable and the most productive. We recommend pruning during the first few years. If planted in rows, they should be spaced 8 or 10 meters apart, and sometimes more, depending on the nature of the soil. In gardens, we usually plant only dwarf trees, which take up very little space and, if well managed, produce a lot in a very short time. In light, warm soils, apple trees grafted on doucin are preferred, as their roots grow deeper than when grafted on paradis. In the latter case, they don't grow very high, but fruit a lot, and the fruit is generally very beautiful. They should be planted about Im, 65 apart. As almost all their roots are on the surface of the soil, they need only be hoeed. In the article on Pear trees, we advised you to place a dwarf apple tree between each cattail; the most advantageous shape to give them is that of a goblet (fig. 24). In the first year, select five or six buds, spaced as far apart as possible, to form the framework of the tree, and trellis them on a hoop. The following year, the mother branches are pruned according to their strength, and always on an eye placed so as to extend them in the same direction. E B Fig. 24 - Goblet apple tree. When disbudding, we pinch off any buds growing inwards, so as to hollow out the inside of the tree; then we also pinch off any buds growing outwards. A second hoop will be placed to support the extension of the branches, and branches will have to be established to fill the gaps left by the spacing of the mother branches. Trees that grow too vigorously generally need to be pruned longer and much later. The boxwood borders of the kitchen garden can be advantageously replaced by young Paradise Apple trees, which are planted about 2 meters apart, and directed horizontally on a single branch (fig. 25.) Before planting, mark the place of each tree with a stake designed to support the wire to which the Apple trees are attached. These stakes must be driven in so that the wire can be tensioned at Om, 40 or Om, 50 from the ground. Suce fe-agn-poay na ee "s h-c Fig. 25 - Cords of apple trees. After planting, the stem of each tree is carefully bent, held along the wire by means of a few wicker ligatures, then the longer or shorter end is cut off, depending on the tree's vigor. To obtain two branches on each apple tree, simply cut the stem below the wire after planting, and the following year direct one branch to the right and one to the left. In subsequent years, all lateral branches are pruned as close as possible to their insertion, to encourage the development of fruit buds; then the cords of Om, 40 to Om 50, are extended, depending on the vigour of the tree, until they have reached a length that allows them to be grafted onto each other, so as to form a single cord. We recommend forming apple hedges whenever you need to fence in areas where you don't have to worry about the devastation of the fruit; graft trees onto doucin, and plant them about 1 m apart. During the first few years, the inclination of the limbs will be maintained by attaching them to stilts placed far apart. Any main branches that cross will be grafted by approach, and the others will be interlaced. At the time of disbudding, the hedge should be pinched on both sides; then, by pruning and disbudding, we'll try to prevent it from thinning out at the bottom. Varieties. - Api, - Calville white. - Red C. - Fenouillet gris, - Pigeonnet. - Winter pigeon. - Gros Rambour. - Reinette de Hollande. - R. franche, - R. du Canada, - R. d'Angleterre, - R. dorée, - R. grise, - R. de Bretagne, - R. d'Espagne, - R. de Granville. PRUNIER (Prunus). - Plum trees, which have tracing roots, do not require very deep soil; they grow well almost everywhere, provided the soil is neither too dry nor too wet. They are grafted on the Prunier Saint-Jullien or on the Damas Noir; and, although they do well in all forms, they are not planted on any other tree.

Espalier trees are rarely used, except in regions where the fruit does not ripen well, when they need to be placed in a good position. It is much more advantageous to plant high-stemmed trees. If lines are to be formed, they should be placed 6 meters apart. Several species can also be planted to form interior hedges, or in places where you don't have to fear the devastation of fruit. Pruning is not absolutely necessary, except in the first few years, to form the framework of the tree. Forced plum cultivation. - Several varieties, such as Monsieur hâtif, Mirabelle and Reine-Claude, can easily be brought forward to maturity. To do this, in autumn, plant 30 young plum trees in Om pots, raised in the form of small cattails; choose them to be as branched as possible, and after potting, bury the pots in a well exposed spot. The following year, in January, place them in a glass greenhouse, where the temperature should not exceed 12° or 14°. Provide air when the sun is shining, and cover the greenhouse at night with straw mats. Other care consists in watering these trees as required, and syringing them from time to time, after they have defoliated; the fruit will be ripe in the first days of May. The same plum trees can be heated two or three years in a row. Varieties. - Early yellow. - Monsieur, - Royale de Tours. - Reine-Claude. - R.-C. Coëgolden drop. - R. C violet. - R.-C. monstrueuse de Bavay, - Mirabelle grosse et petite, - Impériale blanche. - Surpasse-Monsieur, - Jefferson, - Drap d'or d'Esperen. - Washington. - Gros Damas, - Perdrigon rouge. - de Sainte-Catherine, - de Saint-Mactin, - Pond's Seedling. VINE (Vitis vinifera). - Vines grow in almost any type of soil, and there are few places where they cannot produce fine, good grapes if planted in a favorable, well-governed location. However, the best soil is frank, soft and deep, amended from time to time with well-consumed fertilizers. Before planting a Vine espalier, it is necessary to decide on the form in which it will be trained, taking into account the location: for example, to line the overhangs of an orangery or any other building, the Vine can be trained as a palmette; but in all other circumstances, we recommend training it in the Thomery style (fig. 26), which - --- -Oe=-s U . 2- -C = 2 -- ~~~ - 2- -Con -=- -A = ☙E-I=A - Co- - ~ ---- - ☙ -- ~ . -- - 0 -~ A -dmanees -u - -====== ~~~~~ - ~ - - - ---===-- - 2 -=AT - -A -2 - 0 2- -o---A.=A ~ Eo . -7 - - -2 -----i - e === - DE ~ 0 - - - E o - - - R, - -Mo- - 2u - ommi ~ ~~ = ☙ gu - S - -=----- ~ -26 -mO - = - -P. - Aa ~ - - - "- == 2 6a . -6=- - 8 E I ~~ - Vigne à la Thomery. 4 96 6 mode de plantation offrant tous les avantages désirables. 1. Planting. - The distance to be observed between each Vine depends on the nature of the soil; in shallow or poor soil, Vines should be planted closer together than in good soil, in order to give less extension to each member; finally, in soil where satisfactory vegetation can be expected, plant as follows: every Om, 65, make a trench about Om, 33 wide and Om, 35 deep; start it Im, 33 or Im, 65 from the wall, depending on the length of the marcottes, then continue it up to Om, 65 from the wall. In autumn, we'll take rooted marcottes or crossettes (but then we'd need another year to reach the wall); we'll leave each marcotte a single throw, but with all its eyes, even on the part that has to lie in the ground. Place the marcotte in the trench, so that the end that is to emerge from the ground at Om, 65 from the wall is furnished with good eyes, and, to bring it out of the ground, bend it very carefully so as not to break it. The roots should be placed in loose soil, then covered with good earth, as should the part lying in the trench. A bed of good manure should be placed on top and the trench filled with soil. In February or March, we prune all marcottes to two or three eyes above the ground, taking care to cut only a few millimeters above the eye, and to ensure that the bevel of the cut is always opposite the terminal eye. Choose the most vigorous buds from among those that develop, and remove the others to encourage their growth. Tie each bud to it, taking care to remove false buds as they develop. 2. Pruning. - First year. - In the following autumn, the trenches are continued up to the wall and to the same depth as the previous year; then each vine is laid down and moved close to the wall to the place it is to occupy, which must have been marked in advance. When the time is right, all the vines are pruned to two or three eyes above the ground. The buds are trained on the wall as they develop, taking care to remove the false buds as soon as they reach Om, 12 or Om, 15 in length. If the year is favorable, it is likely that each bud will produce a few bunches, so if there are too many, remove some of them, to avoid putting too much strain on the young vines. Second year. - The top bud is cut back onto the one below it, unless it is too weak, as it is always necessary to prune on the most vigorous; then all the a stems are pruned on an eye placed a few centimetres below the first cordon, which is established at Om, 25 from the ground. If the b-stems, at the height of the second cordon, which should be at Om, 75 from the ground, are furnished with eyes which, by their size, promise vigorous buds, they will be pruned like the a-stems. Stems c, d and e are pruned according to their vigor, and in subsequent years, the cords of stems c are set at Im, 25 from the ground, those of stems d at Im, 75, and finally those of stems e at 2m, 25. In general, to cut back on good eyes, the current year's shoot should be cut back to half its length, unless the first or second eye above is at cordon height, on the part of the stem that will be extended each year until it has reached the height at which it should form a cordon. Three or four buds are left on each stem, chosen from among those with the most bunches; they are trellised as they develop, and, as in other years, the false buds are removed, then all the other buds are pinched off very short, and at the next pruning they are removed from the ground floor of the stem. Third year. - The following year, prune the vertical buds of stems which, in the previous year, had been cut back to the height of the cordon, from their insertion on the second eye, including the heel eye, giving rise to two buds, the two horizontal arms of which are formed; but they should only be brought gradually to the place they should occupy, and it would often be better to wait until autumn than risk breaking them. While they are growing, we tie them up as they develop, and remove the false buds, as well as all the buds in front and behind and those developing on the stems. Fourth year. - The two branches forming the cordon are pruned to about Om, 35 in length, depending on their vigour, but always on the eye most favourably placed to extend the cordon in the same direction. Then, to form the fruiting branches, we prune the buds placed on the upper part of the cordon, observing that they must have a distance between them of Om, 16 to Om, 20. As before, we remove all the false buds, as well as the badly placed buds, and we pinch off those closest to the end, in order to encourage the vegetation of the extension buds, which must be observed until the cordon has reached its full length. According to the spacing indicated for planting, each part of the cordon should be Ima, 60 long on each side. Fifth year. - The fruit-bearing branches are pruned back over two eyes, then each part of the cordon is extended by about Om, 35; and as, having reached this point, the care to be given to the Vine during vegetation is exactly the same as that previously indicated, we believe it unnecessary to deal with this subject at greater length. Sixth year. - For this pruning and those which follow, all fruit-bearing branches should be removed from the bud closest to the cordon, in order to rejuvenate them each year; then the spurs should be pruned on one or two eyes, as experience will indicate; for if, after pruning on one eye, buds are too vigorous, the following year they should be pruned on two eyes. Once each cordon has filled the space assigned to it, all that remains to be done is to maintain the balance of the sap, so as to ensure equal vegetation along the entire length of the cordon. To achieve this, you need to keep an eye on the buds at the end of the cordon (as they are always likely to attract a large quantity of sap), and, by pinching and trellising, force the sap to flow back towards the center. These notions, though succinct, are sufficient to illustrate the series of operations required to manage a trellis, and a careful study of the last prunings will help us to understand the others. 3. Palmette vine. - After bringing the vines to the place they are to occupy, they are all pruned three or four eyes above the ground; as soon as the buds have developed, the most vigorous are chosen, directed vertically, and, to encourage vegetation, the others are pinched off or removed. The following year, all the stems are cut back to about half their length, more or less, depending on their vigour; then, on each one, a bud is selected to extend the stem, then the buds placed on the right and left are pruned, taking care to incline them more or less, depending on whether you want to favour the development of one or restrict that of the other; and, as always, the buds placed in front and behind, as well as the false buds, are removed. In subsequent years, the buds on the right and left are pruned to form a two-eyed spur, then the vertical bud is cut back so as to obtain a few new buds each year, until the wall has been filled to its full height. 4. Vine in counter-spalier. - To form a counter-palier, we recommend following the same procedure as for the Thomery trellis, so as to represent the first two cords a, b (fig. 26). After planting, drive a stake in from a distance, and stretch a wire over it to guide each cordon, then another between the two to attach the buds of the first cordon, and finally a fourth at Om, 28 above the second cordon, to also attach the buds. You can heat part of it each year, as we indicated in Chapter II, concerning the layout of a garden; but you should only start after three or four years of planting, and then you can heat a quarter of the length each year, so that by the time you get to the first part, it will have had three years of renos. 5. Forced cultivation of the Vine. - Towards the end of December, in the course of January and even into February, depending on the maturity of the wood, the Vine is pruned; then, depending on its position, either panels are placed in front of it, if it is planted along a wall, or a small movable tarpaulin (1). If the vine is planted against a wall, a manure stove is placed around it and reworked if necessary, then the panels are put in place. However, much quicker results can be achieved by passing a stove pipe or, better still, a thermosiphon through the tarpaulin. This method of heating is very favorable to vegetation, and requires much less supervision to achieve good results. The temperature is regulated as follows: from the time you start heating until the vines begin to vegetate, you maintain a temperature of 15° to 18° in the cover; then you increase it by 4° to 5°, a temperature you maintain until the bunches are well formed, and during flowering you heat to 25° to 30°, but once the berries are well formed you gradually reduce the heat, so that you only have 18° to 20° until they are perfectly ripe. The temperature is all the easier to regulate, as the tarpaulin contains only a very small amount of air. During the night, we cover the panels with mats that we remove every day; and if, when the sun is shining, the thermometer rises higher than we've indicated, we provide a little air by lifting the panels from the top. To maintain the humidity necessary for (1) This tarpaulin consists of a box Om.80 wide by 1m.33 high at the back and Om.33 at the front. The spacing is maintained by bars dovetailed at the top and bottom, and positioned to support the panels. For grapevines, watering should be more or less frequent, depending on the temperature and the progress of vegetation. The water used for these waterings should be left under the tarpaulin for some time before use, so that it is, as far as possible, at the temperature of the atmosphere into which it is being poured. With the Vines treated in this way, the Grapes will be ripe after four or four and a half months, from the time when heating begins. To make use of the space left in front of the Vine, you can put in a few rows of potted Strawberry plants, which do very well at the same temperature. Varieties. - Madeleine noire, - M. blanche, - Chasselas de Fontainebleau, - Ch. de Bar-sur-Aube, - Ch. gros coulard. - Ch. musqué, - Ch. rose. - Muscat blanc, - M. violet, - M. d'Alexandrie, - Corinthe. - Panse commune, - Gromier du Cantal. - Frankental, - Gherkin, - Blackcurrant grape. CHAPTER XIV Tree Diseases. The diseases that attack trees and cause them to perish are due to two distinct causes: some, such as canker and gum, are the result of internal causes, while others are produced by external causes, and especially by the presence of parasitic plants such as Lichens, Mosses and Fungi. The former, which are often fatal, can be cured by amputating the diseased parts, if the tree is only partially attacked, or if the causes which produced them are temporary and have not destroyed any life seeds; but when the tree is invaded in its entirety and its vegetation is modified to the point where decay is a daily occurrence, the tree languishes and soon dies, without the gardener's help being able to save it. The same is not true of diseases caused by parasitic vegetation on the tree's epidermis; lotions with limewater and careful cleaning with a brush or blunt are usually enough to destroy lichens and mosses, and restore the tree's health. As for Fungi, a more energetic means is needed to destroy them, especially for the vine fungus known as Oïdium Tuckeri; lime water has been recognized as insufficient, and hydrosulfate of lime (1), applied in the proportion of 1 liter to 50 liters of water, is, up to now at least, the simplest and most effective means yet found. Flower of sulphur, applied after basking, is also known to destroy grapevine fungus, but whether flower of sulphur or hydrosulphate of lime is used, the key to success is to apply the treatment in good time, i.e. as soon as the first white traces of the disease are seen, as it spreads very rapidly. If, despite the care taken in the operation, the fungus is not destroyed by the first treatment, it should be repeated a few days later. Flower of sulphur and hydrosulphate of lime can also be used successfully to destroy the powdery mildew which is so damaging to peach trees. (1) To make hydrosulfate of lime, take 250 grams of powdered sulfur and about half a liter of freshly slaked lime; make a paste to which 3 liters of water are added. Place the mixture on the stove in a cast-iron or varnished earthenware pot, boil for about ten minutes and then bottle. Our science is limited to these inadequate notions, and the only means we have to prevent disease are attentive care, shelter in bad weather, and the choice of a good exposure. It is to be regretted that this important part of horticulture is so neglected, and that no one takes it seriously. CHAPTER XV The pleasure garden. For this part of the garden, which is subject to modifications depending on the location and shape of the land, as well as the owner's taste, we cannot go into the same details as for the vegetable garden, which has more fixed rules. Although walls are the best form of enclosure for all gardens, they are not essential for a pleasure garden, which can be enclosed by hedges. Although Hawthorn is considered the most suitable tree for a hedge, the following species can be used, depending on the nature of the terrain: White Acacia (Robinia), Judas tree, Acer campestre and Tataricum, Buplevrum fruticosum, Hornbeam, Cornus sanguinea, Clavalier (Xanthoxylum), Caragana arborea, Celtis occidentalis and australis; Cherry, Cypress, Spruce, Eleagnus angustifolia, Common Ash, Gleditschia Sinensis and triacanthos; Holly, Beech, 1f, Lilac, Lycium Barbarum; Maclura aurantiaca, Mulberry, Hazelnut, Elm, Prunus spinosa, incana, Mahaleb and insititia; Pear, Apple, Rhamnus 14 catharticus, Paliurus, hybridus, sempervirens and Alaternus; Elder, Common Privet, Thuja, Viburnum lantana. When a hedge is established, it should be protected from the outside by a dead hedge or a ditch wide and deep enough to protect it from the teeth of livestock. As fences, hedges are less unpleasant to look at than walls, and they allow you to take advantage of every view, an immense advantage in the composition of a pleasure garden. The study of the site's position should aim to provide everything that can contribute to a pleasing perspective, and to conceal the areas that were intended to be hidden. Nothing is more disgraceful in a pleasure garden than the disproportion between its different parts; paths, lawns, beds, ponds, everything must be in proportion to the extent of the land. The trees planted in the beds should not be scattered at random, but in order of their elevation; they should be spaced far enough apart so that vegetation is not hindered. They should be grouped in such a way as to harmonize foliage and flowers, producing a pleasing impression on the eye. We can't recommend enough the introduction into pleasure gardens, instead of useless trees, of high-stemmed fruit trees, such as cherry, apricot, plum, apple, pear, almond, coignass, etc., preferably placed at the top of the beds, so that they are not suffocated by the vegetation of neighbouring trees, and so that they enjoy the air and sunshine first. Many people have been deterred from planting fruit trees by the fear of seeing part of their harvest devoured by birds, and of having only mediocre-sized fruit. This consideration should not be a reason for exclusion: Moreover, fruit trees are not inferior to ornamental trees, both in the beauty of their foliage and in the brilliant color and abundance of their flowers, and they have, moreover, fruits that are as pleasing to the eye as the bunches of Sorbier, the fruit of Mespilus, of Sureaux, etc. Green trees and all the evergreens that provide a harsh, but reminiscent verdure in mid-winter, should also find a place in a pleasure garden. Cedar of Lebanon, Larch, Spruce Fir, Distichous Cypress, the great trees of our forests, such as Beech and Birch, can be planted in isolation and serve to break the monotony of straight lines. When establishing a pleasure garden, the driveway around it should not be too close to the fence, so as to conceal as much as possible the extent of the property. The main paths should be wider than the others, and regularity should be avoided when laying them out. Longer or shorter curves and sinuosities that conceal the path are essential to remove from a garden of this kind the monotonous symmetry of our old public gardens. Depending on the size of the garden, a few rustic buildings can be erected here and there, and green rooms created, where you can arrive unexpectedly in the course of your walk, and you mustn't neglect to place benches from distance to distance, and especially in places where you've created views. Whatever the size of the property, there should always be a section of lawn in front of the house. The lawn should have graceful contours and harmonize with the surrounding areas. The middle of the lawn should be hollowed out to produce a more natural effect, and the edges should be planted with small clumps, so as to contribute to the overall effect without obscuring the view. In France, English Rye-grass is most often used to establish a lawn, with a small amount of Dutch White Clover added; In England, however, Hye-grass has long since been abandoned in favor of Lawn's grass, a mixture of creeping bentgrass, meadow cretelle, meadow bromegrass, sheep's fescue, creeping fescue, sweet vernal grass, meadow grass, English Rye-grass, creeping bentgrass and white clover. Mixed in the right proportions, these plants produce just as good an effect as Rye-grass, and have the advantage of lasting much longer. Although these mixtures are suitable for almost all soils, in cooler soils, Ovine and Creeping Fescues should be replaced by Meadow Fescue, which rises a little higher, it's true, but is more suited than the other species to wet soils. In dry soils, instead of English Ryegrass, you can also sow Italian Ryegrass, which succeeds in the worst conditions. Under tall trees, it's best to sow Paturin des bois, alone or with a little Agrostis tracante, Fétuque ovine, Fétuque hétérophylle and Fétuque des prés, grasses that also do well in the shade. In large gardens, where lawns are often very extensive, you can, after consulting the nature of the terrain, replace the turf with a genuine natural meadow whose hay can be fed to livestock; but you must then choose plants that are suited to the nature of the soil for which they are intended. The best time to sow a lawn is September in light soils, or March in heavy or damp soils; but whatever the time, before sowing, the ground must be well prepared, which means breaking up the clods of earth and removing all roots and stones; then, depending on the nature of the soil, plough more or less deeply. If an old lawn is destroyed to sow a new one, it must be turned over with a spade, so that it is buried to a depth of at least Om, 35; after ploughing, the soil must be harrowed with a fork, then the stones and clods on the surface removed with a rake, so that the soil is perfectly even. If the poor quality of the soil makes it necessary to add fertilizers, they should only be used if they are well consumed. Sowing should only be done when the weather is fine, because of the operations that must follow. The seeds are broadcast, and immediately after sowing, lightly harrowed with a fork; then rolled or, failing that, trodden down with the feet, if the area is not too large; then covered with a very light layer of earth or very fine loam. To keep a lawn in good condition for a long time, it must be cut often. The first cut should be made at the beginning of May, and the last at the end of October or the beginning of November. It's impossible to say how many cuts will have to be made between these two periods, as this will depend on the state of humidity 14. in which they are kept. During the summer drought, watering should be very frequent, and should be done in the morning or evening, using apple watering cans. After each cut, rake the soil to remove anything that might cause rot, and then roll it. Every year, after the last cut, remove the moss with the rake, and spread a light layer of potting soil throughout. Since sowing grass on embankments or benches can only be done with great difficulty, it's best to use sods that have been lifted from meadows or roadsides; fit them next to each other, fix those in a vertical position with small wooden pins, then press them down lightly with a small bat. The best time to do this is usually in March. For small lawns, instead of sowing grasses, you can plant remontant Roses, whose branches are fixed to the ground after spreading them out in all directions. When the ground is completely covered, it's impossible to see anything more ravishing than a lawn of Roses, especially when the colors have been well varied in the planting. Shrubs that flower as long as possible should be massed on the edges of the lawn, and a few beautiful green trees, such as Cedar of Lebanon, Cedar Deodora, Abies Pinsapo, Cryptomeria Japonica, Pinus Sabiniana, Pinus Lambertiana, Taxodium sempervirens, etc., can be thrown in at random. Weeping Willow, Weeping Ash, Purple Beech, variegated Negundo, etc., and, among the lower plants, Tree Peonies, Gynerium argenteum, Bambusa edulis et aurea, Andropogon formosum, Saccharum Maddeni, Rheum, Gunnera scabra, Heracleum, Caladium and Wigandia, also produce a beautiful effect on lawns. The current widespread practice of establishing beds of one or more species of ornamental plants in the pleasure garden is in very good taste, in that it enables the full ornamental value of a host of plants to be appreciated, which only produce their full effect when they occur in groups. Amateurs unfamiliar with the resources offered by horticulture for this kind of decoration are often at a loss when it comes to choosing which plants of each season to use to form beds. In this respect, here are a few indications to help guide them: First decoration. Dutch crocuses, Dutch hyacinths, single-flowered tulips, double-flowered tulips, single-flowered anemones, auriculas, garden primroses, spring hepatica, large-flowered pansies, Aubrietia deltoidea, Cynoglosse omphalode, Corbeille d'or, Thlaspi vivace, Ellébore rose de Noël, Héliotrope d'hiver, Tussilago suaveo'ens, Saxifrage de Sibérie, Doronicum Caucasicum, Silene pendula, Nemophila, Phlox verna, Giroflées jaunes de diverses nuances, l'ieds-d'alouette; Spring Tourette, Myosotis alpestris, Double-flowered Poppies, Annual Lunaria, Dielytra spectabilis and Iris Germanica. This list includes plants of varying heights, from dwarf Dutch Crocus to larkspur and medium-sized poppies, so there's enough to form clumps of any size, depending on the size and location of the garden they're to form part of. To give just one example of what can be done with these plants, a bed of Spring Tourettes, Siberian Saxifrages and Doroniques produces a charming effect in spring. Second trim. N? 1: Mexican Ageratum with a double row of yellow Calceolaria and red Verbena. These three plants, arranged in concentric circles of amethyst-blue, bright yellow and deep red, produce a very nice effect; the other lists are calculated with the same aim of associating the shapes and colors that best match each other, and most pleasingly flatter the eye. 2. Yellow Calceolaria with a row of blue-flowered Lobelia Erinus. 3. Dwarf Nasturtiums with a row of blue Verbena. 4. White-flowered Chrysanthemums with a row of red Geranium or red Verbena. 5. Cuphea ignea with a row of Cerastium tomentosum. 6. Fuschia with a row of variegated Geranium or white Verbena. 7. Red Geranium with a double row of white Petunia and yellow Calceolaria. 8. Red Geranium with a double row of white Petunia and Ageratum Mexicanum. 9. Red or pink Geranium with a row of white Verbena. 10. Variegated Geranium with a row of blue or red Verbena. 11. Lantana Camara with a row of Lantana delicatissima. 12. variegated mint with a row of red Verbena. 13. Perilla Nankinensis with a row of variegated Geranium or white Verbena. 14. Petunia roses with a double row of white-flowered Chrysanthemums and red Geranium. 15. Salvia fulgens with a row of Ageratum Mexicanium. 16. Trianon Marigold with a double row of white Petunia and red Geranium. 17. Tagetes lucida or yellow Calceolaria with a double row of red Geranium and Nierembergia gracilis. 18. Red verbenas with a double row of Nierembergia gracilis and blue-flowered Lobelia Erinus. 19. White verbenas with one row of blue-flowered Lobelia Erinus. 20. Peruvian heliotrope. 21. Coleus Werschaffelti with one row of Cineraria maritima or Centaurea candidissima. 22. Canna discolor. 23. Caladium esculentum. 24. Wigandia Caracasana. 25. Various dahlias. In large gardens, you can also form groups of annuals and sow them immediately in place to avoid cultivation costs. By successively replacing the plants that make up the second group with Chinese Chrysanthemums, we can have, by adopting the rotation we propose, flowerbeds filled with flowers all year round. As long descriptions only serve to enlarge a book, we felt it necessary to follow a different plan in our book from that adopted in other treatises on cultivation: we preferred to group plants according to the place they should occupy in a garden, indicating briefly, for each of them, the time of sowing, that of flowering, the color of the flower and the height of the plant. However, we have devoted a special article to plants whose cultivation requires particular care. SECTION I. - Shrubs for borders. BOXWOOD-A BORDERS. -To establish a border, take clumps of Boxwood and divide them into as many splinters as possible. In autumn or spring, plant these splinters in a thin, regular line, taking care to leave only the tips of the branches above ground. The following year, in September or February, i.e. before or after sprouting, the box tree is pruned back with large scissors, an operation that must be carried out every year; otherwise, it will overgrow. CLIMBING IVY. - For planting under tall trees, where it's always so difficult to have greenery, there's nothing better than climbing ivy or one of its varieties. All can be grown as borders; but the variety known as Irish Ivy is more suitable than the others, because of the beauty of its foliage. To establish a border, in autumn or spring, plant young ivy grown in pots or, failing that, rooted marcottes, which are arranged to line the ground. To facilitate root development along the entire length of the branches, fix them to the ground using small wooden plugs similar to those used for marcotting Carnations. Once recovery is assured, all care consists in cutting off or fixing to the ground all young shoots, so as to always have regular borders. ROSES. - Pompoms and varieties known as Miss Lawrence Roses, with white, pink or crimson flowers, can be grown in borders. True miniatures, the latter grow to no more than Om, 12 to Om, 15. More vigorous, Pump Roses should be preferred; as they are more resistant to the disadvantages of all kinds that border plants have to put up with. 2. Clump-forming perennials. ALYSSE CORBEILLE-D'OR, THLASPI jaune (Alyssum saxatile) - Yellow flowers in April and May; propagation from spring flushes or from seed sown immediately after maturity. AUBRIÈTIE deltoïde (Aubrietia deltoidea), AlySSe deltoïde (Alyssum deltoideum). - Numerous light-blue flowers in spring and summer. Aubriéties form large, low clumps that are replanted every two or three years. ASPERULE odorante (Asperula odorata). - Height, Om, 20 to Om, 30; fragrant white flowers, May; propagated by separation. ASTER Reversii. - In September and October, extremely abundant, meaty white flowers. This charming little plant can be propagated by separating clumps, like all other Asters. BERMUDIAN with pelite flowers (Sisyrinchium Bermudiana). - Height, Om, 18 to Om, 20; white flowers, in June and July; multiplication by seed or by the shattering of plants. BRUNELLE à grandes fleurs (Brunella grandiflora). - Blue, purple, pink or white flowers in July; propagates from seed, sown in March, or from splinters in autumn. CAMPANULA gazonnante (Campanula cespitosa). - All summer long, small but numerous blue or white flowers. Campanula Carrathica and Bocconi can also be grown in borders. All multiply by separating clumps. cottony cedar, ARGENTINA (Cerasttum tomentosum). - In May and June, white flowers; multiplication by seed or traces. SPRING CYNOGLOSS (Cynoglossum Omphalodes). Spring OMPHALODE (O. verna). - Charming little plant that can be placed in the shade. Blue flowers in March and April. Trace propagation. Caucasian DORONIC (Doronicum Caucasicum). - Yellow flowers from March to May; offshoot multiplication in autumn. GENTIANE acaule (Gentiana acaulis). - Very beautiful blue flowers in April and May; multiplication by the bursting of the stems. GRAMINES. - English Rye-gross is one of the most frequently used grasses for sowing borders. However, Agrostis tracante, Sheep's Fescue and Yellow Fescue are just as suitable for this purpose as English Rye-grass. JAPANESE HEMEROCALL (Hemerocallis Japonica, Funkia subcordata). - In August, white, very fragrant flowers. Varieties with blue flowers. Multiplication by separation. Spring hepatica (Anemone hopatica). - From February to March, white, pink or blue flowers, single or double, depending on variety; multiplication by splitting in autumn. Inis d'Allemagne (Iris Germanica). - See article on growing this plant. LIPPIA REPENS. A perennial suitable for lawns on slopes. Hardy, it produces numerous lilac flowers from June to October. Multiplies in any season. LYCHNIDE laciniée (Lychnis flos cuculi). - From June to September, red or white flowers, similar to small carnations. Double-flowered varieties. Multiplication by separating clumps in February. ŒILLET Mignardise (Dianthus moschatus, D. plumarius). - Red, white or pink flowers, single or double, in May and June; multiplication in August by marcottes, without incision. OPHIOPOGON du Japon, Turquoise grass. Grassy plant, suitable for edging dry soils. Multiplies in autumn or spring. Daisy (Bellis perennis). - Perennial; double, white, pink, red or variegated flowers; replant every year after flowering. 15 PHLOX subulata (Phlox subulata). - Pink flowers, marked with a purple star, from April to May; multiply by dividing clumps. PHLOX with narrow leaves (P. setacea). - Pink or purple flowers, spotted with red, in June or July. Varieties with white flowers; same propagation. BEARS EAR (Primula auricula). - Flowers in April and May; very numerous varieties obtained by sowing in February and March; seeds need little covering. Garden Primrose (P. veris). - In March; single or double flowers of all shades; multiplication from splinters, in autumn, or from seeds sown immediately after maturity. PYRETHRUM INODORUM. - In June and July, very attractive double white flowers; propagation by cuttings or splinters. Mountain SABLINE (Arenaria montana). - Height, Om, 20; in June, white flowers; propagation by traces. Sabline grandiflora (A. grandiflora). - Height, Om, 05 or Om, 06; white flowers; same propagation. SALVIA tricolor, a charming variety of Salvia officinalis, with red and yellow variegated leaves. Easy to propagate by cuttings. SAXIFRAGE ombreuse (Saxifraga umbrosa). - White flowers in April and May; all are propagated by separation in autumn or February. SAXIFRAGE mousseuse, Turkish grass (S. hypnoides). - White flowers in May. Geranioid SAXIFAGE (geranioides). - White flowers in May. SEDUM. - All Sedums can be grown in borders; they are hardy and look good, especially Pul. chellum. Sedum acre and cruciatum are also suitable for this purpose. Statice Armeria, common ARMERIA (Armeria vulgaris). - In May, June and July, red, white or lilac flowers. Cutting off the flowers as soon as they have faded gives a second bloom, often just as abundant as the first. Statice bellidifolia can also be grown in borders. Like Statice Armeria, it can be propagated by planting splinters in autumn or spring. THLASPI perennial (Iberis semperflorens.) White flowers in April and May; multiplication in summer by cuttings or marcottes. SPRING TURTLE (Turritis verna,) SPRING ARABETT (Arabis verna). - White flowers in March and April; trace propagation in autumn. fragrant violets (Viola odorata). - Dark blue, white or pink flowers, from February to April; multiplication by seed or, better still, from fragments of plants, in autumn. VITTADINIA triloba. - All summer long, single, white, pinkish flowers, exactly like small daisies; propagation by cuttings, taken in August and September, and overwintered in a greenhouse. 3. Bulbous plants for borders. Golden Am (Allium moly). - Attractive yellow flowers in June; white-flowered variety; clove propagation. AMARYLLIS jaune (Amaryllis lutea) - Yellow flowers in September. CROCUS, Spring saffron (Crocus vernus). - In February and March, yellow, blue or white flowers, or white with purple stripes, depending on the variety, which are very numerous; plant in autumn, to raise every three years. Common GLAILE (Gladiolus communis). - White or red flowers from May to June; plant in autumn. POET'S NARCISSUS (Narcissus poetieus). - In May, white flowers with purple crown; sweet fragrance: double-flowered varieties. Plant in October. Meadow Narcissus (N. Pseudo-Narcissus). - Yellow, double flowers in April; same propagation. OXALIDE de Deppe (Oxalis Deppei). - Red flowers, all summer; plant in spring, to be raised each year in autumn. 4. Annual plants that can be propagated from seed. CAMPANULE miroir-de-Vénus (Campanula speculum), SpECULAIRE miroir-de-Vénus (Specularia speculum) -Violet or white flowers in May, June and July; sow in place in September or spring. COLLINSIA de deux couleurs (Collinsia bicolor). - Lilac or white flowers all summer; sow outdoors in September or spring. Varieties. - Graudiflora, - Multicolor. COLLOMIA scarlet (Collomia coccinea). - Height, Om, 25; scarlet-red flowers all summer; sow outdoors in September or spring. CRÉPIS rose (Creris rubra) BARKHAUSIErouge (Barkhausia rubra). - Height, Om. 25. All summer, pink or white flowers; sow in place or in nurseries, for transplanting in autumn or spring. FLAX LEAF CYNOGLOSS (Cynoglossum linifolium). - Biennial; height, Om, 50, white flowers, June to August; sow in place, September or spring. KAULFUSSIE amelloïde, CHARIEIS à feuilles variées (Charieis heterophylla). - Height, Om, 20; blue flowers all summer; sow in place in spring. JULIENNE de Mahon, GIROFLÉE de Mahon (Cheirantus maritimus, Malcolmia maritima). - Flowers first red, then purple, in June and July; white-flowered variety; sow outdoors in autumn or spring. LEPTOSIPHON androsaceus (Leptosiphon androsaceus), GILIE androsacea (Gilia androsacea). - Height, Om, 25; blue or white flowers; all summer, sow in place in September or spring. Dense-flowered LEPTOSIPHON (L. densiflorus), Dense-flowered GIlie (Gilia densiflora). - Height, Om, 30 to Om, 35; all summer, light pink flowers fading to light blue; same propagation. LEPTOSIPHON AUREUS. - Round tuft, similar to Statice armeria. Attractive golden-yellow flowers all summer long; seed multiplication, sown in September in pots and overwintered under glass. ORCHIS-flowered LINAIRE (Linaria bipartita). - Height, Om, 45; bluish-purple flowers in summer; sow in September or spring. LOBELIE Erine (Lobelia Erinus). - Charming little plant with blue, white or pink flowers, forming large flower-covered clumps that follow one another throughout the summer. Lobelia are sown in March and April, in beds, or better still in September; the plant is then transplanted into pots and overwintered in a frame, Nemesia floribunda. - Height, Om, 40, lilac, yellow and white flowers all summer; sow in place in September or spring. Remarkable Nemophila (Nemophila insignis). - Height, Om, 33; beautiful blue flowers all summer; sow in place in September or spring. Spotted Nemophila (N. maculata). - White flowers, widely spotted with blue; same cultivation. Dwarf larkspur (Delphinium Ajacis). - Height, Om 35 to Om, 40. In May, June and July, double flowers in a wide range of colors; single-flowered plants should always be removed; sow in place in autumn or in February and March. REINE-MARGUERITE hâtive (Aster Sinensis, Callistephus hortensis). - Height, Om, 33; in bloom from July to September; sow from March to June in nurseries, then plant out. Creeping SANVITALIA (Sanvitalia procumbens). - Sow in beds in March. Once transplanted, this plant spreads naturally over the soil and produces an abundance of charming single or double yellow flowers with brown discs. SAPONAIRE de Calabre (Saponaria Calabrica). - All summer long, pink or white flowers, smaller than those of the Silenas, with which they are closely related. Propagate from seed, sown in September in a nursery, or in March and April. SCHIZANTE étalé (Shizanthus porrigens). - Height, Om, 30, lilac and yellow flowers, punctuated with brown, all summer; sow in spring, in the field or in a nursery, to be later transplanted. SILENE with pink flowers (Silene bipartita). - Height, Om 22 to Om 28; pink flowers, in June and July; sow in September, in nursery, or in spring, immediately in place. SECTION II. - Plants for beds and flowerbeds. Annuals and biennials. ABRONIA UMBELLATA, beautiful plant with lilac-pink flowers, somewhat related to valerian. Multiplication from seed, sown in March on a bed. ACROCLINIUM ROSEUM, a new species of immortelle, with satiny-pink flowers, larger than those of Rhodanthe Manglesii. Multiplication from seed, sown on a bed in February and March. ADONIDE d'été (Adonis œstivalis). - Om 30 stems; dark red flowers in June and July; sow in place in autumn or spring. AGERATUM MEXICANUM. - Biennial plant with blue flowers in a terminal corymb. Planted in May, Ageratum Mexicanum produces flowers for most of the year; propagate from seed or cuttings in August and September, and overwinter indoors. This charming plant is particularly suitable for bedding or as a wide border around Canna. AGERATUM CORULEUM. - Also an annual with blue flowers, it can be sown in beds in March, or in the ground in April; it has the same uses as Ageratum Mexicanum. ALYSSUM MARITIMUM (Koniga maritima). - Clump of Om, 20 to Om, 30, covered with small, fragrant white flowers in umbels, from May to November. Sow in March and April, immediately in place; suitable for forming clumps. AMARANTE tail-de-renard (Amarantus caudatus). - Stems from Om, 65 to I meter. Long clusters of red and yellow flowers all summer. Sow indoors in March or outdoors in April. AMARANTE mélancolique (A, melancholicus). - Angular stems from Om, 75 to l meters, blood-red glabrous leaves, rather insignificant light-green flowers; same cultivation. The variety cultivated under the name Amarantus melancholicus ruber is colored with a brighter hue, and is lower than the previous one. AMARANTUS BICOLOR and AMARANTUS TRICOLOR are two plants remarkable for the rich coloring of their leaves; they are considered varieties of Amarantus melancholicus and are grown in the same way. AMARANTE with red leaves (A. sanguineus). - Stem Om 90, washed or streaked with purple, blood-red leaves, purpurine flowers in elongated spikes; same cultivation as the preceding varieties. ANAGALLIS à grandes fleurs (Anagallis grandiftora). - Blue, pink or red flowers all summer and autumn. Anagallis are sown in spring, on a bed, or in September; they are then transplanted into pots and overwintered under a frame. Grown as a greenhouse plant, Anagallis can be kept for several years. Large-flowered ARGEMONE (Argemone grandiflora). - Height, Om, 60 to 1 m; white flowers all summer; sow in place in spring. Garden BALSAMINE (Balsamina hortensis), IMPATIENT BALSAMINE (Impatiens Balsamina). - Succulent stems, about Om, 50; from July to October, white, yellowish, red, pink, violet, flax-gray, single-colored or dotted flowers. We grow a large-flowered, very double race called Camellia, and another dwarf race with equally double and varied flowers: those called Balsamine à rameaug are much taller than the others. They can be sown in the garden in March, or in the open ground in April. Balsamines don't require any special care, but as they vegetate very vigorously in good soil, you need to pinch off the ends of the stems when you see that they exceed ordinary proportions, in order to obtain beautiful flowers. Glandular BALSAMINE (I. glanduligera). - Stems from 1 to 2 meters long; purplish-blue flowers in July; sow on a bed in spring, or better still, in the open ground, as soon as the seeds have ripened. GOLDEN BARTON (Bartonia aurea). - Twiggy stems, approx. Om, 60; beautiful yellow flowers all summer; sow in place in spring. BELLE-DE-JOUR, tricolor LISERON (Convolvulus tricolor). - Height, Om, 33; blue, white or variegated flowers from June to September; sow outdoors in April. BELLE-DE-NUIT FAUX JALAP (Mirabilis Jalappa, Nyctago hortensis). - Om.50 stems; red, white, yellow or variegated flowers from July to September. Propagates from seed, sown in April, or from roots, which are kept like Dahlias. BRACHYCOME à feuille d'ibéris (Brachycome iberidifolia). - Height, Om, 25; all summer, blue or white flowers; 15. sow in March and April, or better in September, in pots, which are wintered under frame. HIGH BROUAILE (Browallia elata). - Height, Om, 65; blue flowers from July to September; sow in spring. Drop-stemmed BROUAIL (B. demissa). - Twiggy stems from Om, 33; bluish-purple flowers, July to September; same cultivation CACALIE à feuilles hastées (Cacalia sagittata), EMILIE à feuilles hastées (Emilia sagittata). - Height, Om, 40; orange-red flowers from July to September; sow in March, on a bed, or in the field in April. Chilean chard (Beta cycla, Var, chiliensis) - Rightly considered an excellent vegetable plant, Chilean chard is nonetheless a beautiful ornamental that can be included in the variegated mix. In either case, however, make sure that each plant is spaced 1 m apart in all directions. CALANDRINE umbellate (Calandrinia umbellata). - Height, Om, 20 to Om, 25; lovely pink-purple flowers, all summer; sow in spring. - Calandrinia Lindleyana and speciosa can be grown in the same way. CAMPANULE à grosses fleurs, VioleTre marine (Campanula medium). - Biennial; stem Om, 65; from June to August, blue, white, pink, single or double flowers; sow in June. CAmpaNUle pyramidale (C. pyramidalis). - Biennial; height, 1.30 to 1.60 m; blue or white flowers, July to September; sow immediately when ripe. CAPUCINA grande (Tropœolum majus). - Previously grown as a climber, the yellow-flowered and brown-flowered Nasturtium grande is ideal for bedding in large gardens. For this purpose, nasturtiums are sown in clumps in spring, and the stems are left to creep along the ground. Dwarf CAPUCIN (T. minus). - The varieties of this plant known as Tom-Pouce nasturtiums are much more suitable for bedding than the tall nasturtium. They are grown in exactly the same way. CENTRANTHUS MACROSIPHON. - An annual valerian with attractive red flowers, sown in September in the garden or in nurseries. It can also be sown in spring. Blooms in May and then again until July. COCK'S CROWN (Celosia cristata). - From June to September, red, violet or yellow flowers, depending on the variety; sow on a bed in spring; prick out in a nursery on a bed, and put back in the ground only in June and July. Celosia species. - Height from Om, 40 to Om, 50, from July to October; pink, red or yellow flowers in long spikes. Propagates from seed, sown in bed in February and March. Celosia is a truly remarkable plant for the abundance and freshness of its flowers. fragrant centaury, yellow beard (centaurea amberboi). - Stems from Om, 40 to Om, 50; large, yellow flowers from July to October; sow in spring. American CENTAURY (C. Americana). - Twiggy stems, 1 metre long; lilac-blue flowers, August and September; same cultivation as above, CENTAURÉE musquée, BARBeAu musqué (C. moschata). - Stems from Om, 40; purplish-white flowers, August and September; same cultivation. CENTAURÉE bluet, BARBEAU (C. cyanus). - Height, Om, 65 to 1 m; variety of all colors, except yellow; blooms in June and August; sow in place, in autumn or spring. Centaurea depressa is a more beautiful species, which can be grown in the same way. MANY-FLOORED CHENOSTOMA (Chenostoma polyanthum). - Pink, inconspicuous flowers all summer. Chenostoma are sown in beds in March and April, and transplanted in rows along the edges of flowerbeds. Ornamental leaf cabbage (Brassica oleracea var.). - Green curly cabbage, red curly cabbage, red variegated cabbage, lilac variegated cabbage, laciniate cabbage and palmetto can be considered true ornamental plants, thanks to the beauty of their leaves. They are sown in spring, like all other cabbages; and, as they are biennial, they can be sown only every two years. Om, 40; yellow flowers with brown disc; sow in spring. Chrysanthemum Burridgeanum and Dunnetti are two fine varieties of Chrysanthemum carinatum, which are grown in exactly the same way. Drummond's PHLOX (Phlox Drummondi). - All summer and autumn, white, pink or purple flowers of extremely varied shades. Phlox Drummond is sown in spring, on a bed, or better still in autumn, as soon as the seeds have ripened; when the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted into small pots, which are then overwintered in a greenhouse. In May, when there are no more frosts to worry about, plant out. Grown in rows or groups, there's really no plant more graceful than these charming little Phloxes. LARGE ALOUETTE (Delphinium Ajacis). - Height, Om, 70. In May, June and July, flowers of various colors, like those of the dwarf race; sow in place, in autumn, or in February and March. All summer long, flowers equally varied, single or double, depending on variety; sow in September, in the field or in a nursery, or in February and March, immediately in the field. PODOLEPIS with meaty flowers (Podolepis gracilis). - Hau. teur, Om, 50 to Om, 60, flowers of various shades, from pink to pure white, all summer; sow in spring. REINE-MARGUERITE (Aster Sinensis Callistephus hortensis). - We have a considerable number of varieties of all shades, the most remarkable of which are all related to the pyramidal race known as ReineMarguerite Truffaut, a true perfection in terms of shape, flower size and plant habit. Some have flat flowers, like the old race, but larger and very soft; others have petals slightly curved towards the center, giving the flowers a globular shape, and earning them the name Peony Queen Daisies. Reines-Marguerites are sown from March 15 to April 20, in beds or in the ground, and each plant is transplanted separately. Although they can grow in almost any soil, to produce beautiful Daisy-Reins, you need to grow them in soil well supplied with fertilizer. During the first few months of their growth, DaisyReins don't need much water, but a fortnight before flowering, they need to be watered more frequently and more abundantly. RESEDA odorata. - Sow in the spring and throughout the summer. CHINESE ROSE (Alcga rosea Sinensis). - Biennial; stems from l mêtre to Im, 30; from July to October, purple flowers, variegated with white; red-flowered variety; sow in beds in spring. SALPIGLOSSIS à feuilles sinuées (Salpiglossis sinuata). - Several varieties are related to this species, and all are now cultivated under the same name. They are sown in April, on a bed, or in May, immediately in place. They flower in July, and continue to flower until September. Cultivated in beds, Salpiglossis produce more effect than separately; by adding to the sowing a few seeds of the yellow-flowered species called aurea, you'll have all the colors this beautiful genus has to offer. SALVIA HORMINUM. - To enjoy the full beauty of this plant, so remarkable for its flowers with red, pink or violet bracts, instead of growing it in small clumps, it should be planted in long clumps. Schizanthus is sown in the first fortnight of September, and the plants are transplanted into pots and overwintered in frames. Alternatively, and much more simply, they can be sown in April, immediately in place; but plants sown at this time will never be as beautiful as those sown in autumn. GOLDEN ACHILLA (Achillea auréa). - Height, Om, 50; golden-yellow flowers, from July to September; for all this genus, seed multiplication, sown as soon as mature or by bursting the stems. Napellus). - Height, 1 m; dark blue flowers in May and June; white-flowered varieties. All Aconite species are poisonous; they multiply from seeds sown immediately after maturity, or by splintering. Species: - Anthora, - Eminens, - Rubicundum, - Speciosum. Alpine ACTEA (Actea spicata). Height, 1m, 30; white flowers in July and August; seed multiplication by sowing immediately after maturity, or by separating the plants. ALSTRŒMÈRE à fleurs tachées, Lis des Incas (Alstromeria pelegrina). - Stems from Om, 35, needing stakes; from June to October, white flowers marked with purpurine spots and several dark purple dots. ALSTREMER from Chile. - Chilean Alstrœmère has been sown for several years, producing a large number of varieties. They can all be grown in light soil; during winter, they need only be covered with leaves, which are removed towards the end of March or in early April. Alstrœmères can be propagated from seeds sown as soon as they reach maturity, either in the field or in pots, and overwintered under a frame. As these plants vegetate very early in autumn, they should be planted in September or October, otherwise they will not flower the following year. AMARYLLIS. - A large number of Amaryllis, considered as greenhouse plants, can, in the Paris climate, be grown in the open air. M. Aimé, from Versailles, who specially cultivates this beautiful genus, has, since 1845, had several beds of Amaryllis in the open ground, which vegetate and flower admirably. To grow Amaryllis in the ground, prepare a trench Om.25 deep, the length and width of which are proportionate to the number of onions you wish to plant. Put a good layer of leaves at the bottom and, after treading them well, fill the trench with soil if it's not too compact, heather soil or, failing that, plant sand and potting soil made from well-consumed leaves mixed in equal parts. When grown in the ground, Amaryllis should be planted deeper than in pots, and watered only when they are in full bloom. After flowering, they should not be watered, and as frost approaches, they should be covered with a layer of leaves, the amount of which should be increased according to the intensity of the cold. All varieties of Amaryllis pulverulenta and Amaryliis cinnamomea can be grown as described above. AMARYILIS with pink flowers (A. Belladora). - From July to October, pink flowers mixed with white; multiplication by cloves, which are removed in September or October, immediately after flowering, and replanted immediately; winter cover. AMARYLLIS à longues feuilles (A. longifolia). - White flowers in June and July; pink-flowered variety; plant in autumn; winter cover. AMARYLLIS, St. James lily (A. formosissima). - Bulbous plants with deep purple-red flowers in July and August; plant outdoors in spring, or in pots in autumn; clove propagation. ANCOLIA commune (Aquilegia vulgaris). - Height, 1 meter; in May and June, red, blue, purplish, white or pink flowers, single or double, depending on variety; multiplication by seed, sown as soon as mature, or by bursting the stems. ANCOLIE du Canada (A. Canadensis). - Height, Om, 40; saffron-red flowers. Siberian ANCOLIA (A. Sibirica). - Height, Om, 40; beautiful blue flowers. Florist's ANEMONE (Anemone coronaria). - Height, 16. Om. 20 to Om, 25; in May and June, single, semi-double or double flowers in a variety of colors. It likes light, substantial soil. It is propagated by seed and tuber. Seeds are sown in September, either in pots or in the ground, in a well-prepared bed; they are lightly covered with fine compost as frost approaches. The young plant should be secured with straw mats, which are laid out on gaulettes so that they are a few centimetres above the ground. Uncover whenever the weather permits; then, in spring, weed and water as necessary; when the leaves have dried out, we pull out the tubers to treat them as mature plants, which means planting in March and April, about Om, 05 deep and Om, 10 or Omi, 15 apart, depending on the strength of each. When you have a large enough number of Anemones of flowering strength, it is a good idea to plant only half of them, so that each tuber only flowers every two years, as the flowers are then more beautiful. ANEMONE Peacock's eye (A. pavonina). - Height, Om, 20 to Om 25; in April and May, red flowers, bluish in the center; multiplication by root separation, as for the following. ANEMONE with blue flowers (A. Apennina). - Blue flowers in March. ANEMONE with umbel (A. narcissiflora). - Height, Om, 33; white flowers, May. Japanese anemone (A. Japonica). - Height Om,50; from August to October, semi-double pink, purple or white flowers. This charming plant grows vigorously in the open ground; it adapts well to all types of soil and can be multiplied by dividing clumps in spring. CHRYSANTHÈME des Indes (Chrysanthemum Indicum, Pyrethrum Indicum). - Stems from Om, 65 to Im, 30; from October to January, flowers of all shades of purple, white and yellow, depending on the variety, which are very numerous; propagated by cuttings or splinters, in April; planted in the ground or in pots. They are watered from time to time to encourage growth, but once well rooted, watering should be sparing. In June, pinch off the tips of each stem, so as to keep the clumps full and low. As soon as the frosts approach, the potted plants must be brought into the greenhouses, if you want to enjoy the full beauty of their bloom, which is so late that it is rare for it not to be surprised by the frosts. The many varieties of this plant are divided into two races. One is cultivated under the name Chrysanthemum pompon. DAHLIA. - Stems from Om.50 to 2 meters; from June to frost, white, yellow, pink, purple flowers, changing from these colors to their most delicate and darkest shades. They thrive in any soil, but bloom better in light soil than in compact or smoky soil. Multiplication by sowing, separating, taking cuttings and grafting onto tubers. 1. Sowing. - Sow in warm soil in March and April; in May, prick out the seedling in the open ground, at a distance of about 1 meter; then treat it with the other Dahlias. 2. Separation. - Dahlias are placed on beds in March or April, and when they come into vegetation, the clumps are divided, ensuring that each detached tuber has a reproductive germ. 3. Cuttings. - You can start taking cuttings soon after the Dahlias start to grow, and continue until June; but whatever the time, only young buds should be taken, and removed as soon as they have reached Om,06 or Om,08 in length; after removing the lower leaves, they are transplanted separately into small pots which are placed on a warm layer. Water them lightly, then cover them with a cloche, which should be shaded when the sun is shining. This care should be continued until you are sure that the cuttings will recover, at which point you lift the bell gradually, removing it when they can stand the air without wilting. 4 - Grafting. - Transplants, like cuttings, must be made in sufficient time to form tubers that can survive the winter. For the manual operation, follow the instructions given in the article on Herbaceous grafting, page 74. Planting. - Dahlias should only be planted towards the end of May: if planted earlier, they often run out of flowers by September, which is the most favorable time for their beauty. They should be placed in a well-ventilated position, about 1m 65 apart. They are planted in the middle of a basin Om, 40 to Om, 50 in diameter, after which a good mulch of half-censored manure is spread around each plant, to conserve water for watering, which should be moderate in the early stages, then more frequent as the drought increases. When the Dahlias are around Om, 30 to Om, 40 in height17 , if there are several stems on each plant, choose the most vigorous and remove the others. Immediately secure it with a stake. When it has reached Om, 60 to 0.70, remove the branches that are too close to the ground, or those that cross over each other, so as to leave only six or eight branches arranged around the main stem. If, at the height indicated, the stem is without branches, the tip must be pinched off, which delays flowering a little, but this delay is less unpleasant than the inconvenience of having very tall Dahlias on a single stem. In July or August, when the Dahlias have reached their maximum development, we start disbudding them, an operation which consists in detaching the secondary buds that grow between the leaves; the result is that, freed of a multitude of useless buds, each clump will produce a beautiful and abundant bloom. Towards the end of October or the beginning of November, as soon as the first frosts appear, Dahlias should be uprooted. If the weather is favorable, let them dry out a little, then place them either in the orangery, under the tier of the geranium greenhouse, or in a dry cellar, to spend the winter. GLADIOLUS (Gladiolus). - Gladiolus are grown in light soil mixed with leaf compost. They are planted in the open ground in March and April; they flower in July and August, and are lifted in October. They can also be planted in pots in autumn, but in this case they must be placed under a frame to overwinter, and they flower as early as May. They can be propagated from seed or cloves. Imis d'Allemagne or FLAMBE (Iris Germanica). - The many varieties of this plant deserve to occupy a distinguished place in gardens; either in borders or in beds, they produce a charming effect at the time of their flowering, which takes place in May and June, especially if the colors have been suitably blended, and their hardiness is an added bonus for enthusiasts. We propagate them from seed or by separating the plants in September; we already have over a hundred beautiful varieties. Bulbous IRIs, XIFHIUM, or Angleterré. - A large number of varieties of all colors are grown; the flowers are remarkable for the narrowness of all their divisions; they bloom in June; they are propagated from cloves, which are planted in autumn; winter cover. Xiphioid or Spanish IRIS. - Like the previous one, this species offers a large number of varieties; same cultivation. JACINTHE (Hyacinthus orientalis). - In April, single or double flowers of all colors, depending on the variety, which are very numerous; propagation by seed or clove. Hyacinths like soft, substantial soil, and the colder and wetter the climate, the lighter the soil. To obtain good vegetation, they should be planted in well-smoked soil, but the manure must be buried deep enough for the roots alone to reach it, otherwise the onions almost always rot shortly after being pulled out. The best time for planting is late September or early October; however, it would be better to delay planting by a fortnight than to plant in wet weather. Planting is carried out as follows. After preparing the soil well, hoe a furrow Om, 10 deep; place the onions about Om, 15 apart on the line, and press them in so that they are covered with Om, 10 of soil; then open the other furrows successively, and Om, 15 apart. After planting, a good mulch is spread over the whole, and other care consists in pulling out weeds. If the cold becomes so intense that we fear the soil will freeze to the roots, we'll cover them with leaves or dry litter, which we'll remove in February; if the weather is bad, we'll cover them more lightly. Once they have deflowered, and as soon as the leaves begin to yellow, cut off the leaves and stems on the ground, and towards the end of June carefully pull them up, which should only be done when the weather is fine. As soon as they are uprooted, place them in a dry, well-ventilated spot, without exposing them to the sun; after a few days, detach the cloves and roots, then, before placing them on the shelves, clean them with a soft brush to remove anything that might cause rot. To fill in the soil until the onions are ready to be lifted, sow a small amount of Larkspur, Nemophila or Silene seed in the spring. When sown light, this will succeed Hyacinths without affecting the maturity of the onions. Planting in pots - Hyacinths whose flowering is to be brought forward should be planted in pots at the same time as in the ground. The soil used for potting should be light and substantial, and the pots in proportion to the size of the onions. After planting, the pots should be placed side by side, so that they are covered with Om, 03 soil. You can place some of them to the south, and others to the north, so that they don't all flower at the same time. If there is a severe frost, they should be covered with leaves or litter, so that the soil in the pots doesn't freeze; and in the course of January you can start putting some of them under a frame or in a heated greenhouse, but always as close as possible to the windows. They'll be in bloom about a month after you start heating them; you should only force them in successively, so as to prolong flowering as long as possible. As soon as they have deflowered, the pots should be placed in the ground to allow the onions to ripen, so that they can be planted the following year. They are planted in spring, in pure heather soil or in a mixture of heather soil, manure loam and well-consumed leaves, mixed in equal parts; in autumn, they are lifted into pots, placed under a frame or on a shelf in the temperate greenhouse or orangery, and lily with golden stripes (Lilium auratum).- Height, Om, 50 to Om, 60; very fragrant flowers, long from Om, 18 to Om, 20, white mottled with purple, with broad bands of a beautiful yellow. LOBELIA CŒLESTIS. - Stems higher than those of other species; flowers also in spikes, of a beautiful celestial blue. Same culture. polyphyllous lupin (Lupinus polyphyllus). - Height, 1 meter; blue flowers in May; white-flowered variety; seed multiplication, sown immediately after maturity; light, siliceous or heather soil. The double-flowered variety needs to be protected from the cold. MATRICAIRE Mandiane (Matricaria parthenioides). - Height, Om, 50; very soft white flowers, all summer and autumn; propagation by cuttings or splinters, in February and March. PEPPERMINT (Mentha piperita). - Height, Om, 50; reddish flowers, in August; cultivated particularly for the scent of its leaves, the essence of which is used to perfume pastilles: variegated-leaf variety. Sucker propagation. Merendera bulbocodium. - Height, Om, 06 to Om, 08; flowers white, then purpurine, in March; propagated by cloves, replanted in July and August. Purpurine MOLEN (Verbascum phœniceum). - Height, Om, 50 to Om, 60; purple flowers, from May to July; varieties with more or less bright pink flowers; seed propagation, sown immediately after maturity. Several other varieties are grown, differing only in flower color. MoriNe à longues feuilles (Morina longifolia). - Height, Om, 65 to I meters; pinkish-white flowers in June and July; seed and splinter propagation. May lily (Convallaria maialis). - White flowers, single or double, in May; pink-flowered variety; propagates offspring in autumn. MUSCARI monstruueux, Earth Lilac (Muscari monstruosum). - Bulbous plant; purplish-blue flowers in June; clove propagation; replant in autumn, along with bulbs. MUSCARI odorant (M. moschatum). - Reddish, fragrant flowers in May; same propagation. MYoSoTIS ALpESTRIS. - Sown in July, Myosotis Alpestris produces charming blue or white flowers in April and May, depending on the variety. Transplanted in separate colors, this plant can be used to create delightful beds until greenhouse plants are ready to be planted in the ground. NARCISSUS à bouquet (Narcissus Tazetta). - Fragrant yellow flowers in May; cloves multiply and are planted in October. This species has produced a large number of varieties, the most remarkable of which are : Constantinop'e, Soleil d'or, Multiflore and Grand Monarque; but as they cannot withstand our winters and succumb to 4 degrees of cold, they should be planted in pots or placed on carafes filled with water, with a few grains of salt; they flower in January and February. NARCISSUS daffodil (N. Jonquilla). - Yellow, fragrant, single or double flowers in April. Requires soft, substantial soil; in any other condition, it not only fails to flower, but quickly perishes. Daffodils are planted in September, at Om, 05 or Om, 06 depth, then lifted when the leaves are dry. Multiplication of Daffodils, planted at this time. Florist's carnation (Dianthus caryophyllus). - This species has produced a considerable number of varieties, all of which can be traced back to two main races: I° Flemish, whose characteristics are: perfectly rounded petals, without serrations and with wide bands of various colors on a white background; 2° Fancy; they are classified in the following order: 1st Series. Ardoisés. They are single-colored, striped or ribboned. 2° Series. Avranchains. Yellow background, more often nankin, with more or less intense flames. 3rd Series. English. Pure white petals, neither laciniated nor crenellated, but embroidered with a border. 4th Series. White background. Petals with streaked white background, sometimes also edged at the same time. 5th Series. Saxon. Petals with striated yellow background, sometimes edged at the same time. 6th Series. Bichons. The colors are only apparent on the surface of the petals. They are propagated from marcottes or seeds, which can be sown immediately after maturity; but as they do not all flower the following year, Carnations are not usually sown until April, either in the open ground or in terracottas, and during June; finally, when the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted in a nursery and spaced Om, 12 or Om, 15 apart. A few waterings help the plants to take root; then, towards the end of August, they are planted, either in flowerbeds or in beds, about Om 40 apart. Immediately after planting, the soil is mulched with half-used manure, and further care is limited to hoeing and watering as needed. Young Carnations are usually very hardy, and able to withstand heavy frosts without suffering; however, it is wiser to cover them in winter, to protect them from snow and frost, which are very harmful to them. The following spring, and as soon as necessary, they should be staked; then, in order to obtain perfect flowers, when the buds appear, their number should be reduced before they are too far advanced. At the end of June or the beginning of July, the flowering period, we select those worth keeping, and during July we lay them, taking care to observe everything we have indicated in the Marcottes chapter. However, as there are sometimes branches so high up on the stems that it is not possible to marcott them in this way, they must be removed and cut into cuttings, which are treated in the following way: after cutting them cleanly below a node, make a small incision and work upwards to divide the cutting in two; then transplant them into light soil, in a shady spot, and cover them with cloches, which are removed when the cuttings begin to grow. When the cuttings have sufficient roots, they are potted up and treated like rooted marcottes, as described below. At the beginning of October, we wean the marcottes, i.e. separate them from the old plants, to plant them in pots of about Om, 08 to Om, 10; the soil used for potting must be healthy and made up of two-thirds good free soil and one-third well-consumed leaf compost, all well mixed and passed through a rack. Immediately after potting, place the pots under the shelter of a wall, and give them a few light sprinklings from time to time. As soon as frost approaches, they should be put inside, to protect the carnations from cold and damp. If there's not enough room under the frames, make a trench about Om.20 deep and I metre wide; then lay out the gaulettes in such a way as to support doormats, which are placed as needed, and removed whenever the weather permits; and if the cold is more severe, it's always enough to spread leaves or litter over the doormats. Finally, around April, when sleet is no longer to be feared, carnations can be planted in the open ground, or repotted more generously if they are to be kept in pots. (EILLET remontant. - Variety of the florist's carnation, far superior to the type for the long duration of its flowers; same cultivation. Poet's carnation, bearded carnation (Dianthus barbatus). - Stems from Om, 30 to Om, 40; in June and July, red, pink, white or variegated, single or double flowers; propagation by cuttings, marcottes, or seeds sown in August. ŒILLET Flon. - Whatever the conditions in which it is grown, this carnation fully justifies the name Dianthus semperflorens given to it in France, and Dianthus hybridus multiflorus in England, by the abundance and long duration of its flowers. Its flowers are pink, red, white or white with pink variegation, semi-double and fragrant, requiring nothing more than other carnations, and it blooms from June to November. Potted plants returned to the greenhouse continue to flower throughout the winter. Propagate with cuttings in August and September. PHLOX ligneux. - Stems from Om, 30 to Im, 20; in July, August and September, pink, purple, lilac, flax-gray, pure white or white lamé flowers in various colors, depending on the variety, which are very numerous. PIGAMON with columbine leaves (Thalictrum aguilegifolium). - Height, Om, 65 to I meters; in May, green flowers with white stamens; varieties with lilac and bright pink stamens; multiplication by seed, sown in spring, or by separating plants in autumn. PIVOINE officinale (Pœonia officinalis). - Height, Om, 65; in May, single or double red flowers; varieties with pink or meaty flowers first, then white; all propagate from seed, sown in August, or by separating the tubers with eyes; they are planted in September, and in such a way that they are only covered with about Om, 02 or Om, 03 of soil. These varieties decorate flowerbeds and flower beds, where they produce a very beautiful effect. Chinese peony (Pœonia Sinensis). - Height, Om, 65; very double, white flowers in June. PLUMBAGO LARPENTÆ. - A trailing plant that can be grown in the shade. Placed in favorable conditions, it forms large clumps and produces a large number of beautiful blue flowers throughout the autumn. It can be propagated by cuttings and splinters. Blue Polemonium, Greek Valerian (Polemonium cœruleum). - Height, Om,65; blue flowers, from May to July; white-flowered variety; multiplication by seed, which sows itself, or by separating clumps. Any soil. POTENTILLA. - A large number of Potentilla species are grown. Their foliage resembles that of the strawberry. The pink, red, purple, yellow or white flowers of these plants follow one another throughout the summer. Multiplication by seed and splinters. PYRETHRUM ROSEUM, RED CAMOMILE. - Height, Om, 30 to Om, 40; in June and July, single bright pink flowers; multiplication by seed or by separating clumps.
