Garden Ranunculus (Ranunculus Asiaticus). - Height, Om, 20 to Om, 25; very numerous varieties, single, semi-double or double, of almost any color. They are planted at O m. 05 depth, towards the end of December, in light soils, then in February and March, in all other circumstances; they are in flower from the end of April to the beginning of June. For cultivation and propagation, follow the same guidelines as for Anemones. ROSE trémière (Alcœa rosea). - Height, 2 m to 2.65 m; from July to September, single, semi-double or double flowers, offering almost every shade; propagation from seed sown in June; plants are transplanted in the nursery in July and planted in autumn; they can also be propagated by suckering. Planted in rows, hollyhocks produce a charming effect. To prevent them from being broken by the wind, they should be staked or tied to trellis slats fixed to stakes which are driven in from a distance. For those planted in isolation, a stake should be placed on each one; and when the clumps are strong, a hoop is placed in the middle of the stems, then they are fixed to it. This arrangement produces a very good effect. To grow hollyhocks to a lower height, simply pinch off the ends of the stems when they have reached the height you want the plants to reach. Large-flowered TIGRIDIA (Tigridia pavonia). - Bulbous plant; height, Om, 40 to Om, 50; yellow or scarlet flowers, punctuated with dark purple; they follow one another from July to August, but each lasts only eight or ten hours; propagation by cloves, which are separated in March and April, and then replanted, along with the bulbs, to a depth of Om, 05. LYING TOWER (Tournefortia heliotropioides). - Height, Om, 35; bluish flowers all summer, overwinter under frame; multiplication by seed and cuttings. GARDEN TUBER (Polianthes tuberosa). - Bulbous plant; height, 1 mt to 1m 30; from July to September, white flowers with a very sweet odor, single or double; multiplication of cloves, which are separated only in spring, for planting on beds. Flowering bulbs should be planted in March, in pots placed on a bed under a frame. As long as the onion is not growing, it should be given little water; but as soon as it has a few leaves, it should be watered frequently. Gessner or Florist's TULIP (Tulipa Gessneriana). - This species has produced a considerable number of brightly colored varieties from seed. They like soft, substantial soil, made light by much-used fertilizers. They are propagated from seed, sown in the ground from September to November, or from cloves, which are replanted by September at the latest. Flowering onions should be planted in late October or early November. Once the soil has been well prepared, it is usually divided into rows about 1 m wide, with one row in the middle, then two on each side, arranged so that they are equally spaced, and the first two at Om, 12 or Om, 15 from the edge, and, as with Hyacinths, a furrow is opened with the hoe, starting the planting with the middle row, for which the tallest plants are chosen. until we have a collection classified by name and color, it must at least be arranged by height. The Tulips are placed about Om, 15 apart on the line, and lightly pressed down so that they are covered with about Om, 08 of soil; then the soil is brought closer together, so that there are no gaps. The same procedure is followed for each row, taking care to place the onions in a chessboard pattern. After planting, a good mulch of half-used manure is spread over the whole area, and until flowering, all care consists in hoeing the soil, pulling out weeds and, in spring, watering the soil if the temperature so requires. Flowering is usually at its most beautiful around the first days of May. If you want to prolong the life of the flowers and enjoy their full brilliance, towards the end of April you need to erect a small framework over which you spread a canvas when the sun is shining. As soon as the tulips have deflowered, remove the heads of those whose seeds you don't want to keep, so that the sap remains concentrated in the onion, increasing its vigor for next year's flowering. Onions are left in the ground until they are fully ripe, which is usually towards the end of June. To pull them out, however, you need to choose good weather, and as you remove them from the ground, you detach the cloves and old roots, then rub lightly with your thumb to remove the old scales. Above all, avoid leaving the onions exposed to the sun, 18. GARDENING MANUAL. as most of them will be lost. Each row is placed immediately in a square with a sequential number, then all the onions are placed in a well-ventilated place, but where they cannot be affected by frost. As with Hyacinths, a little Larkspur seed can be sown in Tulip beds in spring, to fill in the ground until the time comes to remove the onions. TUSSILAGE odorant, HÉLIOTROpE d'hiver (Tussilago fragrans). - Height, Om, 30; from November to January, purplish-white, Heliotrope-scented flowers; sucker propagation. VAĹRIANE rouge (Valeriana rubra) - Height, Om, 65 to I mêtre; red or white flowers, June to October; all multiply from seed, sown in place in autumn or spring, or by separating their plants. California ZAUSCHNÈRIA (Zauschneria Californica). - A charming little plant with scarlet-red flowers, propagated from cuttings or seeds sown in July in a nursery. The young plant is overwintered in a greenhouse, and in spring planted out in groups like Verbena and Petunia. SECTION IV - Greenhouse plants that can be grown in the open ground during the summer. For general care of summer-grown greenhouse plants, see page 91. ALTERNANTHERA SPATULATA - Herbaceous, bushy plant; height Om, 10 to Om, 15, spatula-shaped leaves, light green irregularly variegated with pink, salmon and yellow. Treated like Coleus, this charming plant can be used for exactly the same purposes. BEGONIA. - Grown outdoors in summer, Begonias produce a charming effect, with their beautiful leaves and abundant, long-lasting flowers. They can be planted in the second half of May, in heather soil, in rows or in beds. They can be propagated by taking cuttings and overwintering them in a warm greenhouse. Varieties - Fuchsioides, - Ingrami, - Lucida, - Prestoniensis. CALADIUM ESCULENTUM. - Although this is a hot-greenhouse plant, it can be grown outdoors during the summer. In fact, judging by the way Caladium esculentum develops when grown in the ground, this is the most suitable growing method. Like all vigorous plants, Caladium esculentum require good fertilization and copious watering during hot spells. They can be planted in the second half of May, singly or in clumps, then lifted as frost approaches to overwinter in a warm greenhouse. woody calceolaria. - Much hardier than herbaceous Calceolaria, woody Calceolaria can be grown in the open ground throughout the summer. The beautiful yellow flowers of these plants produce a charming effect in the center of a bed of red Geranium or blue-flowered Lobelia erinus. Woody Calceolaria can be propagated from cuttings taken under cloches in August and September, and overwintered in a greenhouse. These cuttings are good for planting out in May (1). CANNA INDICA. - This plant, most remarkable for its .(1) Herbaceous calceolaria. - Herbaceous Calceolaria are sown in August and September in light soil, made up of heather soil, loam and potting compost, under a frame in a shady spot. When the plant has three or four leaves, it is transplanted into small pots, which are then placed under a frame to overwinter. In February or March, Calceolaria seedlings are given larger pots, after which they can be placed in the temperate greenhouse to enjoy the beauty of their flowers. Calceolaria, with its leaves as well as its flowers, can be grown in the open ground during the summer. With warmth, water and fertilizer, Canna's vigor surpasses anything our climate allows us to hope for. To obtain Canna similar to those seen every year in the squares of Paris, they need to be bedded out in the second half of March. When the suckers are about Om, 10 in length, we detach them with a portion of tuber, then plant them in pots which we keep on bedding until May, when we can put the Canna in the ground. After enjoying the beauty of this plant throughout the summer, it is lifted as frost approaches, to be stored in a dry cellar, or on the tier of a temperate greenhouse, just like the Dahlias. Varieties that don't produce tubers should be pulled out and placed in pots to overwinter in a warm greenhouse. Canna can be propagated from suckers, as described above, or from seed, which is sown on a bed in spring. Varieties. - Annei, - Discolor, Edulis, - Gigantea, - Indica, - Indica superba, - Leptophylla, - Liliiflora, - Lutea, - Peruviana, - Zebrina, - Warscewezii. CASSIA - Cassia are beautiful shrubs with yellow flowers in corymbs, which can be placed on lawns in summer. When young, they produce little effect, but as they vegetate vigorously when grown in the ground, medium-sized specimens can be planted if strong specimens are lacking. They are planted in May, then lifted as frost approaches, to be stored in the orangery until the following spring. Varieties. - Floribunda, - Corymbosa, - Tomentosa. CHAMÆROPS HUMILIS (Dwarf Palm). - Despite this plant's well-known hardiness, in the Paris climate it must be grown in pots. Arranged in groups on lawns, Chamærops humilis produce an entirely new effect in summer, with leaves so different from those of other plants. Chamærops humilis is easy to keep in the orangery in winter. CHEIRANTHUS DELILIANUS - A charming little wallflower with purplish-red, long-lasting flowers that can be grown in the open ground in summer. It can be propagated from cuttings and overwintered in a greenhouse. CHEIRANTHUS MARSHALLI. - Equally recommendable, this plant differs from Cheiranthus Delilianus in the color of its flowers, which are a beautiful yellow; it can be grown in exactly the same way. CHRYSANTHÈME frutescent (Chrysanthemum with white flowers). - This plant, whose flowers bear a slight resemblance to meadow daisies, is particularly well-suited to bedding because of its hardiness and long-lasting blooms. It is propagated from cuttings taken under glass in August and September. Overwintered under glass, frutescent Chrysanthemums can be delivered to the garden in May (1). COLEUS VERSCHAFFELTI. - All Coleus are remar(1) Hybrid Cineraria. - To grow beautiful Cineraria, you need a richly colored leaf, but they are delicate. Only the species named Verschaffelti can be grown in the open ground during the summer, like Lantana, Heliotrope and Salvia. Nothing can give an idea of the beauty of this plant, when it has been properly cared for. Coleus Verschaffelti are propagated by taking cuttings and overwintering them in a warm greenhouse. CUPREA. - Cuphea should, in keeping with their small size, be placed at the edge of beds. Grown in the open ground during the summer, these charming little plants produce flowers until the frosts; they can be propagated from seed or cuttings and overwintered in a greenhouse. Varieties. - Platycentra, - Ignea, - Strigulosa. ERYTHRINA. - Like all plants that vegetate vigorously, Erythrina needs a lot of food to produce beautiful flowers. So, unless the soil is cold and damp, it's better to grow them in the open ground during the summer than to keep them in pots. This shrub is worthy of a place in any garden, thanks to the beauty of its large red flowers. It can be planted singly or in clumps. During winter, it is stored in the orangery, where it remains until spring. Varieties. - Crista galli, - Bellengerii, - Floribunda, - Madame Bellengé. - Marie Bellengé, - Ruberrima. give them a light soil, made up of heather soil, loam and potting soil. Sow in August and September, in a shady frame. Transplanted and repotted at the appropriate time, Cineraria seedlings can be used to decorate greenhouses and apartments in spring. EUCALYPTUS GLOBULUS. - Eucalyptus globulus is a large, beautiful Australian tree that can be grown in the open ground during the summer. Endowed with uncommon vigor, it can reach 5 meters in height in a single year; its leaves, covered with a whitish dust when young, are essentially different from those of our garden trees. Multiplication by seed, sown towards the end of summer, to produce plants suitable for planting the following year. DATURA ARBOREA. - Datura arborea is, if not the first greenhouse plant to be cultivated in the open ground during the summer, at least one of the first; its flowers, often as long as Om, 32, are pendulous, white and very fragrant. In large gardens, beds of Datura arborea can be formed; but everywhere else, it is preferable to grow this beautiful plant on its own. Datura arborea is propagated from cuttings, which are overwintered in the orangery, like the old plants. FUCHSIA. - Grown in the open ground during the summer, Fuchsias produce flowers until the frosts, requiring no care other than watering in proportion to the temperature. They can be propagated from seed or from cuttings, which are grown in greenhouses or under frames. Varieties. - Flowers more or less dark red, or white with red corolla. HELIOTROPES. - For beautiful Heliotropes, grow them in the open ground during the summer. They can be propagated by sowing or, better still, by taking cuttings in layers in January and February. Planted in May, Heliotrope cuttings grow rapidly and produce flowers until the frosts. A few Heliotrope plants overwintered in the greenhouse are enough each year to produce as many cuttings as are needed to fill a garden. Varieties. - From Peru. - Triomphe de Liége, - Gloire des massifs. - Anna Thurell. LANTANA. - Lantana are charming little plants with corymb flowers, first yellow, then red or lilac, which can be used to create delightful beds. Lantana can be propagated from seed or from cuttings taken from the bed in January and February. By giving these cuttings the warmth they need, they can be delivered to the field in the second half of May. Lantana needs light soil, with plenty of fertilizer. Varieties. - Aurantiaca, - Delicatissima. - Mutabilis. LOBELIA RAMOSA. - A border plant with beautiful blue flowers. Grown in the garden in summer, Lobelia ramosa produces flowers until the frosts. It can be propagated from seed or cuttings in August and September. Overwintered in a greenhouse, Lobelia ramosa cuttings can be delivered to the garden in May. MMULUS - The difficulty of preserving Mimulus over winter means that we have to make do with growing them as annuals. Sown in beds in February and March, Mimulus can be delivered to the garden in the second half of May. Grown in light soil mixed with potting mix, 19 Mimulus form large clumps when the ends of the stems are suitably pinched. Varieties. - Variegatus, - Cardinalis. Musk Mimulus is a creeping species with small yellow flowers, which can be grown in the shade, in heathland. NICOTIANA WIGANDIOIDES. - A perennial orangery plant that can be grown in the open ground during the summer, just like Datura. Placed in favorable conditions, Nicotiana wigandioides makes a beautiful ornamental leafy plant that can be propagated from seed or cuttings, NIEREMBERGIA GRACILIS. - Nierembergia gracilis is particularly suitable for making borders, by laying the branches on the ground. Grown in the ground in summer, it produces flowers until the frosts. It can be propagated by sowing or, better still, by taking cuttings and overwintering them in a greenhouse. Much more vigorous, Nierembergia frutescent deserves preference in every respect. PELARGONIUM ZONALE. - Much better known under the name of Geranium than Pelargonium, this plant is now one of the most beautiful additions to our gardens. Just as commendable for its hardiness as for the brilliance, abundance and long life of its flowers, Geranium zonale is particularly suitable for vases and beds. It can be propagated from seed or, better still, from cuttings taken under cloches in August and September. Geranium zonale are winter-harvested in greenhouses or under frames, and can be delivered to the garden in May. As they develop rapidly, it is much simpler to plant young Geraniums each year than to keep the old plants. Varieties. - Red, pink or white flowers, or red with white variegated leaves. The species known as Ivy-leaved Geranium can be grown as a summer border plant. PETUNIA. - Having long since recognized that it is much simpler to grow Petunia as annuals than to keep the old plants, they are now sown in March on beds, then transplanted as soon as the planting is sufficiently advanced, or sown in April, immediately in place. For double-flowered varieties that you want to keep, take cuttings in August and September, and overwinter them under glass. Grown in the open ground during the summer, Petunias produce flowers right up to the frosts. Of all the plants used to decorate gardens, none are better suited than Petunias for lining vases and beds, or making borders by laying branches on the ground (1), SALVIA SPLENDENS. - Although the flowers of this beautiful plant appear a little late, its cultivation should not be neglected, for when delivered to the open ground in June, it produces long spikes throughout the autumn (1) PRIMULA Sinensis. - Primula Sinensis is one of the most suitable plants for decorating greenhouses and apartments during the winter. Sow in March and April in a greenhouse, then transplant into pots. It requires heather soil mixed with a quarter of potting compost. red flowers, of such brilliance that it is impossible to fix them for long. Salvia splendens can be propagated by sowing or, better still, by taking cuttings on a bed in March and April. A few plants of Salvia splendens, overwintered in a warm greenhouse, are enough each year to produce as many cuttings as are needed to fill a garden. SOLANUM. - From an ornamental point of view, Solanum plants are of immense interest. Grown in the open ground during the summer, certain species produce a remarkable effect with their beautiful leaves. They can be used to form clumps; however, it is preferable to place them singly on lawns. Solanum can be propagated from seed or cuttings, and overwintered in a warm greenhouse. Varieties. - Atropurpureum, - Betaceum, - Callicarqum, - Marginatum, - Purpureum, - Pyracanthum, - Robustum. SENECIO CINÉRARIA (Cineraria maritima). - Rough plant with pinnate leaves covered, like the stems, with a tamentous down. Arranged in rows around beds of Coleus or any other brightly-colored plant, Cineraria maritima produces a charming effect; it can overwinter outdoors if necessary, but it's best to take cuttings every year to replace old plants that grow too large. Centaurea candidissima can be used in the same circumstances; it is even whiter than Cineraria maritime, only less hardy. STATICE LATIFOLIA. - This perennial, with radical leaves and branching stems, forms a paniculate corymb; blue flowers from July to September. Propagates from seed in June and July. Although hardy, Statice latifolia should be grown in pots and overwintered in a greenhouse. TAGETES LUCIDA. - The flowers of this plant are a beautiful yellow. They are small, but very numerous and long-lasting. If required, Tagetes lucida can replace woody Calceolaria in the composition of beds where yellow flowers are desired. It can be propagated from seed or cuttings and overwintered in a greenhouse. TRADESCANTIA zebrina. - Creeping plant, suitable for lining the surface of beds dedicated to the cultivation of greenhouse plants during the summer. The exceptional conditions in which Tradescantia zebrina thrives add to the interest of this charming plant, already remarkable for the beauty of its leaves. Tradescantia zebrina is propagated from cuttings and overwintered in a warm greenhouse. hybrid Verbena. - The seeds from these charming little plants produce a considerable number of varieties of all shades every year. Sown in February and March, hybrid Verbena plants can be transplanted into pots, or planted out immediately, like all annuals sown in spring. Despite the simplicity of this method of cultivation, Verbena cuttings are most often propagated in August and September; overwintered in frames, Verbena cuttings are good for putting in the ground in April and May. The brilliance, freshness and long life of Verbena flowers make these plants particularly recommended for making up beds or borders by laying the branches on the ground. woody veronicas. - Woody Veronica is not to be confused with perennial Veronica, whose stems die back each year. Arranged in long clusters of red, pink or lilac, the flowers of woody Veronica turn successively white. They can be propagated from seed or from cuttings taken under cloches in August and September. Overwintered under glass, woody Veronica cuttings can be delivered to the garden in May. Varieties. - Andersoni, - Lindleyana, - Splendida. WIGANDIA Caracasana. - Of all the greenhouse plants cultivated for the beauty of their leaves, none is more ornamental than Wigandia Caracasana. It is propagated from seed or root cuttings in September and October; overwintered in the greenhouse or under glass, Wigandia Caracasana cuttings can be delivered to the field towards the end of May. To obtain the full effect that this beautiful plant can produce, reserve a place for it on lawns and give it a light soil amply supplied with consumed fertilizers. SECTION II. - Plants for water ornament. 1. Plants with floating leaves. Floating ALISMA (Alisma natans). - White flowers. Spiny-fruited CORNIFLOWER (Ceratophyllum demersum). - Herbaceous flowers in June and July; smooth-fruited variety. WATERFLOWER (Sagittaria sagittifolia). - White flowers in June; seed multiplication. NÉNUPHAR odorant (N. odorata). - White, double, fragrant flowers in July. Amphibious RENQUÉE (Polygonum amphibium). - Red flowers in July. water lily (Villarsia nymphoides). - Yellow flowers in July. 2. Plants rising above the surface of the water. Fragrant Acorn (Acorus calamus). - Height, 1 m to Im 50; fragrant flowers, in catkins, in July. Swamp IRIS, Swamp GlaieuL (Iris Pseudo-Acorus). - Height, 1m, 33; beautiful yellow flowers in June. Broad-leaved MASSETTA (Typha latifolià). - Stems 1.65 to 2 meters long, ending in a spike of brown flowers in July. Narrow-leaved MASSETTA (T. angustifolia). - Less tall than its predecessor. NAIADE with lanceolate leaves (Naias monosperma). - Height, Om, 15; herbaceous flowers, August and September; seed multiplication, self-seeding. WATERWORTH (Rumex hydrolapathum). - Height, 1.50 to 2 meters; greenish flowers, July. HEART LEAF PONTEDERIA (Pontederia cordata). - Blue flowers in May. This beautiful plant can also be grown on the water's edge. WATERFOOT (Hippuris vulgaris). - Height, Om, 15 to Om, 20; dirty-white flowers in May. long-leaved pondweed (Ranunculus lingua). - Stems 1 m high; yellow flowers in June, continuing until October. SALICAIRE (Lythrum virgatum). - Height, 1 mt to Im, 33; purple-pink flowers in July and August. All Salicaria can also be grown on the water's edge. Leaning Saururus (Saururus cernuus). - Height, Om, 40; white flowers, arranged in a long cluster curved at the top. POND SCIRP (Scirpus lacustris). - Very tall plant, producing terminal spikes of flowers in July. SPARGANIUM flotant, RUBAN d'eau (Sparganium natans). - Height, Om, 33; flowers in catkins in July. All these plants can be grown in large pots or tubs immersed in water. 3. Plants suitable for waterside decoration. ALISMA water plantain (Alisma plantago). - Height, Om, 65; white or reddish flowers in July. ARUNDO DONAX. - This plant, remarkable for the power of its vegetation, must, like all reeds, be cultivated in isolation; each year, the stems of Arundo donax are cut back from the ground, then covered with leaves during harsh winters. Varieties. - White variegated leaves. - Yellow variegated leaves. The variety with white variegated leaves should be stored in winter. The yellow variegated variety can remain in place. BAMBUSA AUREA. Native to northern China, this beautiful plant can withstand the harshest cold without suffering. Like all bamboos, it produces numerous woody stems with a truly picturesque effect. Multiplication by root cuttings. BUTOMUS umbellatus, Joxc fleuri (Butomus umbellatus). - Height, 1 meter; pink flowers in July; variegated variety. LINAIGRETTE à gaînes (Friophorum vaginatum). - Height, Om, 33; in March and April, spikes covered with long white silks. WILLOW LEAF LYSIMACHY (Lysimachia ephemerum). - Height, 1 m; white flowers, July-September; seed multiplication, sown on beds, frequently watered, one of the brightest plants. LYSIMACHIE thyrsiflore, NAUMBURGIA thyrsiflore (L.thyrsiflora).-Height, Om, 33; yellow flowers, June and July; seed and splinter propagation. LYSIMACHIE ponctuée (L. punctata). - Height, Om, 65; yellow flowers in July. MARSH PARNASSIA (Parnassia palustris). - Height, Om, 25 to Om, 30; white flowers spotted with yellow, in June and July. PHALARIS ribboned, Ribbon reed (Phalaris arundinacea 19. picta). - Height, 1 m; leaves ribboned with yellow; flowers in a spiciform panicle, whitish on the shady side, purple on the sunny side; multiplication by traces. Swamp POPULAR, Swamp Soucr (Caltha palustris). -Height, Om, 33; in April and May, single or double yellow flowers. FLAMMOND (Ranunculus flammula) Height, Om, 40; yellow flowers in June and July. SCORPIONE (Myosotis palustris). - Stems from Om, 20 to Om, 26; celestial-blue flowers, April to August; propagates from seed, sown in spring, from cuttings or by bursting the stems. SPIRITS (Spirœa). - Almost all herbaceous species thrive at the water's edge, producing a pleasing effect. 4. Trees and shrubs. Veined WORTH (Vaccinium uliginosum). - Creeping stems; white or meaty flowers in May and June. Caneberry (V. Oxycoccos). - Stems like above; red flowers in May; red berries with purple spots. PEPPER TREES (Populus). - They thrive in damp soils and are ideal for decorating ponds and rivers. (V. Amenity trees.) WILLS (Salix). - All willows are suitable for aquatic sites in landscape gardens, and can be advantageously placed at the water's edge, where they produce a very picturesque effect. Germanica) - Height: 2.33 to 2.65 meters; pale purple or pink flowers, June to September. Hairy TUPELO (Nyssa villosa). - Large, beautiful tree with greenish flowers in June; blue fruit. AUBRIETIA DELTOIDEA.-Small, clump-forming plant with light-blue flowers throughout spring. ARABIS VERNA. - Small clump-forming plant with white flowers in March. CAMPANULAS. - Campanula cespitosa and Campanula carpathica, already recommended as border plants, are also well-suited to lining rocks. CRUCIANELLA RUBRA. - Spreading stems, from Om, 25 to Om, 30 long; pink flowers, all summer long. EPINEDIUM ALPINUM. -Stems from Om, 35; red and yellow flowers, in April and May. ERINUS ALPINUS - Small plant in clumps; purplish or white flowers; March and April. FOUGÈRES. - Athyrium fœmina, Adianthum pedatum, Aspidium lobatum, astrœa, recurva, Blechnum spicans, Osmunda regalis. Onoclea sensibilis, Pteris aquilina, Scolopendrium officinarum, Struthiopteris Germanica, can be placed between the stones of rocks, especially those in a cool, sheltered spot. SAXIFRAGA. - Saxifraga granulata, hypnoides and sarmentosa are particularly suitable for lining rocks. SEDUM - All Sedums are suitable for lining rocks, especially Sedum acre, elegans, hirsutum, populifolium, Siebolduï and Telephium rubrum. Cut-leaf RONCE. - Pink flowers from July to September. RONCE with pink flowers. - Very double pink flowers. SECTION VII. - Climbing plants for walls, cradles and arbors. 1. Annuals and perennials. CALYSTEGIA PUBESCENS. - All summer long, double flowers of soft pink, shaded with brighter pink. CALYSTEGIA SEPIUM (var. incarnatum). - Single, soft-pink flowers. Calysegia are particularly suitable for lining cradles, rocks and the stems of large trees; they are propagated by root trunks. CAPUCINA grande (Tropœolum majus). - Orange-yellow flowers all summer long; brown-flowered variety; sow outdoors in April. CANARY CAPUCINA. (Tropœolum peregrinum) - Serin yellow flowers all summer; same cultivation. COBEA climber (Cobea scandens). Violet flowers all summer long; sow in a bed in March. As soon as the plants have a few leaves, transplant them into small pots, which are left on the bed until late April or early May, when they are planted out. PERENNIAL CUCUMBER (Cucurbita perennis). - This squash is extremely remarkable for its hardiness, the beauty of its velvety leaves and the pleasant scent of its flowers. The stems of the perennial squash die during the winter, but grow back in the spring with great vigor; it is propagated from seeds, marcottes and offshoots, which are replanted in the spring. The squash, known as Tladiantha dubia, produces tubers for reproduction. annual gourds. - All annual squashes can be grown as climbing plants. They can be sown in March, on a bed, or in May, immediately in place; to this end, a good hole is prepared, filled with manure, the soil from the excavation is placed on the manure, then a basin is made, in the center of which the squash seeds are sown. Pelerine, powder pear, flat Corsican, club, siphon, metuliferous, dipsaceus, myriocarpus (gooseberry), melanosperma and all coloquintes can be processed as described above. Sweet pea (Lathyrus odoratus). - Fragrant violet, pink, red or white flowers; sow outdoors in autumn or spring. GESSE de Tanger (L. Tingitanus). - Deep purple-red flowers from July to October; sow outdoors in spring. GESSE à larges feuilles, Pois vivace (L. Latifolius). - Perennial; purple-pink flowers from July to September; sow in place in autumn or spring. Spanish beetle (Phasœlus coccineus). - Scarlet-red flowers all summer; white-flowered and bicolored varieties; sow outdoors in April. Climbing plants for temperate greenhouses that can be planted out in the garden all summer. BOUSSINGAULTIA BASELLOIDES. - Large, sarmentose plant with small, very fragrant white flowers. Whenever the cultivation of shrubs and trees offers nothing special, we will not go into detail on the subject. All can be propagated by seed, grafting, cuttings or marcottes; but we have not deemed it necessary to indicate the method of reproduction for each, as it is only in nurseries that their education can be carried out with advantage. As for the time of planting, the nature of the soil should always be taken into account, and all that is indicated for fruit trees should be observed. ROSE TREES. - This beautiful genus is very numerous in species, and thanks to remontant varieties, we can now enjoy the beauty of its charming flowers for a good part of the year. Roses like a clean, slightly cool soil, amended from time to time with fertilizer; they can be propagated by seed, offspring, marcots, cuttings and grafts. 1. Sowing. - This method of propagation is only used when new varieties are desired. Seeds are sown as soon as they reach maturity, either in terrines, which are brought in during the winter, or in a bed at sunrise, taking care to cover the seedlings during the frosts. 20. 2 Cuttings. - We remove them in autumn, and put them in gauge to replant them in February or March; they are easy to take up; they don't even need to have many roots; a good heel is often enough. 3. Marcottes. - Softwood species are pruned by incision, in May and June, either in the ground or in pruning pots, and woody species are pruned by vine. (See Marcottes article.) 4. Cuttings. - Almost all species can be propagated with cuttings, but on a bed and smothered. Only the Bengals and the Bourbon Islands can do without this care. Species that are to be raised on stems are propagated mainly by scape grafting, using the long-fruited Rose Hip as the subject, followed by the Four Seasons Rose or the ordinary Bengal for species that have some affinity with the latter, and in cases where only low stems are required. Roses are grafted in July and August, taking care, as indicated in the article on Grafting, to take advantage of the time when the plants are most healthy. As for the choice of species, each person must choose according to his or her taste, and the only recommendation we have to make to people who have roses to graft is that they should always prefer species that can withstand the rigors of our winters without suffering. In addition to grafted Roses, we also grow a large number of free-standing varieties, with which we create charming beds. But as many of them suffer from our winters, to preserve them, you need to butter them as the cold approaches, then cover them with leaves or litter if there are heavy frosts. Finally, in February, when frosts are no longer to be feared, the roses are uncovered and the mounds destroyed. In this way, all the varieties that are usually grown in pots can be brought out into the open. You can easily bring forward the flowering of species such as Quatre-Saisons, du Roi, Bengale ordinaire and some of its varieties. To do this, you need to have roses planted in pots from the previous year; you prune them in autumn, and as early as January you can start to force some of them, either by placing them in a heated greenhouse, always as close as possible to the stained-glass windows, or under a frame; but then you need to dig a good trench around the trunk; then you raise a stove of new manure, 'which you reshuffle more or less often, depending on the season. Cover the frames at night, and give them some air when the sun comes out. All roses grown in the open ground flower in June; later on, those of remontant species flower a second time, but there is necessarily an interruption between the first and second flowerings. To remedy this, simply remove the flower buds from a number of remontant Roses. Stopped for a moment in their development, these Roses soon produce new branches, whose flowers succeed the first Roses. Then comes the second natural flowering, so that by this means you have Roses all through the summer. There's nothing special about growing Roses; all you have to do is prune them in March, more or less short, depending on their vigor, and while they're growing, pinch off the ends of any branches that are growing too vigorously; then carefully remove all the branches that grow from the base or from the stem. White and violet Petunias, or Gladiolus, which also produce a beautiful effect, can be planted at the base of slightly high grafted Roses to garnish the stem. For the same purpose, sow a little dwarf larkspur seed in spring between those grown in beds. Since the introduction of the male-flowered Aucuba, the only female-flowered Aucuba we once had has produced numerous scarlet fruits, adding enormously to the ornamental value of this charming shrub. Experience has shown that a single male-flowered Aucuba is sufficient to fertilize all the female-flowered Aucubas in a plantation; as we can see, nothing is easier than to have fruits on all the Aucubas in our gardens. MAGNOLIA with large flowers (Magnolia grandiflora). - A tall, handsome tree with fragrant white flowers, Magnolia is not choosy about where it grows, but it does not like dampness. The species cultivated under the name of Magnolia Oxoniensis flowers younger, but is more delicate, and is often lost in the Paris climate. Tulip trees should only be planted in spring, and cutting off any branches should be avoided as far as possible, as amputations are very harmful to them, especially when young. Varieties. - With whole leaves, - With yellow flowers. VIRGILIER à bois jaune (Virgilia lutea). - Height, about 10 meters; very beautiful white flowers in June. SECTION XII. - Resinous trees. Resinous trees, commonly called green trees, should be planted young, preferably in April and May, when they begin to vegetate. If you are forced to plant in autumn, you should do so from the end of September, otherwise you risk losing a large number of trees. Yews, Thuias and Sequoia sempervirens can be pruned; as for the others, they should not be cut; and if ever it becomes necessary to remove a few branches, they should be cut a few centimetres from the stem, to avoid a loss of sap, which is always detrimental. Green trees are propagated from seed, sown in April, or by herbaceous grafting, using the most common varieties as subjects. ARAUCARIA IMBRICATA. - Pyramidal tree, very graceful; branches covered with lanceolate leaves, prickly at the top. Cedar of Lebanon (Cedrus Libhani). - Very tall, pyramidal tree; horizontal branches; oblong, oval cones, without asperities. It should always be planted singly, to enjoy its majestic effect. DEODORA CEDAR (Cedrus deodora). - This tree, so remarkable for its pendulous branches and the beautiful glaucous green of its leaves, is fast-growing and capable of great elevation. CRYPTOMERIA JAPONICA. - This tree differs essentially from other green trees in its leaves and the arrangement of its branches. To enjoy the full beauty of Cryptomeria, they should be planted in isolation. CYPRESS (Cupressus). - These shade-green trees are all suitable for the pleasure garden; they can be planted in rows or singly, depending on their shape. Cupressus disticha alone requires cool soil to thrive. Varieties. - Sempervirens (pyramidal), - Funebris, - Lawso niana, - Lambertiana, - Disticha (bald). common juniper (Juniperus communis). - Shrub 4 to 5 meters high; diffuse branches, prickly leaves; spherical, blackish-blue berries. Varieties. - Virginia, - Oriental. Common Ir. (Taxus baccata). - Hardy, very common tree; red berries. Can be made to take different shapes by annual mowing. Varieties. - With variegated leaves, - Pyramidal. EUROPEAN LARCH (Larix Europœa). - Very tall, pyramidal tree; horizontal branches; deciduous leaves; very small cones. Varieties. - With hanging branches, - American black. PINES (Pinus). - Very tall, hardy, evergreen trees, invaluable for landscaped gardens; placed singly or in groups, their dark shade contrasts pleasantly with the foliage of other trees. Varieties. - Sylvestris (from Scotland), - Laricio (from Corsica). - Austriaca. - Benthamiana. - Lambertiana. - Pinaster, - Ponderosa, - Pyrenaica, - Strobus, - Sabiniana. FIR TREES (Abies). - Firs are generally tall trees; they differ from pines in their pyramidal shape. All can be grown in large gardens. Varieties. - Alba (White Fir). - Excelsa (Spruce). - Canadensis (Hemlock), - Cephalonica, - Cilicica, - Balsamea (Balsam). - Douglasii, - Morinda. - Nobilis, - Nordmanniana, Pectinata (S. argenté), - Pinsapo. SEQUOIA SEMPERVIRENS (Taxodium sempervirens). - A large, beautiful, fast-growing, hardy California tree. Sequoia can be left to grow naturally, or pruned like Yew trees, essential qualities that should make it eagerly sought after. SEQUOIA GIGANTEA (Wellingtonia gigantea). - Also native to California, this tree can grow to gigantic proportions. To produce its full effect, it must be grown in isolation. CANADA THUIA (Thuia orientalis). - Height, 8m, 33; pyramidal crown; vertical branches; flat, imbricate, green leaves. THUIA de la Chine (T. occidentalis) -Higher than the preceding; flexible branches; russet-green foliage. Varieties. - Aurca, - Pendula. - Gigantea, - Lobbii CHAPTER XVI Destruction of pests. The vegetable garden, orchard and pleasure garden are, all too frequently for the horticulturist, exposed to the ravages of birds, small mammals and insects, which, at every stage of their lives, from the moment they emerge from the egg to their metamorphosis, live at the expense of the plants we raise for our use or enjoyment. Traps and poisoned pellets are used to destroy small rodents, such as mice, rats, dormice and ferrets. Traps, scarecrows and a few rifle shots keep birds away; but the grower must know how to distinguish between his friends and his enemies, and exclude from this proscription warblers and other fine beaks, swallows and insectivorous birds, which at all times of the year live on insects, and in winter on seeds or a few small berries left on bushes. Sparrows and other granivores destroy a prodigious quantity of insects when their young are being raised, and should be spared during this season; it's around July that you should start to hunt them mercilessly. As for insects, which are so numerous, and which by their multiplicity and smallness escape our means of destruction, their numbers are well reduced, it's true, by the active hunting that birds do for them; but their multiplication is so rapid, that they brave these bad odds and seem only to become more inconvenient. A large number of means have been proposed to eradicate them, but few of them are successful; and of all those that have been employed, careful and persevering research is without doubt the most time-consuming, but the most certain. Hedgehogs, tortoiseshells and small night birds, such as the owl, are bred in gardens. They devour a large number of insects, and are the most destructive of all; their usefulness is all the greater as they only hunt at night, and it is at this time of day that many insects wreak their havoc. We therefore advise horticulturalists to keep one of these animals in their garden, as they cost nothing and provide great service. You should also be careful not to destroy bats, which feed only on moths and butterflies. Some insects, such as ladybugs, hoverflies, calosomes, ichneumons and sphexes, destroy large numbers of insects and caterpillars; ladybugs and hoverflies eat aphids, and can be left to multiply without fear; but it often happens that certain carnivorous insects become pests in their turn when they have nothing left to eat. Ichneumons, sphexes and hoverflies should be excluded: they are always useful and never harmful. Spiders (garden spiders) can also be counted among the animals that render great service. BEES, GUESTS - Ripe fruit is often attacked by these insects, which cause considerable damage to espaliers. They are destroyed by hanging small vials filled with honey water from the branches, in which they drown; wasp nests are sought out, and their inhabitants asphyxiated with sulfur smoke or boiling water. ACARUS, commonly known as the gray one. - One of the most dangerous causes of the destruction of trees in our orchards are the stings of the acarus, which attacks peach trees and kills them. It attaches itself under the leaves, sucking the parenchyma, and, despite its small size, is so numerous that it kills the most vigorous tree. Powdered sulfur, applied after basking, is the most effective remedy that can be used to destroy peach blight. Melons, cucumbers, beans and cabbages attacked by this arachnid can also be treated by the same method. SPIDERS. - If the large spider is harmless, the same cannot be said of the small ones, which run quickly over the soil: they attack young seedlings, particularly carrots, prick the stem, suck the sap and kill them. They are kept at bay by sprinkling powdered soot on the soil, and, when the weather permits, they are bathed in water. COURTILIÈRES. - These insects, which are very large and therefore easy to detect, wreak great havoc on vegetable plants, and particularly on diapers. The various means of destroying them are to sprinkle the soil with water laden with black soap or oil, and to plant pots half-full of water in the direction of the cricket's galleries, in order to drown them. Gardeners simply crush them as they find them by turning over the beds, and deliver small piles of manure in which they come to nest. caterpillars and larvae. - In orchards, careful weeding is one of the most foolproof methods, as it destroys both the eggs and the following generations; and if a few nests escape, it is necessary, as advised by Mr. Samuel Curtis, a skilled English amateur, to sprinkle the trees with quicklime just as the leaves begin to develop, and before the flowers bloom. M. le docteur Bailly also says that caterpillars can easily be destroyed when they are gathered together, by sprinkling them with water mixed with black soap, using a broom: it seems that as soon as they are touched, they are instantly struck dead; he even says that prussic acid does not act with greater promptitude. The larvae of insects of the Tenthrède genus are a source of despair for Rose lovers: together with the other enemies of this shrub, they attack the young shoots, causing the flowers to abort. The best time to destroy them is in spring, in April. The best time of day is in the morning, before they have begun to disperse; look for them in the tips of twigs, which are swollen by their presence, and crush them by pressure, or even split the branch with the tip of a penknife and extract the larva. When the disease is too far advanced, cut off the diseased branches. Hylotoma rosce larvae gnaw on the leaves of roses, and can easily be destroyed by crushing them. As for the caterpillars that devour vegetable plants and hide under plant leaves or in the soil, after other natural means of destruction, try decoctions of soot and walnut stain to kill them, but rely even more on research. Careful horticulturists should destroy all butterflies without mercy, as they are the propagators of the enemies of their crops. FORMS. - The inconvenience of these small insects is well known: they harm roots by lifting the soil in which they make their homes; they attack leaves and fruit, and escape many means of destruction because of their smallness and agility. To keep them from climbing trees, wrap a cord of well-carded wool around their feet; keep them away from pots and crates by surrounding them with water, either by a support or by small vases that are kept constantly full. Bottles of honeyed water hung from trees attract ants, which die in considerable numbers. Finally, you can destroy them, like mites, with water mixed with black soap or oil. KERMES, MEALYBUGS, GALLINSECTS. - Kermes insects cause considerable damage to peach trees and fruit trees in general. In spring, they cling so tightly to the branches that a rough brush is needed to remove them. The best time to look for them is in April, before they have laid their eggs, and before they have left the branches where they have spent the winter, to disperse on the leaves of the trees. There are several species of kermes, all generally applied to branches like small warts. LIMAÇONS (Garden propellers). - To get rid of them22 , the best thing to do is to pick them up as you come across them, especially in the morning or after rain; you should also carefully destroy them whenever you come across eggs. LILACES (or Buhottes). - They are very damaging to plants, which they devour with incredible voracity. The best way to destroy them is undoubtedly to use powdered hydrated lime. To spread it, stand downwind and throw it by hand, shaving the ground as hard and evenly as possible. EARTHWORMS. - Earthworms do no harm to plants other than lifting the soil to dig their galleries, and it is only in beds where young seedlings have been planted that they should be destroyed. They are brought out by beating the soil, or by poking a stick into it and shaking it in all directions: they then emerge from the earth; they are placed in a pot and drowned, crushed or, better still, fed to poultry. EAR DRILLS or FORFICLES - Like all insects that come out particularly at night, earwigs attack plant leaves, flowers and fruit, piercing them to make their homes. Carnations, dahlias and hollyhocks are all prey to their voracity. To destroy them, the simplest method is to place small upside-down flower pots on sticks, with a bit of moss at the bottom; the traps are visited every morning, and any earwigs that have taken refuge in them are destroyed by immersing them in a tub full of water. APHIDS. - Aphids, of which there are a large number of species, attack all plants in general. Whether they are grey, like woolly aphids, or green, like rose aphids, they cause the same devastation and cause plants to perish through the repeated sucking they exert on their leaves; the ants they attract add to the damage they cause. They can be destroyed by cleaning branches or leaves one by one, removing those that have been too deeply altered, or by smoking them with slightly damp tobacco, using the device known as a fumigator, or by washing the plants with a little black soap water; pyrethrum powder, recommended for destroying aphids, can be advantageously replaced by powdered tobacco. TAUPES. - This small insectivorous quadruped is still actively hunted, although it causes little harm except through its galleries, as it lives only on insects and worms; but it stirs up all the soil, upsetting it and cutting roots in the direction of its corridors. The mole works three times a day: in the morning, at midday, and in the evening at sunset; you must take advantage of this time to remove it with a spade, while it re-establishes its gallery, which you first pushed in with your foot. A trap baited with walnuts boiled in lye, which the mole is very fond of, is also placed in the gallery, which is then unblocked. Earthworms cut into pieces and sprinkled with walnuts are also placed in the water. TICKS (BLUE FLEA BEETLE). - These small, extremely agile insects, which leap away from any hand that tries to grab them, wreak considerable havoc on cabbage, radish and turnip seedlings. There are few ways of destroying them, but they can be kept at bay by spraying the plants with a decoction of tobacco or pungent plants. WHITE WOLVES - These insects, harmful to fruit trees, vines, ornamental shrubs and vegetable plants, are difficult to destroy, not because of their agility, since in their larval stage they crawl slowly under the soil, but because they wreak their havoc hidden in the bosom of the earth, and their presence is only noticed when the damage is irreparable. As soon as you see the leaves of a plant wilting, you have to dig at the base, and you're sure to find one or two white grubs. If you want to protect valuable plants, such as fruit trees or young plantations, from grubs, plant strawberries, lettuces, etc. near them, as grubs are very fond of them. Another precaution to take is to pursue chafer beetles with the greatest care, and destroy them as soon as they appear. This must be done in the morning: the beetles, numbed by the cool of the night, are then weakly attached to the branches and easily fall to the ground. M. Duval, who seems to have studied the habits of the cockchafer with great care, claims that simple hoeing is enough to destroy the larvae of this insect. However, hoeing must be carried out at the right time. According to him, it's after egg-laying that the land usually ravaged by the white grub should be hoeed, in order to bring the eggs in the soil to the surface. At this time, the action of light is sufficient, according to his observations, to destroy all the eggs that can be reached. CHAPTER XVII Vocabulary of the main gardening terms. Accor. Manure raised around layers to prevent the cold from penetrating. Apos. Sloping ground facing the sun. Ados are used for sowing and transplanting young plants. AMEND. To improve a soil by fertilizing. ANNUAL. This name is given to plants that germinate, flower, bear seeds and die during the year. AUGUST. Said of young branches that have reached the necessary consistency to withstand winter. ARROSER. Synonymous with wet. BASSINER. To water lightly with a watering can, so that the water falls as rain. BIFURQUÉ. To divide into two branches. BINER. To divide the surface of the soil with the hoe, so that it does not harden. BISANNUAL. Plants that last two years. BOURGEONS. Leaves and stems that are beginning to develop. BUTTERFLY. Eyes placed in the axils of leaves and at the tips of twigs. ADVENTIONAL BUTTERFLIES: those born elsewhere than in the leaf axils and at the ends of twigs. 22. BRINDLE. Thin, short, fringed branch. BULB. Onion plant. BUTTER. To raise the soil around the base of plants to protect them from frost, to whiten them or to promote tuber development. CADUQUES (leaves). Falling every year. CAÏEU. A bud that forms on the side of large onions. CHARGE UNE COUCHE. Placing on it the soil or potting compost required by the plants to be grown. COLLET. A kind of knot placed between the root and the stem. COUNTER-ESPALIER. Trees planted parallel to the espalier and whose branches are attached to a low trellis. COUNTER-PLANTING. This operation consists of planting between the rows of a bed lined with plants that are half or three-quarters grown, the plants that will follow them. CÔTIÈRE. A more or less wide bed, sheltered by a wall or windbreak, where vegetables are grown that come earlier than in the garden. COTYLEDON. Seminal lobes or leaves. COURSON. Branches cut short. DRAGEONS. Young shoots growing from roots. ÉCLAIRCIR. To pull up a plant when it has been sown too thickly, so that the remaining plant will benefit more. ECLATER. To separate the roots of a plant that grows several stems. ESPALIER. A tree whose branches are spread out against a wall. SPLIT. To cut with the nails the main stem of a plant, so as to facilitate the development of the lower branches. FORCE. To force a plant or tree to produce earlier than it would naturally. FRAPPÉ. Said of a melon that, having reached its full size, begins to change color and hue. GERM. Part of the seed from which a plant is formed. GOBTER. To cover mushroom wheels with light earth. HALE. Dry, drying wind. HERBACEOUS. Green, soft, succulent stems. HERSER. This operation consists in breaking up clods of earth with a fork after plowing. GAUGE. The ditch formed by the earth that must be removed before ploughing. LARDER. To introduce the white into mushroom wheels. LINEAR. From the nature of wood. FURNITURE. Said of land well divided by ploughing. WET. Synonymous with watering. EYE. A small point which appears in the axils of leaves and at the end of twigs. In the following spring, this small point becomes a woody or fruit bud. EYE. Offshoots produced by certain plants and used to propagate the species. OMBRACE. To spread canvas or mulch over the frames, to attenuate the intensity of the sun's rays. PAILLER. To spread short manure on the ground, to prevent the rapid evaporation of water from watering. PALISSER. This operation consists in fixing only the branches and buds of espaliered trees. PERSISTENT. Leaves that remain on the tree for several years are called evergreen. PINCH. To cut the tips of young branches with the nails, to encourage the development of lower branches. RAMEAU. A small branch that is a division of the larger ones. RATISSER. To cut the grass between two plots of land with a rake, in alleys and plantations. RECHAUD. New manure raised around layers to warm or maintain heat. REMONTANTS. Roses and other plants that bloom twice in the same year. REPIQUERING. Transplanting consists in planting young plants from seedlings with a planter. RUSTIC. Easy to grow, winter hardy plant. SARCLER. To pull out weeds that grow in cultivated beds. SENTIER. A narrow path left between each bed. TERREAUTER. Spreading a layer of compost over a seedling. TRACER. To make lines along the length of the boards, for sowing or planting. TUBERCULES. Fleshy, rounded parts from which small fibrous roots usually sprout. TURION. Eye or bud arising immediately on roots. VIVACE. Perennials are all plants that survive for more than three years, whether or not they lose their leaves or stems each year. 2 4 S 63 6 55 N 5 1E I 4 61 S E 7 60 60 2 27 28 ? BIBLIOTHÈQUE DES PROFESSIONS INDUSTRIELLES ET AGRICOLES FOUNDED BY E. LACROIX X Ex-Officier de marine, Ingénieur eivil Membre de la Société industrielle de Mulhouse, de l'Institut royal des Ingénieurs hollandais de la Société des Ingénieurs de Hongrie, etc, AVEC LA COLLABORATION de MM. les Rédacteurs des Annales du Génte civil ET CELLE d'Ingénieurs, de Professeurs et de Savants français et étrangers Avertissement. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . ... Table in alphabetical order of authors' names... Pages, Alphabetical catalog of subjects... 17 Catalogue in methodical order of subjects or series. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 25 General catalog of works published by the Lacroix bookshop, other than those forming part of the library . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 71 OAO PROFESSIONS INDUSTRIELLES ET AGRICOLES Publiée par Eugène LACROIX 2 AVERTISSEMENT Since 1816, i.e. in the sixty years since our company was founded (1), our predecessors have published, and we continue to publish, works on the sciences applied to industry, arts and crafts, and agriculture. Taken together, these publications form a very varied collection: we had therefore created a Bibliothèque des professions industrielles et agricoles. But the scope of some of these works, the more or less scientific or more particularly practical teaching they contain, the typographical form, different for most, and finally the high price (1) Merger (in 1856) of the former houses Malher et Ce (founded in 1816), Aug. Mathias (founded in 1827), Comptoir des Imprimeursunis (founded in 1842), G. Comon (founded in 1848). Some of them could not be understood serially in an encyclopedia accessible, by form, content and price, to people who most often need practical indications on the profession they are learning, or in which they want to become more intelligently skilled. For these people, of whom there are many, there is a need for accurate practical guides, in a convenient format, at a moderate price, written with clarity and method, in the same way that direct teaching from teacher to pupil or master to apprentice is clear and methodical. Such was our thinking when we began, in 1863, the publication of the Bibliothèque des professions industrielles et agricoles. We are confident that we will achieve the goal we have set ourselves, thanks to the sustained sales of the series already published, to the number and merit, both as scholars and as practitioners, of the contributors to the work, and to the subscriptions we receive from all sides and in all forms. Our publication is aimed at the engineer, the industrialist, the mechanic in each of the special professions, the craftsman in all trades, the schoolteacher, the farmer; some series are suitable for the man of the world who wishes to satisfy his curiosity, or who wishes to augment the notions already acquired, with specific knowledge of the professions which provide society as a whole with the elements of material well-being, the indispensable basis of moral progress. It is therefore to a very large number of readers, or rather workers, that we offer effective assistance in the study and application of questions of private or public utility. We appeal directly to them, reminding them that the only way to lower the selling price of a book is to print it in a very large number of copies, in anticipation of a large number of buyers: indeed, the initial expenses, i.e. engraving the wood and plates, typographical composition of the text and the author's work, are the same for one copy as for a thousand, ten thousand, etc. We hope that the number of copies will increase in the future. In the hope that the number of subscribers to our work will not cease to increase, - that editors and subscribers will lend us their increasingly effective support, - we shall continue to publish the volumes announced, as quickly as possible. The selling price of each volume will be determined by the cost of producing it. This Library is composed of nine Series, subdivided as follows: SERIES A. - Exact sciences... ... ... . 9 vols. B. - Observational sciences... . 24 " C. - Civil engineering... ... ... ... ... ... ... D. - Mining and metallurgy . . . . . " E. - Power machinery... " F. - Military and maritime professions. G. - Industrial occupations... ..." H. - Agriculture, gardening, etc. ..." I. - Home economics, accounting29 57 lity, legislation, mixtures... 29 1 X 1 Total. ... 250 vols. The volumes in this series are published in the large in-18 format, most of them illustrated with engravings to enhance understanding of the text; atlases contain drawings that need to be represented on a larger scale and in greater detail. Montefiore Levi. 1 vol. 133 pages, 39 tables. 4 fr. 3 Leçons de Géométrie élémentaire, by M. Ch. ROZAN, professor of mathematics. 1 vol. 262 pages and an atlas of 31 engraved double plates, 5 fr. bound, 6 fr. These lessons are based on a completely new plan. M. Rozan's main concern is to show the connection between the essential principles of elementary geometry, and the way in which they flow from one another through a continuous chain of deductions and consequences. The division into lessons gradually leads the student to acquire, even without a teacher, knowledge of the most advanced theorems of geometry. The atlas is composed of carefully engraved plates. 6. ORSERVATION SCIENCES, CHEMISTRY, PHYSICS, ELECTRICITY, ETC. 1 Eléments de Chimie, by Dr. Sacc, professor at the Neufchatel Academy, etc. 2 vols. Br. 5 fr. Hardback 7 fr. Sold separately. 1° Chimie minérale ou synthétique. 1 vol. 3 fr. 50. 2° Chimie organique ou asynthétique. 1vol. 3 fr. 50. 2. Mechanical theory of heat, by R. CLAUSIUS, professor at the University of Würzburg, translated from German by F. FOLIE, professor at the Ecole industrielle and tutor at the Ecole des mines de Liège, Part I, 1 vol. xxIV-441 pages - Part II Memoranda on the application of the mechanical theory of heat to electrical phenomena and on the molecular movements accepted for the explanation of heat. 1 vol. vi-307 p. Price of 2 volumes. 15 fr." Since heat has been used as a motive force by means of steam engines, and we have thus been practically led to regard a certain quantity of work as equivalent to the heat necessary to produce it, it has been natural to seek theoretically for a definite relationship between a quantity of heat and the work it is possible to make it produce, and to use this relationship to deduce conclusions about the essence and laws of heat itself." So says Mr. Clausius at the beginning of his first Memoir. These lines suffice to illustrate the importance of a question whose study is essential today for scientists and practical men who want to derive from the steam engine all the useful effect it can give. 4. Télégraphie électrique, ou Vade mecum pratique a l'usage des employés des lignes télégraphiques, suivi du programme des connaissances exigées pour être admis au surnumérariat dans l'administration des lignes télégraphiques, by M. B. MIECE, telegraph line station manager. 1 vol. x1-148 pages, with 45 figures in text. Br. 2 fr. Hardcover. 3 fr. M. Miége's intention was not just to produce a useful book, but an indispensable guide. Preliminary notions on magnetism, the different sources of electricity and the properties of currents are followed by descriptions of all the devices in use, with indications of the signals generally adopted. Extremely simple formulas are used to determine the intensity of currents, and to identify the cause of malfunctions. M. Miége's work will also be of undeniable use to anyone wishing to acquire knowledge of the laws of electricity as applied to telegraphy. 5. L'Etudiant photographe, by A. CHEVALIER, with processes by Messrs. Civiale, Bacot, Cavelier, Robert, 1 vol. da 216 pages, with 68 figures. 3 fr. Hardcover. 4 fr. No theoretical considerations, but lots of information and details on the instruments used and how to operate them. In addition, the author is keen to bring the reader up to date with the latest procedures. 9. Analyse qualitative, instruction pratique à l'usage des laboratoires de chimie, by M. le Docteur H. WILL, professeur agrégé de l'université de Giessen; translated from the German by M. le Dr. G.-W. BICHON, translator of M. Justas Liebig's Lettres sur la chimie, and author of several works on this science. 1 vol. 248 pages. 3 fr. Special treatises on analytical chemistry are either too voluminous or incomplete, in the sense that the latter lack the indications essential for the student to be able to guide himself. Dr. Will has avoided both these shortcomings: his guide teaches, in a simple, substantial and methodical way, everything you need to know to be able to discover and separate the constituent parts of compound bodies. 10. Guide pratique pour réconnaître et pour déterminer le titre véritable et la valeur commerciale des Potasses. des Soudes, des Cendres, des Acides et des Manganèses, avec neuf tables de déterminations, par le docteur R. FRÉSÉNIUS and Dr. WILL, assistants and preparators at the Giessen laboratory, translated from the German by Dr. G.-W. BICHON, former student of M. Justus Liébig, with additional notes, tables and documents taken from the Annales du Génie civil. 1 vol. vi-229 pages 3 fr. 11. Introduction to the study of Chemistry, containing the general principles of this science, chemical proportions, atomic theory, the relationship of atomic weights to the volume of bodies, isomorphism, the uses of atomic weights and chemical formulas, isomeric combinations of catalytic bodies, etc..., accompagnée de considérations détaillées sur les acides, les bases et les sels, par M. J. LIEBIG, translated from the German by Ch. GHÉRARD, augmentée d'une table alphabétique des matières prèsant les définitions techniques et les relations des corps. 1 v., 248, pages. 2 fr. 50 The favorable reception this translation has met with in France is reminiscent of the success achieved in Germany by the original edition of the illustrious scholar, rightly considered one of the princes of modern chemistry. 12. Guide pratique pour reconnaître et corriger les Fraudes et maladies du vin, suivi d'un traité d'ANALYSE CHIMIQUE de tous les vins, by M. JACQUES BRUN, vice-présisent de la Société suisse des pharmaciens, 2 ed. 1 vol. 191 p., with numerous tables. Br. 2 fr. 50. 3 fr. The art of wine adulteration has made rapid progress in recent years. Chemistry must not be outdone by fraud: it must stand up to it and always be able to point to the added substance. This task, says Brun, falls mainly to pharmacists. His book is a summary of the different treatments he has found really useful, and which, in his long practice, have been the most successful for the chemical examination of suspect wines. 14. Guide pratique de Minéralogie appliquée (histoire naturelle inorganique) ou connaissance des combustibles minéraux, des pierres précieuses, des matériaux de construction, des argiles céramiques, des minerais manufacturiers et des laboratoires, des minerais de fer, de cuivre, de zinc, de plomb, d'étain, de mercure, d'argent, d'antimoine, d'or, de platine, etc., par M. A.-F. NOGUES. professeur de sciences physiques et naturelles. 2 vols, together 919 p. and 248 fig. Br. 10 fr. Hardcover. 12 fr. As the author states in his preface, this guide has been written primarily for people wishing to acquire accurate, practical and customary knowledge of metalliferous ores and minerals used in the arts and industry, students taking courses at Faculties, students at special and industrial schools, engineers, students at mining schools, miners, farmers, mine managers, mine wardens, amateurs and people from all walks of life who wish to acquire a practical knowledge of mineralogy, will consult it with fruitfulness. This guide has been conceived in an essentially practical and industrial spirit. 17. Leçons élémentaires d'Électricité ou exposition concise des principes généraux de l'ÉLECTRICTIÉ ET DE ses APPLICATIONS, by SNOW-HARRIS, of the Royal Society of London, etc., annotated and translated by E. GARNAULT, former student of the Ecole normale, professor of physics at the Imperial Naval School. 1 vol. 264 pages, with 72 figures in the text. 3 fr. Mr. Snow-Harris' lessons have been a great success in England. The author has concentrated on giving sound, practical and theoretical ideas on the general principles of electricity and the simplest facts, which he demonstrates with easily repeated experiments. His elegant translator, Mr. Garnault, has added notes to the English work, in which he gives, above all, insights into the main applications of electricity in industry. Mr. Maurice, the elegant translator of Mr. Fairbairn's book, has supplemented, with notes and appendices, all that the original text may have been too laconic or too exclusively written with English metallurgy in mind. Dessoye takes the art of steelmaking at its origin and shows us its progress. He points out the nature and native properties of steel, indicates the different ways in which it is made, and ends his guide with a study on the use of steel in the manipulations it undergoes. As Mr. Grateau points out in his skilful introduction, this book is aimed at all those who are called upon to buy and consume steel of any quality, in any form, and should be consulted by all practitioners. Notions on aluminum were scattered throughout numerous collections published in France and abroad. Guide pratique de l'Alliage des métaux, by M. A. GUETTIER. 1 vol. vii-342 pages. Br. 3 fr. Hardback. 4 fr. After giving a few preliminary explanations on the physical and chemical properties of metals and alloys, the author examines, from the point of view of their alloying with each other, metals that are specially industrial, i.e. of very widespread common use (copper, tin, zinc, lead, iron, cast iron, steel). He then gives some general indications on metals belonging to other industries, but occupying only a secondary place (bismuth, antimony, nickel, arsenic, mercury), and on rich metals belonging to the arts or luxury industries (gold, silver, aluminum, platinum); finally, he considers metals of restricted industrial use, from the possible point of view of their association with alloys of some interest in the industrial arts. 39 15. In such circumstances, there is obviously danger, and this is why we have published this work, so that it can be put into the hands of all workmen who, without knowing the first word of the theory of heat or mechanics, are put in a position to operate a steam engine. Le Livre de poche du Charpentier, aprlication pratique a 1 usage des CHANTIERS, des ELÈVES DES ÉCOLES PROFESSIONNELLES, etc. Collection of 140 DRAWINGS, with explanatory tax, by Mr. J.-F. MERLY, carpenter, public works contractor, member of the Société industrielle d'Angers and the Académie nationale de Paris, author of the album du Trait théorique et pratique, etc. 1 vol. 287 p., 5 fr. bound. 6 fr. Regarding the Livre de poche du Charpentier, we repeat what was said about another book by M. Merly. Both works deserve the same praise. Mr. Merly is not a scholar who must strive to forget the technology of the School in order to speak the ordinary language of most of his listeners; Mr. Merly is, on the contrary, a worker, a practical man, who has sought to make himself understood by the fellow workers to whom he addressed himself, and who has arrived at demonstrations so clear, at explanations so natural, that theorists themselves have soon had to draw inspiration from his work. Nothing is sharper than his drawings, nothing simpler than his precepts: in a way, it is by playing with himself that he arrives at the most complicated designs. L'Album du Trait théorique et pratique will remain as proof of the results that intelligence, perseverance and love of work can produce. 14. Guide du Teinturier, by FOL, 1 large vol. with numerous figs. In press. For subscribers. 8 fr. 23. Guide pratique du Bijoutier. Most published works on drainage are the result of theoretical studies that experience has not yet sanctioned. Mr. Kielmann has taken a different approach: he has resorted to theory only when necessary to explain certain phenomena. As he says in his preface, his aim was to offer those just starting out in the field of drainage, and even the simple farmer, a manual such that the reader could say, after reading it: It's easy to understand, now I'll be able to work. - The success of the Guide pratique du drainage proves that this goal has been largely achieved. 9. Gobin's aim was to write a very practical guide indicating everything that needs to be observed to obtain the best results and avoid useless expenses: but as he says in his preface, if the very title of his book has made it a law to restrict himself to the cultivation of forage plants and to abstain from scientific considerations useless to the goal he pursues, he has not forbidden himself the practical applications of science insofar as they relate to the explanation of phenomena or the improvement of cultivation methods. a "This is what we mean by practice, and not just manual routine, which consists in knowing how to hold the ploughshanks, load a wagon with sheaves or handle a scythe - the latter is sufficient for a worker, the former is necessary for the least intelligent farmer.
